\part{Resolvent, Continued-Fraction, and Transform Representations of the Fabius--Rvachev System}
% Absorbed from Fabius_Rvachev_Representation_Frontiers/rvachev_fabius_representation_frontiers.tex




\begin{abstract}
This report audits the LaTeX corpus in the repository subtree
\path{Analysis/FabiusFunction/docs}, including its living expositions, consolidated frontier
volume, active draft families, archive lineages, and paper transcriptions \cite{p1:Repository}.  The repository already contains an unusually
broad theory of the Fabius function, Rvachev's \(\RvachevUp\)-function, their dyadic values,
Thue--Morse expansions, Fourier products, moment and cumulant algebra, inverse asymptotics,
and spectral decay.  The objective here is therefore not to reproduce that material, but to
organize it as a representation atlas and then add constructions that are absent from the
audited corpus.

The principal new object is the Cauchy--Stieltjes transform
\[
 R(z)=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\RvachevUp(x)}{z-x}\,\Differential x.
\]
We derive its hyperbolic-product Laplace representation, its one-sided Fourier--Laplace
representation, an exact logarithmic fixed-point identity, the functional-differential law
\[
 R'(z)=2\bigl(R(2z+1)-R(2z-1)\bigr),
\]
and an all-orders Thue--Morse derivative tree.  Finite dyadic convolution laws yield an
explicit \(2^N\)-term logarithmic formula for \(R_N\), whose boundary jump is exactly the
finite Thue--Morse spline for the density.  This gives a new ``logarithm-to-spline'' bridge.

We also construct the Jacobi continued fraction of \(R\), equivalently the Stieltjes
continued fraction of the ordinary even-moment generating function.  Its coefficients are
positive rational numbers; the first forty are computed exactly.  The first coefficients are
\[
 \beta_1=\frac19,\quad \beta_2=\frac{32}{225},\quad
 \beta_3=\frac{619}{3675},\quad
 \beta_4=\frac{835232}{4640643}.
\]
The Denisov--Rakhmanov theorem proves \(\beta_n\to1/4\), while exact computation through
\(n=40\) supports strict monotonicity and a logarithmically corrected convergence law.
Further new representations include a trace-class Fredholm determinant for the Fourier
image, three equivalent meromorphic logarithmic-derivative expansions, a digamma/Hurwitz-
zeta hierarchy, exact one-sided Laplace and Fourier transforms of \(\RvachevUp\) and \(F\), and
pushforward transform identities for the inverse Fabius function.  A commented Python
program, exact CSV data, numerical checks, and figures accompany the report.
\end{abstract}


\newpage

\section{Executive summary and novelty boundary}

\subsection{What was audited}

The repository contains several kinds of LaTeX source that must not be conflated:

\begin{enumerate}
\item a living primary synthesis of the formalized and analytic theory;
\item a large consolidated non-formalized frontier volume;
\item current draft reports on arithmetic rays, half-integer spectra, self-sampling,
      inverse asymptotics, Newton/exponent-sequence hierarchies, Fourier decay, and related
      topics;
\item archived earlier lineages, retained because some contain historically distinctive
      formulas even when superseded;
\item seven paper-source transcriptions or corrected transcriptions.
\end{enumerate}

The repository's own pinned audit ledger records 67 LaTeX paths at the following commit:
\begin{center}
\small\texttt{32d6d36c51d803289e6d6a0dc0c37753766eba47}.
\end{center}
The live tree inspected on 27 August 2026 contains additional current drafts, notably the
self-sampling, inverse, and Newton/Rvachev reports.  Appendix~\ref{p1:app:corpus} records the pinned ledger and the live
additions used in this study.

\subsection{Non-duplication protocol}

Every formula in the atlas is tagged conceptually as follows.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}c p{0.78\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Tag & meaning \\
\midrule
\statusknown & already explicit in the audited repository or classical source; included for orientation \\
\statusderived & an immediate transform or recombination of known identities; useful but not claimed as new \\
\statusnew & apparently absent from the audited corpus; proved or conjectured here \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The word ``new'' throughout this report means \emph{new relative to the audited repository}.
No claim of global bibliographic priority is made without a separate literature search.
In particular, the current frontier report explicitly proposes a Jacobi continued fraction
for the moment generating function or Cauchy transform as an open direction.  The present
report constructs that fraction and develops its consequences.

\subsection{Main new results}

The most substantial additions are:

\begin{enumerate}
\item the Cauchy transform \(R\) and its moment, Laplace-product, Fourier--Laplace,
      boundary-value, and logarithmic fixed-point representations;
\item the exact resolvent equation and its all-orders Thue--Morse derivative tree;
\item a finite-level \(2^N\)-term logarithmic formula converging to \(R\), paired by
      boundary jumps with the finite Thue--Morse spline density;
\item the Jacobi \(J\)-fraction of \(R\), the contracted Stieltjes \(S\)-fraction of the
      even moments, exact rational coefficients through order 40, Gaussian quadrature
      convergents, and the theorem \(\beta_n\to1/4\);
\item a Fredholm determinant realization of the Fourier image and a trace-resolvent
      interpretation of its logarithmic derivative;
\item cotangent, tangent, valuation-weighted partial-fraction, digamma, and Hurwitz-zeta
      representations of the logarithmic derivatives of the Fourier image;
\item exact one-sided Laplace and Fourier transforms of \(\RvachevUp\), exact transforms of
      \(F\) on \([0,1]\), and a master pushforward identity for \(F^{-1}\).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Normalization and structural dictionary}

\subsection{The functions}

Let \(F:[0,1]\to[0,1]\) denote the bounded Fabius function \cite{p1:Fabius1966,p1:AriasTranslation}, extended by \(0\) on
\(( -\infty,0]\) and by \(1\) on \([1,\infty)\).  On the unit interval it satisfies
\begin{align}
 F(0)&=0, & F(1)&=1, & F(1-x)&=1-F(x), \label{p1:eq:F-symmetry}\\
 F'(x)&=2F(2x) &&(0\le x\le\tfrac12). \label{p1:eq:F-diff}
\end{align}
The Rvachev function is written
\begin{equation}
 \rho(x):=\RvachevUp(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 F(x+1),&-1\le x\le0,\\
 F(1-x),&0\le x\le1,\\
 0,&|x|\ge1.
 \end{cases}                                                \label{p1:eq:rho-fold}
\end{equation}
Then \(\rho\) is even, strictly positive on \((-1,1)\), normalized by
\(\int\rho=1\), flat at \(\pm1\), and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \rho'(x)=2\bigl(\rho(2x+1)-\rho(2x-1)\bigr).                \label{p1:eq:rho-dde}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fourier convention and basic transforms}

We use
\begin{equation}
 \FourierTwoPi{f}(\xi)=\int_{\RealNumbers}f(x)e^{-2\pi i\xi x}\,\Differential x,
 \qquad
 f(x)=\int_{\RealNumbers}\FourierTwoPi{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi i\xi x}\,\Differential\xi.       \label{p1:eq:fourier-convention}
\end{equation}
Set
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(\xi):=\FourierTwoPi{\rho}(\xi),
 \qquad
 \SincPi(z):=\frac{\sin(\pi z)}{\pi z},\quad \SincPi(0)=1. \label{p1:eq:Phi-def}
\end{equation}
The classical products are
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\SincPi\!\left(\frac{z}{2^n}\right)
 =\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}\cos\!\left(\frac{\pi z}{2^m}\right)^m. \label{p1:eq:Phi-products}
\end{equation}
The scaling law is
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\SincPi(z)\Phi(z/2).                                \label{p1:eq:Phi-scaling}
\end{equation}

\subsection{The probability model}

Let \(U_1,U_2,\dots\) be independent random variables, uniform on \([-1,1]\), and set
\begin{equation}
 X=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}U_k.                              \label{p1:eq:X-series}
\end{equation}
Then \(X\) has density \(\rho\).  Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 X\overset{\mathrm d}=\frac{U+X'}2,                           \label{p1:eq:X-fixed-point}
\end{equation}
where \(U\sim\operatorname{Unif}[-1,1]\) and \(X'\) is an independent copy of \(X\).
The moment generating function is
\begin{equation}
 M(t):=\Expectation e^{tX}
 =\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sinh(t/2^k)}{t/2^k}
 =\Phi\!\left(\frac{it}{2\pi}\right).                       \label{p1:eq:M-product}
\end{equation}
The even moments are
\begin{equation}
 \mu_{2n}:=\Expectation X^{2n}=\int_{-1}^{1}x^{2n}\rho(x)\,\Differential x,
 \qquad \mu_{2n+1}=0.                                      \label{p1:eq:moments}
\end{equation}
Finally, if
\begin{equation}
 Y=\frac{X+1}{2},                                             \label{p1:eq:Y-affine}
\end{equation}
then \(F\) is the distribution function of \(Y\), and
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=2\rho(2x-1),\qquad 0<x<1.                              \label{p1:eq:F-density}
\end{equation}

\section{Audited representation atlas}

This section compresses the principal representation families already present in the
repository and places the new formulas beside them.  Proofs of the new entries begin in
Section~\ref{p1:sec:cauchy}.

\subsection[Real-space and probability representations of rho]{Real-space and probability representations of \(\rho\)}

\paragraph{Infinite convolution (\statusknown).}
Since \(2^{-k}U_k\) is uniform on \([-2^{-k},2^{-k}]\),
\begin{equation}
 \rho=\mathop{\ast}_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \left(2^{k-1}\IndicatorOf{[-2^{-k},2^{-k}]}\right).        \label{p1:eq:rho-convolution}
\end{equation}
This is the real-space counterpart of the sinc product.

\paragraph{Infinite cube integral (\statusderived).}
For every bounded continuous test function \(g\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}g(x)\rho(x)\,\Differential x
 =\int_{[-1,1]^{\NaturalNumbers}}g\!\left(\sum_{k\ge1}2^{-k}u_k\right)
   \prod_{k\ge1}\frac{\Differential u_k}{2}.                           \label{p1:eq:cube-pushforward}
\end{equation}
Formally, this may be written as a Dirac-delta integral for \(\rho(x)\).

\paragraph{Finite Thue--Morse splines (\statusknown).}
Put
\begin{equation}
 X_N=\sum_{k=1}^{N}2^{-k}U_k,
 \qquad
 a_{N,j}=\frac{2^N-1-2j}{2^N},
 \qquad
 \eps_j=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(j)}.                                      \label{p1:eq:aNj}
\end{equation}
The density \(\rho_N\) of \(X_N\) is
\begin{equation}
 \rho_N(x)=\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^N-1}\eps_j\,(x+a_{N,j})_+^{N-1}.             \label{p1:eq:finite-spline}
\end{equation}
For each fixed derivative order, \(\rho_N\to\rho\) uniformly after \(N\) is sufficiently
large relative to that order.  The repository develops several centered and one-sided
variants with explicit error control.

\paragraph{Fourier inversion and cosine form (\statusknown).}
Because \(\Phi\) is real and even,
\begin{align}
 \rho(x)&=\int_{\RealNumbers}\Phi(\xi)e^{2\pi i x\xi}\,\Differential\xi,
                                                                  \label{p1:eq:rho-fourier-inversion}\\
 \rho(x)&=2\int_0^{\infty}\Phi(\xi)\cos(2\pi x\xi)\,\Differential\xi.
                                                                  \label{p1:eq:rho-cosine}
\end{align}
The audited corpus also contains Poisson-summation formulas, Legendre expansions,
periodized cosine series, dyadic spline limits, and endpoint transseries.  They are not
rederived here.

\subsection[Product, zero, gamma, and exponential representations of Phi]{Product, zero, gamma, and exponential representations of \(\Phi\)}

\paragraph{Canonical zero product (\statusknown).}
Let \(\TwoAdicValuation(n)\) be the exponent of \(2\) in \(n\).  The nonzero integer \(n\) is a zero of
multiplicity \(1+\TwoAdicValuation(|n|)\), and
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}
 \left(1-\frac{z^2}{n^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}.                \label{p1:eq:canonical-product}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Gamma product (\statusderived).}
Euler's reflection identity gives
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{1}{\Gamma(1+z/2^k)\Gamma(1-z/2^k)}.                  \label{p1:eq:gamma-product}
\end{equation}
This is the natural entry point for the Barnes and multiple-gamma compressions studied in
current repository drafts.

\emph{Formal version and boundary.}  At formal source checkpoint
\path{71ab6f6728fceb753c88d8b0573077a59acf2682},
\leanPartx{Fabius.shiftedReciprocalGamma_mul_neg} proves the total pointwise reciprocal
reflection formula and \leanPartx{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma_mul_neg} multiplies it over
the dyadic scales to give \eqref{p1:eq:gamma-product}; the exact Gamma-denominator form is
\leanPartx{Fabius.rvachevFourierProduct_eq_one_div_dyadicGamma_mul}.  These declarations live
in \path{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean}.  They do not formalize the Barnes or multiple-gamma
compressions mentioned in the following sentence.

\paragraph{Spectral-zeta exponential (\statusknown).}
For \(|z|<1\),
\begin{equation}
 \log\Phi(z)
 =-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{\zeta(2m)}{m(1-2^{-2m})}z^{2m}.                       \label{p1:eq:logPhi-zeta}
\end{equation}
The coefficient sequence is the spectral zeta function
\begin{equation}
 Z_{\Phi}(s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}{n^s}
 =\frac{\zeta(s)}{1-2^{-s}}.                                \label{p1:eq:spectral-zeta}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Fredholm determinant (\statusnew).}
Section~\ref{p1:sec:fredholm} upgrades \eqref{p1:eq:canonical-product} to
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\det(I-z^2\cK),                                    \label{p1:eq:fredholm-preview}
\end{equation}
for an explicit positive trace-class diagonal operator \(\cK\).  This makes
\eqref{p1:eq:spectral-zeta} literally the trace zeta function of \(\cK\).

\subsection{Moment, cumulant, and Bell-polynomial representations}

The moment generating function has the convergent Taylor series
\begin{equation}
 M(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu_{2n}\frac{t^{2n}}{(2n)!}.       \label{p1:eq:M-moment-series}
\end{equation}
Its logarithm is
\begin{equation}
 \log M(t)=
 \sum_{m=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{2^{2m-1}B_{2m}}{m(2m)!(4^m-1)}t^{2m},                \label{p1:eq:M-cumulant}
\end{equation}
so all moments are complete Bell polynomials in explicit Bernoulli/Mersenne cumulants.
These formulas, together with reciprocal Bell expansions for \(1/\Phi\), are developed
extensively in the repository.

\subsection[Transform representations of F and inverse F]{Transform representations of \(F\) and \(F^{-1}\)}

The CDF inversion formula gives
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\frac12+\frac1\pi\int_0^{\infty}
 \Phi(\xi)\frac{\sin(2\pi(2x-1)\xi)}{\xi}\,\Differential\xi,
 \qquad 0<x<1.                                               \label{p1:eq:F-CDF-Fourier}
\end{equation}
Section~\ref{p1:sec:fabius-transforms} adds closed Laplace and Fourier transforms of \(F\) as
a function, as well as transforms of its derivative measure and its inverse.

\(R\) & moment Laurent series & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:R-moment} \\
\(R\) & Laplace transform of the hyperbolic product & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:R-Laplace} \\
\(R\) & one-sided Fourier--Laplace integral & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:R-Fourier-Laplace} \\
\(R\) & logarithmic fixed-point expectation & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:R-log-fixed} \\
\(R\) & dyadic functional-differential equation & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:R-dde} \\
\(R^{(n)}\) & finite Thue--Morse affine orbit of \(R\) & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:R-derivative-tree} \\
\(R\) & finite \(2^N\)-logarithm limit & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:RN-log} \\
\(R\) & Jacobi continued fraction & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:R-J-fraction} \\
\(R\) & Gaussian-quadrature partial fractions & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:gauss-partial} \\
\(\sum\mu_{2n}t^n\) & Stieltjes continued fraction and product integral & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:O-S-fraction} \\
\(F\) & CDF sine-product integral & \statusderived & \eqref{p1:eq:F-CDF-Fourier} \\
\(F\) & exact Laplace transform on \([0,1]\) & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:F-Laplace} \\
\(F\) & exact Fourier transform on \([0,1]\) & \statusnew & \eqref{p1:eq:F-Fourier} \\
\(\rho|_{[0,1]}\) & exact one-sided Laplace/Fourier/sine transforms & \statusnew & Thm.~\ref{p1:thm:one-sided} \\

\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\normalsize

\section{The Cauchy--Stieltjes transform}\label{p1:sec:cauchy}

\begin{definition}
For \(z\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1]\), define
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\rho(x)}{z-x}\,\Differential x=\Expectation\frac1{z-X}. \label{p1:eq:R-def}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Formal Cauchy--Stieltjes core]
\path{CauchyTransform.lean} defines the report-oriented transform as
\path{rvachevCauchyTransform}; Mathlib's \path{resolventTransform} uses
\((x-z)^{-1}\), so the formal definition is its negative.  The declarations
\path{rvachevCauchyTransform_apply} and
\path{rvachevCauchyTransform_eq_integral_rvachevUp} give respectively the
measure and density forms of \eqref{p1:eq:R-def}, while
\path{analyticOn_rvachevCauchyTransform} and
\path{hasDerivAt_rvachevCauchyTransform} prove holomorphy off the complexified
support interval and the measure-form first derivative
\[
 R'(z)=-\int (z-x)^{-2}\,\Differential\mu_{\RvachevUp}(x).
\]
Expanding \(\mu_{\RvachevUp}=\rho(x)\,\Differential x\) recovers the corresponding density formula.
The total Lean definition follows the Bochner-integral convention at points where the
kernel is not integrable; the analytic Cauchy-transform interpretation is asserted only
off the cut.

The companion \path{CauchyCDF.lean} proves the atom-exact compact-support formula

\[
 \int\frac{\Differential\mu(x)}{z-x}
 =\mu(\RealNumbers)\frac1{z-b}
  -\int_a^b\frac{\mu((-\infty,t])}{(z-t)^2}\,\Differential t .
\]

Its generic declaration
\path{integral_inv_sub_eq_mass_smul_sub_intervalIntegral_measureReal_Iic}
assumes only a finite measure, \(a\leq b\), almost-everywhere support on
\([a,b]\), and \(z\) outside the complexified interval.  The declaration
\path{integral_inv_sub_eq_sub_intervalIntegral_cdf} gives the probability
normalization, and
\path{fabiusStieltjesTransform_eq_inv_sub_one_sub_intervalIntegral_fabiusReal}
specializes it on \path{fabiusStieltjesDomain} to
\[
 S(z)=\frac1{z-1}-\int_0^1\frac{F(t)}{(z-t)^2}\,\Differential t .
\]
Closed lower rays retain atoms exactly.  These declarations do not supply the
higher resolvent-power, logarithmic, inverse-quantile, or boundary formulas below.
\end{remark}

The transform \(R\) is odd because \(\rho\) is even.  It is an anti-Herglotz function:
when \(\operatorname{Im}z>0\),
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Im}R(z)
 =-\operatorname{Im}z\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\rho(x)}{|z-x|^2}\,\Differential x<0. \label{p1:eq:R-anti-Herglotz}
\end{equation}
In particular, \(R\) has no zeros off the cut.  For real \(z>1\), \(R(z)>0\); for
\(z<-1\), \(R(z)<0\).

\begin{theorem}[Four complementary representations of \(R\)]\label{p1:thm:R-representations}
The following formulas hold.

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item For \(|z|>1\),
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{2n}}{z^{2n+1}}.          \label{p1:eq:R-moment}
\end{equation}
\item For \(\operatorname{Re}z>1\),
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-zt}
 \prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sinh(t/2^k)}{t/2^k}\,\Differential t.    \label{p1:eq:R-Laplace}
\end{equation}
\item For \(\operatorname{Im}z>0\),
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=-2\pi i\int_0^{\infty}e^{2\pi i z\xi}\Phi(\xi)\,\Differential\xi. \label{p1:eq:R-Fourier-Laplace}
\end{equation}
For \(\operatorname{Im}z<0\), take the conjugate counterpart.
\item For \(-1<x<1\), the non-tangential boundary values satisfy
\begin{align}
 \rho(x)&=-\frac1\pi\lim_{y\downarrow0}\operatorname{Im}R(x+iy),
                                                               \label{p1:eq:R-boundary-density}\\
 \PV\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\rho(t)}{x-t}\,\Differential t
 &=\lim_{y\downarrow0}\operatorname{Re}R(x+iy)
 =2\pi\int_0^{\infty}\Phi(\xi)\sin(2\pi x\xi)\,\Differential\xi.     \label{p1:eq:R-boundary-Hilbert}
\end{align}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(|z|>1\), expand \((z-X)^{-1}=z^{-1}\sum_{m\ge0}(X/z)^m\), use
\(|X|\le1\), and then symmetry.  This gives \eqref{p1:eq:R-moment}.

For \(\operatorname{Re}z>1\),
\[
 \frac1{z-X}=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-(z-X)t}\,\Differential t.
\]
Fubini's theorem is justified because \(X\le1\).  Taking expectation and using
\eqref{p1:eq:M-product} yields \eqref{p1:eq:R-Laplace}.

For \(\operatorname{Im}z>0\),
\[
 \frac1{z-x}=-2\pi i\int_0^{\infty}e^{2\pi i(z-x)\xi}\,\Differential\xi.
\]
The exponential damping from \(\operatorname{Im}z>0\), together with the rapid decay of
\(\Phi\), permits Fubini and gives \eqref{p1:eq:R-Fourier-Laplace}.  Finally,
Plemelj's boundary formula gives \eqref{p1:eq:R-boundary-density}; taking real parts of
\eqref{p1:eq:R-Fourier-Laplace} gives \eqref{p1:eq:R-boundary-Hilbert}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The four formulas emphasize different structures.  The Laurent series exposes exact
rational moments; the Laplace integral exposes the hyperbolic product; the Fourier--Laplace
integral exposes spectral decay; and the boundary values recover both \(\rho\) and its
Hilbert transform.  Thus \(R\) is a single analytic object joining the moment, product,
and inversion theories.
\end{remark}

\section{A dyadic functional equation for the resolvent}

\subsection{Logarithmic fixed-point identity}

\begin{theorem}[Logarithmic fixed point]\label{p1:thm:R-log-fixed}
For real \(z>1\),
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\int_{-1}^{1}\rho(y)
 \log\!\left(\frac{2z+1-y}{2z-1-y}\right)\,\Differential y.           \label{p1:eq:R-log-fixed}
\end{equation}
The identity extends by analytic continuation to any simply connected subdomain of
\(\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1]\), with the logarithm chosen by continuation from large positive
\(z\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the fixed point \eqref{p1:eq:X-fixed-point} and condition on \(X'\):
\begin{align*}
 R(z)
 &=\Expectation\left[\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}
   \frac{\Differential u}{z-u-X'/2}\right]\\
 &=\Expectation\log\!\left(\frac{z+1/2-X'/2}{z-1/2-X'/2}\right),
\end{align*}
which is \eqref{p1:eq:R-log-fixed} after multiplying numerator and denominator by \(2\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Functional-differential equation}

\begin{theorem}[Resolvent equation]\label{p1:thm:R-dde}
For every \(z\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1]\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{R'(z)=2\bigl(R(2z+1)-R(2z-1)\bigr).}                \label{p1:eq:R-dde}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate \eqref{p1:eq:R-log-fixed} first for \(z>1\):
\begin{align*}
 R'(z)
 &=\Expectation\left(\frac{2}{2z+1-X'}-\frac{2}{2z-1-X'}\right)\\
 &=2\bigl(R(2z+1)-R(2z-1)\bigr).
\end{align*}
Both sides are holomorphic on \(\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1]\), so the identity theorem gives the
stated continuation.
\end{proof}

This is the transform-side analogue of the Rvachev equation \eqref{p1:eq:rho-dde}.  It is
not merely a Fourier rephrasing: it acts on the spectral parameter of a Cauchy transform and
therefore interacts directly with moments, Pad\'e approximation, and orthogonal polynomials.

\subsection{All-order Thue--Morse derivative tree}

\begin{theorem}[Thue--Morse derivative orbit]\label{p1:thm:R-derivative-tree}
For every integer \(n\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 R^{(n)}(z)=2^{n(n+1)/2}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^n-1}\eps_j\,
 R\bigl(2^n z+2^n-1-2j\bigr).
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:R-derivative-tree}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\rho(x)}{(z-x)^{n+1}}\,\Differential x
 =\frac{(-1)^n2^{n(n+1)/2}}{n!}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^n-1}\eps_j
 R\bigl(2^n z+2^n-1-2j\bigr).                               \label{p1:eq:R-kernel-tree}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The case \(n=0\) is trivial and \(n=1\) is \eqref{p1:eq:R-dde}.  Assume the formula at
order \(n\).  Differentiating contributes a factor \(2^n\), and then applying
\eqref{p1:eq:R-dde} contributes another factor \(2\).  The exponent therefore changes by
\(n+1\), from \(n(n+1)/2\) to \((n+1)(n+2)/2\).

For the shifts, write \(a_{n,j}=2^n-1-2j\).  The two children are
\[
 2a_{n,j}+1=2^{n+1}-1-2(2j),
 \qquad
 2a_{n,j}-1=2^{n+1}-1-2(2j+1).
\]
The signs obey \(\eps_{2j}=\eps_j\) and \(\eps_{2j+1}=-\eps_j\), exactly matching the
subtraction in \eqref{p1:eq:R-dde}.  This proves \eqref{p1:eq:R-derivative-tree}.  Formula
\eqref{p1:eq:R-kernel-tree} follows from
\[
 R^{(n)}(z)=(-1)^n n!\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\rho(x)}{(z-x)^{n+1}}\,\Differential x.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Generic affine-difference orbit; formalized]
\label{p1:prop:affine-difference-formal}
Over a commutative ring, let
\[
 (\Delta_{b,c}f)(z)=f(bz+c)-f(bz-c),\qquad
 \xi_{n,T}(z)=b^nz+c\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}b^k-2c\sum_{k\in T}b^k .
\]
For every function with values in an additive commutative group,
\begin{equation}
 (\Delta_{b,c}^{\,n}f)(z)
 =\sum_{T\subseteq\{0,\ldots,n-1\}}(-1)^{|T|}f\bigl(\xi_{n,T}(z)\bigr).
 \label{p1:eq:affine-cube-formal}
\end{equation}
Moreover, suppose the ring is a nontrivially normed field, let \(S\) be open and invariant
under \(z\mapsto bz\pm c\), and let \(f\) take values in a normed vector space.  If
\(f'(z)=a\,\Delta_{b,c}f(z)\) for \(z\in S\), then no separate smoothness assumption is
needed: for every \(n\geq0\) and \(z\in S\),
\begin{equation}
 f^{(n)}(z)=a^n b^{\binom n2}\,(\Delta_{b,c}^{\,n}f)(z).
 \label{p1:eq:affine-derivative-formal}
\end{equation}
Constant scalar multiplication commutes with every iterate.  Taking \(S\) to be the
whole field gives the global form, while \(b=2,c=1\) gives
\begin{equation}
 (\Delta_{2,1}^{\,n}f)(z)
 =\sum_{j=0}^{2^n-1}\eps_j f\bigl(2^nz+2^n-1-2j\bigr).
 \label{p1:eq:affine-dyadic-formal}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Lean proof and boundary]
The following declarations in \path{AffineDifferenceOrbit.lean} prove the statements above:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{l}
\texttt{affineDifference\_iterate\_const\_smul}\\
\texttt{affineDifference\_iterate\_apply}\\
\texttt{iteratedDeriv\_eq\_affineDifference\_iterate\_on}\\
\texttt{iteratedDeriv\_eq\_affineDifference\_iterate}\\
\texttt{affineDifference\_iterate\_two\_one\_apply}.
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The first formula is a
Boolean-cube induction.  The second differentiates that finite operator orbit on the
invariant open set; its one-step derivative hypothesis bootstraps all regularity needed.
The last formula reindexes subsets by binary support, so its signs are precisely the
Thue--Morse signs.

Thus the conditional passage from \eqref{p1:eq:R-dde} to
\eqref{p1:eq:R-derivative-tree} is formalized in greater generality.  On the density side,
\path{iteratedDeriv_rvachevUp_eq_thueMorse_sum} applies the same engine to the
already formalized differential equation for \(\rho\), globally on the real line.  The
transform definition, density formula, natural-domain holomorphy, first Cauchy-kernel
derivative, and affine identity \(S(z)=2R(2z-1)\) are formalized in
\path{CauchyTransform.lean}.

The five public theorems in \path{CauchyRenormalization.lean} now close the
one-step and derivative-orbit obligations.  The declarations
\path{mapsTo_rvachevCauchyDomain_two_mul_add_one} and
\path{mapsTo_rvachevCauchyDomain_two_mul_sub_one} prove that both dyadic
branches preserve the slit domain.  For every bounded Fabius solution and
every \(z\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1]\),
\path{hasDerivAt_rvachevCauchyTransform_affineDifference} and
\path{deriv_rvachevCauchyTransform} prove \eqref{p1:eq:R-dde} directly, without
using the logarithmic fixed point.  Finally,
\path{iteratedDeriv_rvachevCauchyTransform_eq_thueMorse_sum} instantiates the
generic open-domain engine and proves exactly the derivative formula
\eqref{p1:eq:R-derivative-tree} for every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\).  The corresponding
unit-interval relation is now a direct named theorem, not merely a corollary through the
affine bridge:
\path{hasDerivAt_fabiusStieltjesTransform_refinement} proves
\(S'(z)=4(S(2z)-S(2z-1))\) throughout the unit slit domain.
The bridge itself has an all-power version
\path{fabiusStieltjesPower_eq_two_pow_mul_rvachevCauchyPower}, and
\path{fabiusStieltjesPower_succ} and \path{rvachevCauchyPower_succ} give the two
adjacent-order recurrences throughout their complex slit domains.

The real \(z>1\) instance of the logarithmic identity
\eqref{p1:eq:R-log-fixed} is a direct theorem of
\path{StieltjesLogFixedPoint.lean}, namely
\path{integral_inv_sub_eq_integral_log}; it is not a complex-logarithm or branch
continuation theorem.  \path{StieltjesResolventHierarchy.lean} gives the real positive
integer hierarchy, while \path{StieltjesGeneralizedOrder.lean} lowers real
\(\alpha>1\) at real \(z>1\).  The real and complex exterior moment/Laurent series,
finite-height Herglotz--Poisson formulas, integrated interval Stieltjes--Perron inversion,
and initial exact Jacobi data are likewise direct named results in the corresponding
\path{Stieltjes*.lean} and \path{OrthogonalPolynomialJacobi.lean} modules.
The later six-module tranche---\path{PolynomialMomentGramDeterminant.lean},
\path{LegendrePolynomialRational.lean}, \path{GramStieltjesNaturality.lean},
\path{RvachevRationalJacobi.lean}, \path{FabiusLegendreHankelDeterminant.lean}, and
\path{FabiusLegendreRationalGram.lean}---supplies polynomial-basis determinant
transport, executable rational ordinary-Legendre coefficients, scalar naturality, the exact
all-degree rational centered Jacobi system, and both the real and executable rational
Legendre Gram specializations.  Its exhaustive 63-name inventory and its zero-based-index
convention \(n\leftrightarrow\beta_{n+1}\) are recorded at the finite-algebra boundary below.
Continued-fraction identification and asymptotics are not part of that tranche.

Independently, \path{QBinomialReciprocity.lean} has no public definitions and exactly
four public theorems: \path{gaussianBinomial_reciprocity_units},
\path{gaussianBinomial_reciprocity},
\path{gaussianBinomial_neg_one_eq_zero_of_odd_degree}, and
\path{gaussianBinomial_neg_one_even_odd_eq_zero}.  The first is unit-valued reciprocity over
every commutative semiring, total above the diagonal; the second assumes precisely a nonzero
base in a semifield; and the last two give the odd-degree and total even-row/odd-column
zeros at \(q=-1\) over every commutative ring.  No quotient, cancellation, domain,
characteristic, topology, or convergence hypothesis is present.

The bounded-measure Laurent status is stronger than those two named
specializations.  \path{MeasureCauchyMomentLaurent.lean} contains exactly one
public definition and fifteen public theorems.  For every finite real measure,
every scalar field \(\mathbb K\) through Lean's
\texttt{\{K\} [RCLike K]} interface, arbitrary center \(c\in\mathbb K\),
radius \(R\ge0\), and almost-everywhere relative-ball bound
\(\lVert(x:\mathbb K)-c\rVert\le R\), it defines
\[
 m_n^{(c)}=\int ((x:\mathbb K)-c)^n\,\Differential\mu(x)
\]
and, when \(R<\lVert z-c\rVert\), proves the exact finite identity
\[
 \int\frac{\Differential\mu(x)}{z-x}
 =\sum_{k<N}\frac{m_k^{(c)}}{(z-c)^{k+1}}
  +(z-c)^{-N}\int\frac{((x:\mathbb K)-c)^N}{z-x}\,\Differential\mu(x).
\]
The same module proves measurability and the required kernel/remainder
integrability, the mass-scaled moment estimate, the sharp uniform remainder
bound
\[
 \left\lVert\int\frac{\Differential\mu(x)}{z-x}
 -\sum_{k<N}\frac{m_k^{(c)}}{(z-c)^{k+1}}\right\rVert
 \le \mu(\RealNumbers)(\lVert z-c\rVert-R)^{-1}
       \left(\frac{R}{\lVert z-c\rVert}\right)^N,
\]
and the resulting \path{Summable}, \path{tsum}, and \path{HasSum} forms;
complex wrappers connect the direct integral to
\path{measureCauchyTransform}.  The earlier up-measure statements in
\path{StieltjesMomentLaurent.lean} and
\path{StieltjesCauchyTransform.lean} are the \(c=0,R=1\) instances.
The compatibility declarations
\path{ae_norm_sub_zero_le_one_rvachevMeasure} and
\path{measureCauchyMoment_rvachevMeasure_zero} supply respectively the
relative-ball hypothesis and the coefficient identification with
\path{upMoment}.  None of these declarations proves the coefficient recursion
or the compact moment-determinacy/uniqueness argument used later in this
report.

Still open are the complex logarithmic continuation, complex order, pointwise or
nontangential Sokhotski--Plemelj/Hilbert-principal-value formulas, the full
J-fraction/Pad\'e package, and the separate conversion of the all-order derivative orbit
into the explicit higher-kernel identity \eqref{p1:eq:R-kernel-tree}.
\end{proof}

Taking boundary imaginary parts in \eqref{p1:eq:R-derivative-tree} recovers the familiar
iterated derivative formula for \(\rho\).  The new point is that the same finite
Thue--Morse orbit controls every higher Cauchy kernel, not only the density derivatives.

\subsection{Uniqueness in the Stieltjes class}

\begin{proposition}\label{p1:prop:R-uniqueness}
Among Cauchy transforms of probability measures supported on \([-1,1]\), \(R\) is the
unique solution of \eqref{p1:eq:R-dde} satisfying
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\frac1z+\BigO(z^{-3})\qquad(z\to\infty).               \label{p1:eq:R-normalization}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(G(z)=\sum_{r\ge0}m_r z^{-r-1}\) be the Laurent expansion at infinity of any such
Cauchy transform.  The normalization gives \(m_0=1\) and \(m_1=0\).  Substitute this
expansion into \eqref{p1:eq:R-dde}.  In the coefficient of \(z^{-r-2}\), the contribution
containing \(m_r\) is
\[
 -(r+1)m_r+\frac{r+1}{2^r}m_r
 =-(r+1)(1-2^{-r})m_r,
\]
while every other contribution contains only \(m_0,\ldots,m_{r-2}\).  Since the displayed
factor is nonzero for \(r\ge1\), the differential equation determines all moments
recursively.  The transform \(R\) is one solution, so \(G\) and \(R\) have identical
moments.  Probability measures on the compact interval \([-1,1]\) are determinate by their
moments (equivalently, polynomials are dense in \(C[-1,1]\)); hence the two measures and
their Cauchy transforms coincide.
\end{proof}

\section{Finite logarithmic resolvents and the spline boundary jump}

The finite random sum \(X_N\) has two exact representations: its density is a
Thue--Morse spline, while its Cauchy transform is a Thue--Morse sum of logarithms.  The
second formula appears to be absent from the audited corpus.

\begin{theorem}[Finite logarithmic formula]\label{p1:thm:finite-log}
Let
\[
 R_N(z)=\Expectation\frac1{z-X_N},
 \qquad X_N=\sum_{k=1}^{N}2^{-k}U_k.
\]
For \(z\notin[-1+2^{-N},1-2^{-N}]\), with logarithms continued consistently from
\(z=+\infty\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 R_N(z)=\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^N-1}\eps_j
 (z+a_{N,j})^{N-1}\PrincipalLogarithm(z+a_{N,j}).
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:RN-log}
\end{equation}
Moreover, \(R_N\to R\) locally uniformly on \(\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1]\), so
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^N-1}\eps_j
 (z+a_{N,j})^{N-1}\PrincipalLogarithm(z+a_{N,j}).                           \label{p1:eq:R-log-limit}
\end{equation}
Finally, the upper boundary jump of \eqref{p1:eq:RN-log} is exactly the spline
\eqref{p1:eq:finite-spline}:
\begin{equation}
 \rho_N(x)=-\frac1\pi\operatorname{Im}R_N(x+i0).
                                                                    \label{p1:eq:RN-boundary}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(a>0\), convolution with the uniform density on \([-a,a]\) sends a Cauchy transform
\(G'(z)\) to
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{2a}\int_{-a}^{a}G'(z-u)\,\Differential u
 =\frac{G(z+a)-G(z-a)}{2a}.                                  \label{p1:eq:uniform-difference}
\end{equation}
Start from the point mass at zero, whose Cauchy transform is \(1/z\).  An \(N\)-fold
antiderivative is
\[
 H_N(z)=\frac{z^{N-1}\PrincipalLogarithm z}{(N-1)!};
 \qquad H_N^{(N)}(z)=\frac1z.
\]
Applying \eqref{p1:eq:uniform-difference} successively with \(a_k=2^{-k}\) gives
\[
 R_N(z)=\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{N}2a_k}
 \sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm1\}^N}
 \left(\prod_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_k\right)
 H_N\!\left(z+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_k a_k\right).
\]
Since
\[
 \frac1{\prod_{k=1}^{N}2a_k}=2^{N(N-1)/2},
\]
and binary subsets identify the shifts and signs with \(a_{N,j}\) and \(\eps_j\), this is
\eqref{p1:eq:RN-log}.

The residual tail \(X-X_N\) has support in \([-2^{-N},2^{-N}]\), so \(X_N\to X\)
almost surely and uniformly in support.  The kernels \((z-x)^{-1}\) are uniformly
continuous on compact subsets away from \([-1,1]\); hence \(R_N\to R\) locally uniformly.

For the boundary value, \(\operatorname{Im}\PrincipalLogarithm(x+a+i0)=\pi\) when \(x+a<0\) and zero
when \(x+a>0\).  The alternating finite difference annihilates every polynomial of degree
less than \(N\), so the sum of the full powers \((x+a_{N,j})^{N-1}\) is zero.  Replacing the
negative-part sum by minus the positive-part sum yields exactly
\eqref{p1:eq:finite-spline}.  This proves \eqref{p1:eq:RN-boundary}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Two complementary approximation geometries]
The finite-convolution approximant \(R_N\) is a sum of logarithms and preserves the dyadic
construction.  The Jacobi approximants in Section~\ref{p1:sec:jacobi} are rational functions
and preserve moments.  Both converge locally uniformly off the support, but their poles,
branch cuts, and error mechanisms are completely different.  This duality suggests hybrid
algorithms: use finite convolution to resolve endpoint geometry and a short Jacobi fraction
to compress the remaining moment error.
\end{remark}

\section[Exact transforms of F, one-sided up, and inverse F]{Exact transforms of \(F\), one-sided \(\RvachevUp\), and \(F^{-1}\)}\label{p1:sec:fabius-transforms}

\subsection[The Laplace and Fourier transforms of F]{The Laplace and Fourier transforms of \(F\)}

\begin{theorem}[Transform of the Fabius function]\label{p1:thm:F-transform}
For complex \(s\), with the value at \(s=0\) understood by continuity,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \int_0^1 e^{-sx}F(x)\,\Differential x
 =\frac{e^{-s/2}M(s/2)-e^{-s}}{s}.
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:F-Laplace}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for real or complex \(\xi\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \int_0^1F(x)e^{-2\pi i\xi x}\,\Differential x
 =\frac{e^{-\pi i\xi}\Phi(\xi/2)-e^{-2\pi i\xi}}{2\pi i\xi}.
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:F-Fourier}
\end{equation}
At \(\xi=0\), the value is \(1/2\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The distributional Laplace transform of \(Y\) is
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1 e^{-sx}\,\Differential F(x)=\Expectation e^{-sY}=e^{-s/2}M(s/2).       \label{p1:eq:dF-Laplace}
\end{equation}
Integration by parts gives
\[
 \int_0^1e^{-sx}\,\Differential F(x)
 =e^{-s}+s\int_0^1e^{-sx}F(x)\,\Differential x,
\]
which is \eqref{p1:eq:F-Laplace}.  Substitute \(s=2\pi i\xi\) and use
\(M(\pi i\xi)=\Phi(\xi/2)\).
\end{proof}

Separating real and imaginary parts gives the explicit sine and cosine transforms
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1F(x)\cos(2\pi\xi x)\,\Differential x
 &=\frac{\sin(2\pi\xi)-\Phi(\xi/2)\sin(\pi\xi)}{2\pi\xi},    \label{p1:eq:F-cosine-transform}\\
 \int_0^1F(x)\sin(2\pi\xi x)\,\Differential x
 &=\frac{\Phi(\xi/2)\cos(\pi\xi)-\cos(2\pi\xi)}{2\pi\xi}. \label{p1:eq:F-sine-transform}
\end{align}

\subsection{One-sided transforms of the up-function}

On \([0,1]\), \(\rho(x)=F(1-x)=1-F(x)\).  Therefore the previous theorem immediately
yields the following closed forms.

\begin{theorem}[One-sided transform package]\label{p1:thm:one-sided}
For complex \(s\), with the removable value at zero,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \int_0^1e^{-sx}\rho(x)\,\Differential x
 =\frac{1-e^{-s/2}M(s/2)}{s}.
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:rho-one-sided-Laplace}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \int_0^1e^{-2\pi i\xi x}\rho(x)\,\Differential x
 =\frac{1-e^{-\pi i\xi}\Phi(\xi/2)}{2\pi i\xi}.
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:rho-one-sided-Fourier}
\end{equation}
The half-line cosine and sine transforms are
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\rho(x)\cos(2\pi\xi x)\,\Differential x
 &=\frac12\Phi(\xi),                                        \label{p1:eq:rho-half-cos}\\
 \int_0^1\rho(x)\sin(2\pi\xi x)\,\Differential x
 &=\frac{1-\Phi(\xi/2)\cos(\pi\xi)}{2\pi\xi}.             \label{p1:eq:rho-half-sin}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

The sum of \eqref{p1:eq:rho-one-sided-Laplace} at \(s\) and \(-s\) recovers
\(M(s)\) precisely because
\begin{equation}
 M(s)=\frac{2\sinh(s/2)}{s}M(s/2),                           \label{p1:eq:M-scaling}
\end{equation}
which is the hyperbolic continuation of \eqref{p1:eq:Phi-scaling}.

\subsection{The inverse Fabius function as a transport map}

Let \(I=F^{-1}\) on \([0,1]\).  Since \(I\) pushes Lebesgue measure forward to the law
of \(Y\), every integral of a composition with \(I\) reduces to the up-law.

\begin{theorem}[Inverse pushforward master identity]\label{p1:thm:inverse-pushforward}
For every Borel function \(g\) for which one side is integrable,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \int_0^1g(I(u))\,\Differential u
 =\int_0^1g(x)\,\Differential F(x)
 =\int_{-1}^{1}g\!\left(\frac{x+1}{2}\right)\rho(x)\,\Differential x.
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:inverse-master}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1 e^{-sI(u)}\,\Differential u
 &=e^{-s/2}M(s/2),                                           \label{p1:eq:inverse-Laplace}\\
 \int_0^1 e^{-2\pi i\xi I(u)}\,\Differential u
 &=e^{-\pi i\xi}\Phi(\xi/2),                              \label{p1:eq:inverse-Fourier}\\
 \int_0^1\frac{\Differential u}{z-I(u)}
 &=2R(2z-1),\qquad z\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus[0,1],                   \label{p1:eq:inverse-Cauchy}\\
 \int_0^1I(u)^n\,\Differential u
 &=2^{-n}\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{n/2}}
   \binom{n}{2r}\mu_{2r}.                                   \label{p1:eq:inverse-moments}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\), then \(I(U)\) has CDF \(F\), hence the same law as
\(Y\).  This proves \eqref{p1:eq:inverse-master}.  The remaining formulas follow by taking
\(g(x)=e^{-sx}\), \(e^{-2\pi i\xi x}\), \((z-x)^{-1}\), and \(x^n\), respectively,
and then using \eqref{p1:eq:Y-affine} and symmetry of \(X\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Formula \eqref{p1:eq:inverse-Cauchy} transports the entire Jacobi-fraction theory of \(R\) to
the inverse Fabius function.  Thus a function whose pointwise inverse asymptotics involve
Lambert \(W\) nevertheless has a globally simple Cauchy transform when sampled with
uniform measure in its range variable.
\end{remark}

\section{Logarithmic derivatives of the Fourier image}

\subsection{Three meromorphic expansions}

\begin{theorem}[Cotangent, tangent, and zero-set formulas]\label{p1:thm:three-logder}
Away from the integer zero set of \(\Phi\),
\begin{align}
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
 \left[
 \frac{\pi}{2^k}\cot\!\left(\frac{\pi z}{2^k}\right)-\frac1z
 \right],                                                    \label{p1:eq:logder-cot}\\
 &=-\pi\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{m}{2^m}
 \tan\!\left(\frac{\pi z}{2^m}\right),                    \label{p1:eq:logder-tan}\\
 &=-2z\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}{n^2-z^2}.       \label{p1:eq:logder-zero}
\end{align}
All three series converge locally normally on compact subsets avoiding the poles.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the logarithm of each of the three products
\eqref{p1:eq:Phi-products} and \eqref{p1:eq:canonical-product}.  For large \(k\), the bracket
in \eqref{p1:eq:logder-cot} and the term in \eqref{p1:eq:logder-tan} are \(\BigO(4^{-k})\)
uniformly on compact sets.  The terms in \eqref{p1:eq:logder-zero} are bounded by a constant
times \((1+\TwoAdicValuation(n))/n^2\), which is summable by \eqref{p1:eq:spectral-zeta} at \(s=2\).
\end{proof}

The three forms have different numerical regimes.  The tangent sum is usually the fastest
away from its poles; the cotangent sum tracks the original sinc factors; and the zero-set
sum exposes multiplicities and supports rigorous rational tail bounds.

\subsection{Digamma and Hurwitz-zeta hierarchy}

The valuation weight has the divisor decomposition
\begin{equation}
 1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\IndicatorOf{2^k\mid n}.     \label{p1:eq:valuation-indicators}
\end{equation}
This turns the zero sum into a dyadic superposition of classical special functions.

\begin{theorem}[Digamma and Hurwitz-zeta representations]\label{p1:thm:hurwitz}
Let \(\psi=\Gamma'/\Gamma\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-k}
 \left[
 \psi\!\left(1-\frac{z}{2^k}\right)
 -\psi\!\left(1+\frac{z}{2^k}\right)
 \right].
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:logder-digamma}
\end{equation}
For every integer \(r\ge2\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \frac{\Differential^r}{\Differential z^r}\log\Phi(z)
 =(r-1)!\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-kr}
 \left[
 -\zeta\!\left(r,1-\frac{z}{2^k}\right)
 +(-1)^{r-1}\zeta\!\left(r,1+\frac{z}{2^k}\right)
 \right].
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:logder-hurwitz}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
From \eqref{p1:eq:canonical-product}, for \(r\ge1\), paired symmetrically when \(r=1\),
\begin{equation}
 (\log\Phi)^{(r)}(z)
 =(r-1)!\sum_{n\ge1}(1+\TwoAdicValuation(n))
 \left[-\frac1{(n-z)^r}+\frac{(-1)^{r-1}}{(n+z)^r}\right].   \label{p1:eq:logder-general-zero}
\end{equation}
Insert \eqref{p1:eq:valuation-indicators}, write \(n=2^k m\), and sum over \(m\ge1\).
For \(r\ge2\), the inner sums are Hurwitz zeta functions.  For \(r=1\), the convergent
paired sum is the standard digamma difference.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Local jet data at nonzero zeros]
Let \(N\ne0\) be an integer and \(d_N=1+\TwoAdicValuation(|N|)\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}=\frac{d_N}{z-N}+H_N(z),             \label{p1:eq:local-logder-zero}
\end{equation}
where \(H_N\) is holomorphic near \(N\), and every derivative of \(H_N\) can be written
as an absolutely convergent valuation-weighted Hurwitz-zeta sum.  Thus the complete local
Taylor jet of \(\Phi(z)/(z-N)^{d_N}\) is algorithmically explicit.
\end{corollary}

\section{A Fredholm determinant for the Fourier product}\label{p1:sec:fredholm}

The canonical product can be realized as an ordinary Fredholm determinant, not merely as a
formal spectral product.

\begin{definition}
Let
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal I=\{(n,r):n\ge1,\ 1\le r\le 1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)\},
\end{equation}
and define the positive diagonal operator \(\cK\) on \(\ell^2(\mathcal I)\) by
\begin{equation}
 \cK e_{n,r}=\frac1{n^2}e_{n,r}.                             \label{p1:eq:K-def}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Fredholm determinant representation]\label{p1:thm:fredholm}
The operator \(\cK\) is trace class and
\begin{align}
 \boxed{\Phi(z)=\det(I-z^2\cK)},                             \label{p1:eq:Phi-fredholm}\\
 \boxed{M(t)=\det\!\left(I+\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2}\cK\right)}.  \label{p1:eq:M-fredholm}
\end{align}
Furthermore,
\begin{align}
 \TraceOperator(\cK^m)&=\frac{\zeta(2m)}{1-2^{-2m}},        \label{p1:eq:K-traces}\\
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}
 &=-2z\,\TraceOperator\!\left(\cK(I-z^2\cK)^{-1}\right). \label{p1:eq:K-resolvent-trace}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{p1:eq:spectral-zeta},
\[
 \TraceOperator\cK=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}{n^2}
 =\frac{\zeta(2)}{1-2^{-2}}<\infty,
\]
so \(\cK\) is trace class.  Its Fredholm determinant is the product over its eigenvalues,
with multiplicity:
\[
 \det(I-z^2\cK)
 =\prod_{n\ge1}\left(1-\frac{z^2}{n^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}
 =\Phi(z).
\]
Substituting \(z=it/(2\pi)\) gives \eqref{p1:eq:M-fredholm}.  Formula
\eqref{p1:eq:K-traces} is again \eqref{p1:eq:spectral-zeta}, and differentiating the Fredholm
logarithm gives \eqref{p1:eq:K-resolvent-trace}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
This representation converts several scalar identities into operator identities.  For
example, the zeta expansion \eqref{p1:eq:logPhi-zeta} is the standard trace expansion
\[
 \log\det(I-z^2\cK)=-\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{z^{2m}}m\TraceOperator(\cK^m).
\]
It also suggests perturbation theory for generalized exponent sequences: changing the
multiplicity sequence changes only the diagonal spectral multiplicities.
\end{remark}

\section{Jacobi continued fractions and exact rational coefficients}\label{p1:sec:jacobi}

\subsection{Exact moment recurrence}

\begin{proposition}[Moment recurrence]\label{p1:prop:moment-recurrence}
The even moments satisfy \(\mu_0=1\) and, for \(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 (4^n-1)\mu_{2n}
 =\sum_{j=1}^{n}\binom{2n}{2j}\frac{\mu_{2n-2j}}{2j+1}.
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:moment-recurrence}
\end{equation}
In particular, every \(\mu_{2n}\) is rational.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Raise \eqref{p1:eq:X-fixed-point} to the \(2n\)-th power and take expectations:
\[
 4^n\mu_{2n}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{2n}{2j}\Expectation U^{2j}\,\mu_{2n-2j}.
\]
Since \(\Expectation U^{2j}=1/(2j+1)\), the \(j=0\) term is \(\mu_{2n}\).  Move it to the left.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Monic orthogonal polynomials}

Let \(P_n\) be the monic orthogonal polynomials for the probability measure
\(\rho(x)\Differential x\), and let
\begin{equation}
 h_n=\int_{-1}^{1}P_n(x)^2\rho(x)\,\Differential x.                    \label{p1:eq:hn}
\end{equation}
Evenness forces the diagonal Jacobi parameters to vanish, so
\begin{equation}
 P_0(x)=1,\
 P_1(x)=x,
 \qquad
 P_{n+1}(x)=xP_n(x)-\beta_nP_{n-1}(x),
 \qquad
 \beta_n=\frac{h_n}{h_{n-1}}>0.                              \label{p1:eq:monic-recurrence}
\end{equation}
The first polynomials are
\begin{align}
 P_0(x)&=1,\\
 P_1(x)&=x,\\
 P_2(x)&=x^2-\frac19,\\
 P_3(x)&=x^3-\frac{19}{75}x,\\
 P_4(x)&=x^4-\frac{62}{147}x^2+\frac{619}{33075}.
\end{align}

\subsection{The Jacobi and Stieltjes fractions}

\begin{theorem}[Jacobi continued fraction]\label{p1:thm:J-fraction}
The Cauchy transform has the locally uniform continued fraction
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 R(z)=\cfrac{1}{z-\cfrac{\beta_1}{z-\cfrac{\beta_2}{z-
           \cfrac{\beta_3}{z-\ddots}}}}.
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:R-J-fraction}
\end{equation}
The coefficients are positive rational numbers.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Positivity follows from \(\beta_n=h_n/h_{n-1}\), and rationality follows because
Gram--Schmidt applied to the rational moment functional \eqref{p1:eq:moment-recurrence}
uses only rational operations.  Markov's theorem for compactly supported positive measures
identifies the continued fraction generated by \eqref{p1:eq:monic-recurrence} with the Cauchy
transform and gives local uniform convergence off the support \cite{p1:VanAssche1993,p1:SimonOPRL}.
\end{proof}

The all-degree rational coefficients, positivity, zero diagonal, determinant
cross-ratio, orthogonality, and centered three-term recurrence are now formalized in
\path{RvachevRationalJacobi.lean}.  The generic polynomial-basis determinant transport and
its ordinary-Legendre specialization are now formalized in
\path{PolynomialMomentGramDeterminant.lean} and
\path{FabiusLegendreHankelDeterminant.lean}.  The second-kind-polynomial construction,
identification of the finite or infinite continued fraction with \(R\), and Markov
convergence used in this theorem remain paper-level claims rather than results of those
Lean modules.

Define the ordinary even-moment generating function
\begin{equation}
 \EvenMomentOrdinaryGeneratingFunction(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu_{2n}t^n
 =\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\rho(x)}{1-tx^2}\,\Differential x,
 \qquad |t|<1.                                               \label{p1:eq:O-def}
\end{equation}
Substituting \(t=z^{-2}\) into \eqref{p1:eq:R-J-fraction} gives the contracted fraction.

\begin{corollary}[Stieltjes fraction and product integral]\label{p1:cor:S-fraction}
For \(|t|<1\),
\begin{align}
 \boxed{
 \EvenMomentOrdinaryGeneratingFunction(t)=\cfrac{1}{1-\cfrac{\beta_1t}{1-\cfrac{\beta_2t}{1-
               \cfrac{\beta_3t}{1-\ddots}}}}
 }                                                           \label{p1:eq:O-S-fraction}\\
 \EvenMomentOrdinaryGeneratingFunction(t)=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-u}
 \prod_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{\sinh(\sqrt t\,u/2^k)}{\sqrt t\,u/2^k}\,\Differential u.    \label{p1:eq:O-product-integral}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The continued fraction follows from
\(\EvenMomentOrdinaryGeneratingFunction(z^{-2})=zR(z)\).  For the integral, use
\[
 \frac1{1-tx^2}=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-u}\cosh(\sqrt t\,xu)\,\Differential u
\]
and then average over \(X\), obtaining \(M(\sqrt t\,u)\).
\end{proof}

For \(s\ge0\), \(\EvenMomentOrdinaryGeneratingFunction(-s)\) is completely monotone:
\begin{equation}
 (-1)^m\frac{\Differential^m}{\Differential s^m}
 \EvenMomentOrdinaryGeneratingFunction(-s)
 =m!\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{x^{2m}\rho(x)}{(1+sx^2)^{m+1}}\,\Differential x>0. \label{p1:eq:O-complete-monotone}
\end{equation}
Thus the even moments form a Stieltjes moment sequence, and the fraction
\eqref{p1:eq:O-S-fraction} belongs to the classical positive Stieltjes class.

\subsection{Hankel determinants}

Let
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_n=\det[\mu_{i+j}]_{i,j=0}^{n-1},\qquad \Delta_0=1.  \label{p1:eq:Hankel}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 h_n=\frac{\Delta_{n+1}}{\Delta_n},
 \qquad
 \boxed{\beta_n=\frac{\Delta_{n+1}\Delta_{n-1}}{\Delta_n^2}}. \label{p1:eq:beta-Hankel}
\end{equation}
Because odd moments vanish, each \(\Delta_n\) factors after a parity permutation into an
even-even and odd-odd moment determinant.  This parity factorization is useful for exact
arithmetic and should be exploited in future Lean formalization.

\begin{remark}[Formal finite-algebra boundary]
\path{FiniteMomentGram.lean} and \path{GramStieltjes.lean} provide a
measure-free version of the finite determinant algebra: an arbitrary scalar
sequence gives a moment functional, symmetric Hankel pairing, fixed-order
nondegeneracy criterion, fraction-free adjugate polynomial, and---over a field
with the leading minor nonzero---the unique normalized monic orthogonal
polynomial with self-pairing \(\Delta_{n+1}/\Delta_n\).  The complementary
up-measure modules \path{MomentHankelMatrix.lean},
\path{MomentHankelValues.lean}, and \path{OrthogonalPolynomial*.lean} prove
strict positivity, identify the exact moments, construct the monic
polynomials, prove parity and the symmetric three-term recurrence, and compute
the first exact Jacobi data.  The generic continuation
\path{FiniteMomentJacobi.lean} defines the norm quotients, diagonal and
subdiagonal Jacobi coefficients, proves the unconditional total-field identity
\[
 \beta_{n+1}=\frac{\Delta_{n+2}\Delta_n}{\Delta_{n+1}^2},
\]
and, under the three consecutive nonzero-minor hypotheses, proves
\[
 XP_{n+1}=P_{n+2}+\alpha_{n+1}P_{n+1}+\beta_{n+1}P_n.
\]
Its separate theorem
\path{gramStieltjesPolynomial_three_term_zero} supplies the base equation
$XP_0=P_1+\alpha_0P_0$ from the nonzero first minor.  This complete finite
recurrence layer uses no measure or positivity.  Finally,
\path{OrthogonalPolynomialGramBridge.lean} proves that
the up-moment functional is integration against the canonical up measure and
identifies the generic matrix, determinant, fraction-free polynomial, and
monic polynomial exactly with \path{momentHankel}, \path{hankelDet},
\path{hankelOrthoPolynomial}, and \path{upOrthoPolynomial}; it also identifies
the generic norm with \path{hankelRatio}, proves that symmetry kills the
generic diagonal coefficient, and identifies the generic subdiagonal with the
quotient of consecutive Hankel ratios.  Root and
quadrature theorems, second-kind polynomials, continued-fraction
identification, and analytic convergence remain open.

The polynomial-basis transport leaf
\path{PolynomialMomentGramDeterminant.lean} has exactly two public
definitions---\path{polynomialCoefficientMatrix} and
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix}---and seven public theorems:
\path{polynomialCoefficientMatrix_apply},
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix_apply},
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix_eq_transpose_mul_hankel_mul},
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix_det_eq_coefficient_det_sq_mul},
\path{polynomialCoefficientMatrix_det_eq_prod_coeff},
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix_det_eq_prod_coeff_sq_mul}, and
\path{gramStieltjesJacobiSubdiagonal_eq_polynomialMomentGramMatrix_det_ratio}.
For a family whose \(k\)-th polynomial has degree at most \(k\), the matrix
congruence is over a commutative semiring and the determinant identities are
over a commutative ring.  The final field-level formula additionally assumes
every diagonal coefficient is nonzero; it needs no Hankel nonvanishing because
division is total.  This is measure-free finite algebra and makes no
positivity, orthogonality, root, quadrature, continued-fraction, or convergence
claim.

The rational ordinary-Legendre leaf \path{LegendrePolynomialRational.lean} has exactly two
public definitions---the executable coefficient sum
\path{legendrePolynomialCoeffRat} and the noncomputable polynomial wrapper
\path{legendrePolynomialRat}---and six public theorems:
\path{legendrePolynomialRat_cast}, \path{coeff_legendrePolynomialRat},
\path{natDegree_legendrePolynomialRat},
\path{coeff_legendrePolynomialRat_self},
\path{coeff_legendrePolynomialRat_self_ne_zero}, and
\path{coeff_legendrePolynomialRat_self_div_succ}.  These identify every wrapper coefficient
with the bounded rational sum and its real cast with the ordinary Legendre polynomial, and
prove degree \(n\), leading coefficient \(2^{-n}\binom{2n}{n}\), its nonvanishing, and
consecutive quotient \((n+1)/(2n+1)\).  They prove no root, Christoffel, quadrature,
orthogonality, or asymptotic statement.

The scalar-base-change leaf \path{GramStieltjesNaturality.lean} has zero
definitions and exactly six public theorems:
\path{momentPairing_map}, \path{map_gramStieltjesNumerator},
\path{map_gramStieltjesPolynomial}, \path{map_gramStieltjesNorm},
\path{map_gramStieltjesJacobiDiagonal}, and
\path{map_gramStieltjesJacobiSubdiagonal}.  The numerator result is valid at
ring level; the normalized-polynomial and Jacobi-quotient results use fields.
All six are finite algebra, with no measure, positivity, or convergence.

The coherent-basis module \path{PolynomialMomentGramDeterminant.lean} has two
public definitions, \path{polynomialCoefficientMatrix} and
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix}, and seven public theorems: the two entry formulas,
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix_eq_transpose_mul_hankel_mul},
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix_det_eq_coefficient_det_sq_mul},
\path{polynomialCoefficientMatrix_det_eq_prod_coeff},
\path{polynomialMomentGramMatrix_det_eq_prod_coeff_sq_mul}, and
\path{gramStieltjesJacobiSubdiagonal_eq_polynomialMomentGramMatrix_det_ratio}.
Thus a degree-coherent family satisfies \(G_n=C_n^{\mathsf T}H_nC_n\), with determinant
equal to the monomial Hankel determinant times the squared product of its diagonal
coefficients, and nonzero diagonal coefficients transport the finite Jacobi cross-ratio.
The coefficient matrix and entry formula are semiring-level; the Gram matrix and
congruence are commutative-semiring-level; determinants are commutative-ring-level; and
the quotient is field-level.

The rational specialization \path{RvachevRationalJacobi.lean} has exactly four

\path{rvachevOrthoPolynomialRat}, \path{rvachevOrthoNormRat}, and

the positive squared norm, zero diagonal, and positive subdiagonal, as well as
the real-cast bridges, rational orthogonality, determinant cross-ratio, and

noncomputable adjugate-based finite algebra; the separate executable values leaf
below now evaluates the report's displayed \(H_4\) and \(\beta_4\) fractions.
The combined tranche still proves no roots, Christoffel formulas, second-kind polynomials, Gaussian
quadrature, continued-fraction/Pad\'e identification or convergence, central
products, or Szeg\H{o}/Nevai/Rakhmanov asymptotics.

The Legendre specialization \path{FabiusLegendreHankelDeterminant.lean} has

alone does not prove a Gaunt/Wigner-symbol expansion or entry rationality by that

The executable rational specialization \path{FabiusLegendreRationalGram.lean} has exactly

The bounded rational entry sum, finite matrix, and determinant agree with the abstract

The exact values leaf \path{FabiusLegendreRationalGramValues.lean} has zero public

\path{moment_four},

The first theorem computes the raw eighth moment as \(132809/32531625\).  The next five
compute the order-one through order-five rational Legendre Gram determinants as
\(1\), \(1/9\), \(8/2025\), \(39616/602791875\), and
\(16544275456/27453718922765625\).  The next two give the report values
\[
 H_4=\frac{26727424}{55791736875},
 \qquad
 \beta_4=\frac{835232}{4640643},
\]
with the subdiagonal index zero-based.  Those eight rational computations have no
analytic input.  Each of the three real/integral transports requires an input
\(F\) of type \path{BoundedFabius} together with a certificate
\(h_F\) of type \path{IsFabius}; they identify the
real fourth Hankel ratio, its squared orthogonal-polynomial integral, and its quotient
by the third ratio.  This values leaf closes the displayed low-order values, not by
itself the finite Gaunt/Wigner/\(3j\) entry expansion, Christoffel reconstruction,
root or quadrature theory, infinite Jacobi products/continued fractions, or
asymptotics.  The current-source overlay immediately below closes the finite
zero-row-square part of the Gaunt/Wigner boundary.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Current-source finite Gaunt--Wigner-square overlay]
The exact Gaunt surface now consists of four modules.  The predecessor
\path{LegendreGaunt.lean} and \path{FabiusLegendreGaunt.lean} modules expose,
respectively, four definitions plus twelve theorems and one definition plus eight
theorems.  The new \path{LegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} leaf exposes exactly two
definitions plus twenty-five theorems, and
\path{FabiusLegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} exposes zero definitions plus three
theorems.  The overlay therefore has seven definitions and forty-eight theorems,
fifty-five public declarations.

The two new definitions are the parity-and-weak-triangle predicate
\path{legendreGauntAdmissible} and the total rational integer-index zero-row square
datum \path{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat}.  Admissibility is equivalent to
\(i=b+c\), \(j=a+c\), and \(k=a+b\).  For \(s=a+b+c\), the exact central-binomial
formula is
\[
 W_{ijk}^{2}=
 \frac{\binom{2a}{a}\binom{2b}{b}\binom{2c}{c}}
      {(2s+1)\binom{2s}{s}},
\]
with equivalent factorial and half-sum forms.  The declarations
\path{legendreGauntRat_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat} and
\path{legendreGaunt_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat} give
\[
 G_{ijk}^{\RationalNumbers}=2W_{ijk}^{2},
 \qquad
 G_{ijk}^{\RealNumbers}=2(W_{ijk}^{2}:\RealNumbers).
\]
The square datum and both Gaunt coefficients are positive exactly on the
admissible support and zero off it, and all are nonnegative.  The coefficient of
\(P_k\) in \(P_iP_j\) is \((2k+1)W_{ijk}^{2}\).  This strengthens the predecessor's
one-way parity and triangle vanishing statements to exact support characterizations.

The three Fabius-specific declarations
\path{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\path{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\path{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}
substitute that identity into the finite Gaunt sums.  Rationally and without analytic
hypotheses,
\[
 H_{ij}^{\RationalNumbers}=2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}c_rW_{ij,2r}^{2};
\]
the real up-law matrix version requires exactly an input of type
\path{BoundedFabius} satisfying \path{IsFabius}.

This is deliberately a square-level statement: it makes no signed-symbol or
Condon--Shortley phase choice and proves no half-integer, nonzero-magnetic-index,
general \(3j/6j/9j\), Wigner-orthogonality, recoupling, or infinite-series result.
Accordingly, historical claims below that the finite Wigner identification was open
describe their pinned predecessor snapshots; they do not describe the current source.
\end{remark}

\eqref{p1:eq:monic-recurrence}.  Selected results are:

\end{center}
