\part{Representation Atlas and New Analytic Bridges for the Fabius and Rvachev Functions}
% Absorbed from fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier/fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier_report.tex




\begin{abstract}
This report gives a source-audited atlas of integral, series, product, convolution,
probabilistic, spectral, and transform representations of the bounded Fabius function
$F$, Rvachev's compactly supported even function $\RvachevUp$, the inverse Fabius function
$Q=F^{-1}$, and the entire Fourier image
\(
 \Phi(z)=\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(z).
\)
The baseline material is organized from the current formal exposition and the living
non-formalized frontier documents in the \texttt{ProveIt} repository.  Previously present
results -- including the sinc and cosine products, the arithmetic canonical product,
Fredholm determinants, half-integer spectral series, $q$-binomial moment formulae,
Thue--Morse splines, the generalized-gamma-convolution dual, and the logistic dual -- are
explicitly quarantined from novelty claims.

Five new-to-the-audited-corpus theorem packages are developed.  First, a meromorphic
\emph{dyadic gamma function}
\[
 \Gamma_{\!\mathrm{dy}}(z)=\prod_{h\ge0}\Gamma(1+2^{-h}z)
\]
factors the Rvachev transform as
\(
 \Phi(z)=\bigl(\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{dy}}(z)
 \Gamma_{\!\mathrm{dy}}(-z)\bigr)^{-1}
\), with functional equations, canonical products, digamma and heat-trace integrals, and a
geometric-$q$ extension.  Second, the existing dyadic logistic series is identified in law
with an explicit Gaussian variance mixture driven by the existing discrete Thorin random
variable; this yields a convolution semigroup, a closed Levy density, complete monotonicity
of the density as a function of $x^2$, and dimension-free radial positive definiteness.
Third, Jensen's circular mean of $\log|\Phi|$ is evaluated exactly by binary digit sums and a
finite factorial product.  Fourth, the unbounded zero multiplicities prove that $\Phi$ is
not D-finite, and neither are the dyadic gamma factors.  Fifth, distributional and compact
Fourier formulae for $F$ are completed by an odd-harmonic sine series, while $Q$ receives a
new nonlinear Fourier transform and sine-series representation in terms of oscillatory
integrals of $F$.

The formal source checkpoint
\path{71ab6f6728fceb753c88d8b0573077a59acf2682} now covers the entire reciprocal product,
its Mahler equation and zero orbit, the reflected sinc product and dyadic Rvachev bridge, and
the exact dyadic zero and meromorphic pole orders.  It does not cover the raw product of
Gamma factors away from its poles, the arithmetic canonical regrouping, logarithmic or
digamma expansions, Malmsten or heat-trace integrals, the displayed Fourier integrals,
non-D-finiteness, the reciprocal-Gamma derivative coefficient used by the separate
Thue--Morse tower, or the general integer-base collision order; those portions remain
frontier claims despite occurring in the same theorem package below.

\emph{Current post-checkpoint overlay.}  The historical boundary above is retained
verbatim for \path{71ab6f6728fceb753c88d8b0573077a59acf2682}.  At source commit
\path{0ba35abd4}, \path{ReciprocalGammaJets.lean} adds exactly five public
declarations:
\leanPartx{Fabius.deriv_Gamma_inv_neg_nat},
\leanPartx{Fabius.hasDerivAt_Gamma_inv_neg_nat},
\leanPartx{Fabius.hasDerivAt_Gamma_inv_zero},
\leanPartx{Fabius.analyticOrderAt_Gamma_inv_neg_nat}, and
\leanPartx{Fabius.tendsto_Gamma_inv_div_add_nat}.  They give, for every
\(r\in\NaturalNumbers\), the first jet \((\Gamma^{-1})'(-r)=(-1)^r r!\), simple analytic
order one, and the same punctured local coefficient.  These are theorems about
the entire reciprocal function; they do not assign a derivative to raw Gamma
at a pole.

The same commit gives the first eight public declarations of
\path{ThueMorseGammaTower.lean}:
\leanPartx{Fabius.hasDerivAt_dirichletMellinContinuation_neg_nat},
\leanPartx{Fabius.deriv_dirichletMellinContinuation_neg_nat},
\leanPartx{Fabius.thueMorseGammaLog},
\leanPartx{Fabius.thueMorseGammaTower},
\leanPartx{Fabius.thueMorseGammaLog_eq_mellin},
\leanPartx{Fabius.thueMorseGammaLog_eq_integral},
\leanPartx{Fabius.thueMorseGammaLog_dyadic}, and
\leanPartx{Fabius.thueMorseGammaTower_dyadic}.  For every \(r\in\NaturalNumbers\) and
\(a>0\), they identify the continuation first jet with
\((-1)^r r!\) times the Mellin value, give its real-integral form, and prove the
dyadic GammaLog and exponentiated tower laws.  The GammaLog and tower
definitions are total for every real \(a\), but those identification theorems
assume \(a>0\).  GammaLog is a chosen derivative coordinate, not a proved
\leanPartx{Complex.log} identity.  The current integrated tree adds the ninth
public declaration \leanPartx{Fabius.ofReal_exp_mpLimit_eq_gammaTower_div},
the level-zero tower quotient for positive \(a,b\).  Accordingly the first-jet,
Mellin/integral GammaLog, dyadic log/tower, and ratio layers are no longer
frontiers.  The parameter-\(a\) differential and iterated differential ladder
remains open, as do the raw Gamma tprod, canonical regrouping,
logarithmic/digamma, Gamma-product Malmsten/heat-trace integrals, D-finite, and
general-base collision-order claims
listed above.

A fully commented Python program independently checks the principal identities, produces
four figures, and records truncation diagnostics.  ``Proved here'' means proved in this
report and not found by targeted collision searches in the audited repository; it is not a
claim of priority over all external literature.
\end{abstract}


\clearpage

\section{Scope, source audit, and status discipline}
\label{p2:sec:audit}

\subsection{Repository boundary}

The requested source boundary was the recursive collection of \texttt{*.tex} files below
\begin{center}
\small
\url{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt/tree/main/Analysis/FabiusFunction/docs}.
\end{center}
The primary formal exposition was pinned to repository commit
\CommitID, dated 27 August 2026.  During the audit the
branch changed: in particular, a support-free quantile substitution theorem entered the
primary exposition.  Consequently, quantile pushforward formulae that looked new against an
earlier snapshot are classified here as repository consequences, not new results.

The recursive audit included the current formal exposition, the consolidated frontier
notebook, the glossary and Lean walkthrough, the active frontier directories, the embedded
literature sources, and the archive provenance map.  The active frontier root contained
seventeen named draft packages at the time of the audit.  One of those packages records a
snapshot with sixty-seven distinct TeX paths.  Historical byte-identical copies were not
counted as independent mathematical sources: the archive provenance file was used to map
them to their living successors, after which the successor source was audited at formula
level.  This avoids manufacturing ``novelty'' merely by overlooking a renamed or moved draft.

\subsection{Collision quarantine}

The following structures were already present and are therefore baseline in this report:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item the infinite sinc product, weighted cosine product, and integer-zero canonical product;
\item the arithmetic multiplicities $a_n=1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$, their Dirichlet series, counting law
      $\sum_{n\le N}a_n=2N-\BinaryDigitSum(N)$, lobe signs, heat traces, spectral zeta functions, and
      Fredholm determinant;
\item half-integer cosine expansions, spectral quadrature, sampling and alias formulae;
\item moments and cumulants through Bell and Bernoulli numbers, $q$-binomial and multiple-zeta
      formulae, Appell sequences, Barnes-type prefixes, and Thue--Morse finite blocks;
\item the discrete-Thorin generalized gamma convolution
      $\Expectation\EulerE^{-wV_\tau}=\Acal(w)^{-\tau}$ and its complete-Bernstein/Stieltjes consequences;
\item the dyadic logistic series whose characteristic function is the reciprocal moment
      generating function of the compact Rvachev law;
\item exact negative-Laplace decompositions, periodic lower-Lambert endpoint asymptotics,
      inverse asymptotics, and the now-formal quantile substitution theorem.
\end{itemize}
Targeted searches did not find the phrases or exact structures ``dyadic gamma'', ``Gaussian
variance mixture'', ``Jensen circular mean'', ``D-finite'', the principal-value transform
of the global Fabius CDF, or the inverse sine coefficients derived in
\cref{p2:sec:inverse-fourier}.  These results are marked \New.  Formulae obtained by combining
known repository theorems without a substantively new bridge are marked \Derived.

\subsection{Status legend and limits of the novelty statement}

\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.18\textwidth}p{0.75\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Status & Meaning \\
\midrule
\endhead
\Repo & Present in the formal exposition or an audited frontier document. \\
\Derived & A direct synthesis or reformulation of repository results; no novelty claim. \\
\New & Proved in this report and not located by targeted collision searches in the audited
       corpus.  This is a corpus-relative statement, not universal priority. \\
\Conj & A plausible but unproved statement, separated from theorems. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\section{Normalization and the master probability diagram}
\label{p2:sec:normalization}

\subsection{The compact law and its CDF}

Let $(V_j)_{j\ge1}$ be independent uniform random variables on $[0,1]$, let
$U_j=2V_j-1$ be uniform on $[-1,1]$, and define
\begin{equation}
 X=\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}V_j,
 \qquad
 Y=2X-1=\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}U_j.                       \label{p2:eq:XY}
\end{equation}
Then $X\in[0,1]$, $Y\in[-1,1]$, $X\distPartx1-X$, and $Y\distPartx-Y$.  The bounded Fabius
function and Rvachev density are
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\Probability(X\le x),
 \qquad
 \Probability(Y\in\Differential y)=\RvachevUp(y)\Differential y.                       \label{p2:eq:F-up-law}
\end{equation}
The normalizations used below are
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(y)=
 \begin{cases}
 F(y+1),&-1\le y\le0,\\
 F(1-y)=1-F(y),&0\le y\le1,\\
 0,&|y|>1,
 \end{cases}                                           \label{p2:eq:F-up-link}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)\qquad(0<x<1).                         \label{p2:eq:Fprime-up}
\end{equation}
Thus $\RvachevUp$ is even, nonnegative, $C^\infty$, supported on $[-1,1]$, and has integral one.
The density of $X$ is $f_X(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)$ on $[0,1]$.

\subsection{Fixed-point integral equations}

The random affine fixed point
\begin{equation}
 Y\distPartx\frac{U+Y'}2,                                    \label{p2:eq:random-fixed}
\end{equation}
where $U$ is uniform on $[-1,1]$ and $Y'$ is an independent copy of $Y$, gives the exact
refinement equation
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \RvachevUp(x)=\int_{2x-1}^{2x+1}\RvachevUp(t)\Differential t.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:up-integral-refinement}
\end{equation}
Differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp'(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x+1)-2\RvachevUp(2x-1).                         \label{p2:eq:up-differential-refinement}
\end{equation}
The bounded Fabius function is the fixed point of
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\int_0^{2x}F(t)\Differential t,&0\le x\le\tfrac12,\\[1ex]
 \displaystyle1-\int_0^{2-2x}F(t)\Differential t,&\tfrac12\le x\le1,
 \end{cases}                                            \label{p2:eq:F-fixed}
\end{equation}
with constant continuation outside $[0,1]$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 2F(2x),&0<x<\tfrac12,\\
 2F(2-2x),&\tfrac12<x<1.
 \end{cases}                                            \label{p2:eq:F-diffeq-pieces}
\end{equation}
These integral equations are often the most efficient starting point for exact quadrature,
monotonicity, and contraction arguments.

\subsection{The signed global extension and derivative tower}

The repository's signed global extension $\FabiusGlobal$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \FabiusGlobal'(x)=2\FabiusGlobal(2x),                                   \label{p2:eq:global-diffeq}
\end{equation}
so iteration gives the all-order identity
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \FabiusGlobal^{(m)}(x)=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}\FabiusGlobal(2^m x).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:global-derivative-tower}
\end{equation}
After the appropriate unit translation, this yields
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp^{(m)}(x)=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}\FabiusGlobal(2^m(x+1)).         \label{p2:eq:up-derivative-tower}
\end{equation}
Thus every derivative representation of $\FabiusGlobal$ immediately becomes one for $\RvachevUp$, and
vice versa.  It is important not to apply \eqref{p2:eq:global-diffeq} directly to the bounded
CDF outside its valid normalization.

\section{Infinite convolutions, finite splines, and head--tail representations}
\label{p2:sec:convolution}

\subsection{Box convolution}

The density of $2^{-j}U_j$ is $2^{j-1}\IndicatorOf{[-2^{-j},2^{-j}]}$.  Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \RvachevUp=\mathop{\ast}_{j=1}^{\infty}
 \left(2^{j-1}\IndicatorOf{[-2^{-j},2^{-j}]}\right).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:infinite-box-convolution}
\end{equation}
This identity may be read in probability, in $L^1$, or through uniform convergence of
successive compact splines.  It directly explains positivity, compact support, smoothness,
and the sinc product of \cref{p2:sec:fourier-atlas}.

Let
\begin{equation}
 Y_N=\sum_{j=1}^N2^{-j}U_j,
 \qquad
 u_N=\mathop{\ast}_{j=1}^{N}
 \left(2^{j-1}\IndicatorOf{[-2^{-j},2^{-j}]}\right).        \label{p2:eq:finite-box-spline}
\end{equation}
Then $u_N$ is a piecewise polynomial of degree $N-1$ and
\begin{equation}
 Y=Y_N+2^{-N}Y',
 \qquad
 \RvachevUp=u_N\ast\left(2^N\RvachevUp(2^N\,\cdot)\right).           \label{p2:eq:head-tail-up}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\Expectation\left[2^N\RvachevUp\bigl(2^N(x-Y_N)\bigr)\right].   \label{p2:eq:head-tail-up-expectation}
\end{equation}
For the CDF,
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\Expectation\left[
 F\left(2^Nx-\sum_{j=1}^N2^{N-j}V_j\right)
 \right],                                                \label{p2:eq:head-tail-F}
\end{equation}
where the inner $F$ is the bounded, clamped CDF.  Formula \eqref{p2:eq:head-tail-F} is an exact
$N$-dimensional cube integral and a useful recursive numerical scheme.

\subsection{General truncated-power spline}

For positive widths $a_1,\dots,a_N$, the density of
$\sum_{j=1}^Na_jU_j$ is
\begin{equation}
 b_N(x)=\frac{1}{(N-1)!\,2^N\prod_{j=1}^Na_j}
 \sum_{\varepsilon\in\{\pm1\}^N}
 \left(\prod_{j=1}^N\varepsilon_j\right)
 \PositivePart{x+\sum_{j=1}^N\varepsilon_ja_j}^{N-1}.        \label{p2:eq:general-spline}
\end{equation}
Its CDF is obtained by replacing $(N-1)!$ and the exponent $N-1$ by $N!$ and $N$.
For $a_j=2^{-j}$, binary indexing turns \eqref{p2:eq:general-spline} into the exact
Thue--Morse spline
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 u_N(x)=\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \sum_{k=0}^{2^N-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(k)}
 \PositivePart{x+\frac{2^N-1-2k}{2^N}}^{N-1}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:TM-spline}
\end{equation}
The corresponding finite CDF is
\begin{equation}
 U_N(x)=\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{N!}
 \sum_{k=0}^{2^N-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(k)}
 \PositivePart{x+\frac{2^N-1-2k}{2^N}}^{N}.                  \label{p2:eq:TM-spline-cdf}
\end{equation}
Prouhet cancellation makes the apparent polynomial tails cancel exactly.  These formulae
simultaneously represent the finite probability law, a compact spline, and a signed
Thue--Morse power sum.

\subsection{Hypercube and section representations}

For bounded measurable $g$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}g(y)\RvachevUp(y)\Differential y
 =\int_{[-1,1]^{\NaturalNumbers}}g\left(\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}u_j\right)
   \bigotimes_{j\ge1}\frac{\Differential u_j}{2}.                 \label{p2:eq:infinite-cube}
\end{equation}
At finite depth this gives a coarea interpretation of $u_N$ as the weighted $(N-1)$-
dimensional volume of the cube section
\(
 \sum_{j=1}^N2^{-j}u_j=x.
\)
The infinite-dimensional expression is often more useful than a closed formula: it converts
integrals against $\RvachevUp$ into product quadrature and supports concentration and coupling
arguments.

\section{Fourier-product atlas and spectral representations}
\label{p2:sec:fourier-atlas}

\subsection{Conventions and the three canonical products}

We use
\begin{equation}
 \FourierTwoPi{g}(z)=\int_{\RealNumbers}g(x)\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit xz}\Differential x,
 \qquad
 \SincRad w=\frac{\sin w}{w},                               \label{p2:eq:FT-convention}
\end{equation}
with the removable value at zero.  Put
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(z).                                  \label{p2:eq:Phi-def}
\end{equation}
The repository establishes the entire products
\begin{align}
 \Phi(z)
 &=\prod_{h=0}^{\infty}\SincRad\left(\frac{\pi z}{2^h}\right),
                                                               \label{p2:eq:Phi-sinc}\\
 &=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}\left(\cos\frac{\pi z}{2^m}\right)^m,
                                                               \label{p2:eq:Phi-cos}\\
 &=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{z^2}{n^2}\right)^{a_n},
 \qquad a_n=1+\TwoAdicValuation(n).                                  \label{p2:eq:Phi-canonical}
\end{align}
They satisfy the Mahler-type renormalization
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\SincRad(\pi z)\Phi(z/2).                           \label{p2:eq:Phi-scale}
\end{equation}
The zero at every nonzero integer $n$ has exact order $a_{|n|}$.

\subsection{Logarithmic Taylor series and partial fractions}

For $|z|<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \log\Phi(z)=-\sum_{r\ge1}
 \frac{\zeta(2r)}{r(1-4^{-r})}z^{2r}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:logPhi-series}
\end{equation}
Logarithmic differentiation of the canonical product gives, away from the integer zeros,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}
 =-2z\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{a_n}{n^2-z^2}.                   \label{p2:eq:Phi-logderivative}
\end{equation}
The arithmetic spectrum has Dirichlet generating function
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n\ge1}\frac{a_n}{n^s}
 =\frac{\zeta(s)}{1-2^{-s}},\qquad \Re s>1,              \label{p2:eq:a-Dirichlet}
\end{equation}
and exact counting function
\begin{equation}
 A(N)=\sum_{n\le N}a_n
 =\sum_{h\ge0}\Floor{N/2^h}
 =2N-\BinaryDigitSum(N).                                             \label{p2:eq:a-count}
\end{equation}
Thus the zero divisor simultaneously encodes the Riemann zeta function, dyadic valuation,
and binary digit sum.

\subsection{Linear and quadratic heat traces}

Two Lambert/heat kernels occur naturally:
\begin{align}
 L_1(t)&=\sum_{n\ge1}a_n\EulerE^{-nt}
       =\sum_{h\ge0}\frac{1}{\EulerE^{2^ht}-1},               \label{p2:eq:L1}\\
 K(t)&=\sum_{n\ge1}a_n\EulerE^{-n^2t}
       =\sum_{h\ge0}\sum_{m\ge1}\EulerE^{-4^hm^2t}.          \label{p2:eq:Kheat}
\end{align}
Their Mellin transforms are
\begin{align}
 \int_0^\infty L_1(t)t^{s-1}\Differential t
 &=\Gamma(s)\frac{\zeta(s)}{1-2^{-s}},&&\Re s>1,
                                                               \label{p2:eq:L1-Mellin}\\
 \int_0^\infty K(t)t^{s-1}\Differential t
 &=\Gamma(s)\frac{\zeta(2s)}{1-4^{-s}},&&\Re s>\tfrac12.
                                                               \label{p2:eq:K-Mellin}
\end{align}
The consolidated frontier work develops theta completion and complex-dimension oscillations
for $K$.  The linear kernel $L_1$ will reappear in the dyadic-gamma integral and in the Levy
density of the Gaussian-mixture dual.

\subsection{Frullani--Lambert integral for the Fourier product}

Summing the elementary Frullani identity over the zero divisor gives the mixed
integral/product representation
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \log\Phi(z)
 =-2\int_0^\infty\bigl(\cosh(zt)-1\bigr)L_1(t)\frac{\Differential t}{t},
 \qquad |\Re z|<1.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Frullani-Phi}
\end{equation}
In particular, for real $y$,
\begin{equation}
 \log\Phi(\ImaginaryUnit y)
 =2\int_0^\infty\bigl(1-\cos(yt)\bigr)L_1(t)\frac{\Differential t}{t}.  \label{p2:eq:Phi-imag-integral}
\end{equation}
This writes the Wick-rotated product as the exponential of a positive Lambert-kernel energy.
It is also the Levy--Khintchine exponent that underlies \cref{p2:sec:gaussian-mixture}.

\subsection{Fredholm determinant and spectral zeta}

Let $D$ be the positive diagonal operator whose eigenvalue $n$ occurs with multiplicity
$a_n$.  Since $D^{-2}$ is trace class,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \Phi(z)=\det(I-z^2D^{-2}).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Fredholm}
\end{equation}
The associated spectral zeta function is
\begin{equation}
 \zeta_D(s)=\TraceOperator(D^{-s})
 =\frac{\zeta(s)}{1-2^{-s}},\qquad \Re s>1.              \label{p2:eq:spectral-zeta}
\end{equation}
The determinant, zero multiplicities, heat trace, and arithmetic Dirichlet series are thus
four views of the same spectrum.

\section{Fourier inversion, sampling, and interval series}
\label{p2:sec:fourier-inversion}

\subsection{Whole-line inversion}

Because $\RvachevUp\in C_c^\infty(\RealNumbers)$, its transform is rapidly decreasing on the real axis and
\begin{align}
 \RvachevUp(x)
 &=\int_{\RealNumbers}\Phi(\xi)\EulerE^{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit x\xi}\Differential\xi,           \label{p2:eq:up-inversion}\\
 &=2\int_0^\infty\Phi(\xi)\cos(2\pi x\xi)\Differential\xi.       \label{p2:eq:up-cos-integral}
\end{align}
Every derivative may be obtained under the integral:
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp^{(m)}(x)=\int_{\RealNumbers}(2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi)^m
 \Phi(\xi)\EulerE^{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit x\xi}\Differential\xi.                   \label{p2:eq:up-derivative-Fourier}
\end{equation}
Combining this with \eqref{p2:eq:Phi-sinc}, \eqref{p2:eq:Phi-canonical}, or the dyadic-gamma
factorization below gives distinct integral--product representations of the same bump.

\subsection{Poisson partition and half-integer spectrum}

The integer zeros imply the partition of unity
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\RvachevUp(x-k)=1.                                \label{p2:eq:partition-unity}
\end{equation}
On $[-1,1]$, the half-integer samples give the rapidly convergent cosine expansion
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \RvachevUp(t)=\frac12+\sum_{k\ge0}
 \Phi\left(k+\frac12\right)
 \cos\bigl((2k+1)\pi t\bigr).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:up-half-cosine}
\end{equation}
The active half-integer spectral report develops weighted Prouhet identities, exact alias
transforms, and spectral quadrature from this series.

\subsection{Paley--Wiener sampling of the Fourier image}

Since $\RvachevUp$ is supported on $[-1,1]$, $\Phi$ belongs to the Paley--Wiener space of bandwidth
one.  Shannon sampling at spacing $1/2$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\Phi(k/2)
 \SincRad\bigl(\pi(2z-k)\bigr).                             \label{p2:eq:Phi-Shannon}
\end{equation}
The rapid decay of the samples makes the series locally uniformly and absolutely
convergent.  Since $\Phi(m)=0$ for every nonzero integer $m$,
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\SincRad(2\pi z)+
 \sum_{\substack{k\in\IntegerNumbers\\k\ \mathrm{odd}}}
 \Phi(k/2)\SincRad\bigl(\pi(2z-k)\bigr).                   \label{p2:eq:Phi-Shannon-odd}
\end{equation}
Thus the entire transform is reconstructed from its half-integer arithmetic ray.

\subsection{Fourier--Legendre representation}

The compact interval also supports the uniformly convergent Legendre expansion
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{2m+1}{2}
 \left(\int_{-1}^1\RvachevUp(t)P_m(t)\Differential t\right)P_m(x).        \label{p2:eq:Legendre}
\end{equation}
Evenness removes the odd indices.  The coefficient can be expressed either as a moment
combination or by inserting \eqref{p2:eq:up-cos-integral} and using the Fourier--Bessel transform
of $P_m$.  This is a useful bridge between exact moments and orthogonal approximation.

\section{Laplace, Mellin, moment, and \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-series representations}
\label{p2:sec:laplace-moments}

\subsection{Moment-generating and Laplace products}

The random series gives the entire moment-generating functions
\begin{align}
 M_Y(t)=\Expectation\EulerE^{tY}
 &=\Phi\left(\frac{\ImaginaryUnit t}{2\pi}\right)
 =\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{\sinh(t/2^j)}{t/2^j},               \label{p2:eq:MY}\\
 M_X(t)=\Expectation\EulerE^{tX}
 &=\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{\EulerE^{t/2^j}-1}{t/2^j},             \label{p2:eq:MX}\\
 L_X(s)=\Expectation\EulerE^{-sX}
 &=\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{1-\EulerE^{-s/2^j}}{s/2^j}.           \label{p2:eq:LX}
\end{align}
For $c>0$, the CDF has the Bromwich representation
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit}\int_{c-\ImaginaryUnit\infty}^{c+\ImaginaryUnit\infty}
 \frac{L_X(s)}{s}\EulerE^{sx}\Differential s.                          \label{p2:eq:F-Bromwich}
\end{equation}
This contour formula is the natural entrance to the lower-Lambert saddle theory.

\subsection{Mellin moments, including negative orders}

For $\Re\alpha>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation X^{-\alpha}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)}
 \int_0^\infty s^{\alpha-1}L_X(s)\Differential s.                 \label{p2:eq:negative-moment-Mellin}
\end{equation}
The endpoint flatness makes every negative moment finite.  For $0<\Re\alpha<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation X^{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}
 \int_0^\infty\bigl(1-L_X(s)\bigr)s^{-\alpha-1}\Differential s. \label{p2:eq:positive-fractional-moment}
\end{equation}
For integer $m\ge1$, integration by parts gives the survival representation
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation X^m=m\int_0^1x^{m-1}\bigl(1-F(x)\bigr)\Differential x
 =m\int_0^1x^{m-1}\RvachevUp(x)\Differential x.                          \label{p2:eq:moments-survival}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Cumulants, Bernoulli numbers, and Bell polynomials}

From \eqref{p2:eq:logPhi-series}, the nonzero cumulants of $Y$ are
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \kappa_{2r}(Y)
 =\frac{B_{2r}}{2r(1-4^{-r})}
 =(-1)^{r+1}\frac{(2r)!\zeta(2r)}
 {r(1-4^{-r})(2\pi)^{2r}},
 \quad \kappa_{2r+1}(Y)=0.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Y-cumulants}
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation Y^{2n}=B_{2n}\bigl(0,\kappa_2,0,\kappa_4,\dots,0,\kappa_{2n}\bigr), \label{p2:eq:Bell-moments}
\end{equation}
where the $B_{2n}$ on the right is the complete exponential Bell polynomial, not a Bernoulli
number.  The first values are
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation Y^2=\frac19,\qquad
 \Expectation Y^4=\frac{19}{675},\qquad
 \Expectation Y^6=\frac{583}{59535}.                               \label{p2:eq:first-moments}
\end{equation}
Moments of $X=(1+Y)/2$ follow by binomial expansion.

\subsection{Wick rotation, Newton recurrence, and \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-MZV formula}

Define
\begin{equation}
 \Acal(w)=\Phi(\ImaginaryUnit\sqrt w)
 =\prod_{n\ge1}\left(1+\frac{w}{n^2}\right)^{a_n}
 =\sum_{r\ge0}C_rw^r.                                    \label{p2:eq:A-def}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 C_r=\frac{(2\pi)^{2r}}{(2r)!}\Expectation Y^{2r}.                 \label{p2:eq:C-moment}
\end{equation}
Writing
\begin{equation}
 p_r=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{a_n}{n^{2r}}
 =\frac{\zeta(2r)}{1-4^{-r}},                            \label{p2:eq:pr}
\end{equation}
Newton's recurrence is
\begin{equation}
 rC_r=\sum_{j=1}^r(-1)^{j+1}p_jC_{r-j},\qquad C_0=1.    \label{p2:eq:Newton-C}
\end{equation}
At $q=1/4$, the active frontier report further expands $C_r$ by partitions.  If
$\lambda=(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_\ell)\vdash r$, $m_j(\lambda)$ is the multiplicity of
part $j$, and
\begin{equation}
 Z_\lambda=\sum_{\substack{n_1,\dots,n_\ell\ge1\\n_i\ne n_j}}
 \frac{1}{n_1^{2\lambda_1}\cdots n_\ell^{2\lambda_\ell}},                \label{p2:eq:Zlambda}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 C_r=\sum_{\lambda\vdash r}
 \frac{Z_\lambda}{\prod_{j\ge1}m_j(\lambda)!}
 \prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\lambda)}
 \frac{q^{\lambda_i(\lambda_i-1)/2}}{(q;q)_{\lambda_i}},
 \qquad q=\frac14.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:q-MZV}
\end{equation}
Moreover $Z_\lambda$ is a symmetrized multiple-zeta value.  This formula keeps both the
integer spectrum and its dyadic layer occupancy visible.

\subsection{Appell and logistic representations already in the corpus}

The Appell sequence associated with the compact Rvachev law is defined by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\EulerE^{xt}}{M_Y(t)}=\sum_{n\ge0}A_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}. \label{p2:eq:Appell}
\end{equation}
The audited Barnes/inverse draft introduces independent standard logistic variables
$\Lambda_j$ and
\begin{equation}
 Z=\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{\Lambda_j}{\pi2^j},                 \label{p2:eq:logistic-Z}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tZ}=\frac{1}{M_Y(t)},
 \qquad
 A_n(x)=\Expectation(x+\ImaginaryUnit Z)^n.                                   \label{p2:eq:logistic-dual-baseline}
\end{equation}
This logistic dual is baseline.  The new result in \cref{p2:sec:gaussian-mixture} is its exact
identification as a Gaussian variance mixture driven by the independently developed Thorin
variable.

\section{Thue--Morse, dyadic arithmetic, and finite product-to-sum bridges}
\label{p2:sec:thue-morse}

Let $\varepsilon_k=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(k)}$.  The finite Prouhet product is
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{j=0}^{N-1}(1-z^{2^j})
 =\sum_{k=0}^{2^N-1}\varepsilon_kz^k.                    \label{p2:eq:Prouhet-product}
\end{equation}
With $z=\EulerE^t$ this becomes
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k<2^N}\varepsilon_k\EulerE^{kt}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}(1-\EulerE^{2^jt}).                         \label{p2:eq:Prouhet-exponential}
\end{equation}
Coefficient comparison yields
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k<2^N}\varepsilon_kk^m=0\quad(0\le m<N),
 \qquad
 \sum_{k<2^N}\varepsilon_kk^N
 =(-1)^NN!2^{N(N-1)/2}.                                  \label{p2:eq:Prouhet-sharp}
\end{equation}
These cancellations explain the compactness of the truncated-power spline
\eqref{p2:eq:TM-spline}; they also power the exact dyadic-value formulae, Gaussian-binomial
interpolation, and Richardson extrapolants in the repository.

The finite Fourier product
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_N(z)=\prod_{h=0}^{N-1}\SincRad\left(\frac{\pi z}{2^h}\right)           \label{p2:eq:PhiN}
\end{equation}
can be converted into a signed exponential sum by expanding the sine factors.  Conversely,
Prouhet cancellations force high-order zeros in those finite sums.  This is the basic
product-to-sum bridge connecting Fourier analysis to exact binary arithmetic.

\section{Endpoint Laplace phases, Lambert \texorpdfstring{$W$}{W}, and inverse asymptotics}
\label{p2:sec:endpoint}

The active frontier synthesis writes
\begin{equation}
 g(s)=\log L_X(s)
 =-\frac{(\log s)^2}{2\log2}+\frac12\log s
   +R(\log_2s)+E(s),                                     \label{p2:eq:g-decomp}
\end{equation}
where $R$ is real-analytic and one-periodic and
\begin{equation}
 0\le E(s)\le\frac{\EulerE^{-s}}{(1-\EulerE^{-s})^2}.              \label{p2:eq:E-bound}
\end{equation}
The CDF integral \eqref{p2:eq:F-Bromwich} has saddle equation
\begin{equation}
 x=-g'(s),                                                \label{p2:eq:saddle-eq}
\end{equation}
whose exact phase is solved by a lower branch of Lambert $W$, perturbed by the periodic
function and its derivatives.  The periodic term is essential: every nonzero Fourier mode is
explicitly nonzero in the formal exposition, so replacing it by a constant loses the claimed
sharp error scale.

The inverse $Q=F^{-1}$ consequently lives on the stretched-logarithmic scale
\begin{equation}
 Q(y)\asymp
 \sqrt{\log(1/y)}
 \exp\left[-\sqrt{2\log2\,\log(1/y)}\right]
 \qquad(y\downarrow0),                                  \label{p2:eq:Q-scale}
\end{equation}
with a sharper periodic Lambert expansion in the audited inverse reports.  Reflection gives
$Q(1-y)=1-Q(y)$.  These asymptotic representations complement, rather than replace, the exact
Fourier and quantile identities developed below.

\clearpage
\part*{New theorem packages}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{New theorem packages}

\section{The dyadic gamma factorization}
\label{p2:sec:dyadic-gamma}

\subsection{Definition, convergence, and poles}

\begin{definition}[Dyadic gamma function \New]
For $z\in\ComplexNumbers$, away from the negative integers, define
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \GammaDy(z)=\prod_{h=0}^{\infty}\Gamma(1+2^{-h}z).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:GammaDy-def}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Meromorphy and Mahler equation \New]
The product \eqref{p2:eq:GammaDy-def} converges locally uniformly away from its poles and defines
a meromorphic function satisfying
\begin{equation}
 \GammaDy(z)=\Gamma(1+z)\GammaDy(z/2),
 \qquad \GammaDy(0)=1.                                  \label{p2:eq:GammaDy-Mahler}
\end{equation}
Its pole at $-n$, $n\ge1$, has exact order $a_n=1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$ and it has no other poles.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Uniformly on compact sets, $\log\Gamma(1+w)=-\gamma w+\BigO(w^2)$ as $w\to0$.
The sums $\sum_h2^{-h}$ and $\sum_h4^{-h}$ converge, so the logarithms form a normally
convergent series after finitely many factors are removed.  Separating the factor $h=0$
gives \eqref{p2:eq:GammaDy-Mahler}.  At $z=-n$, the factor indexed by $h$ has a simple gamma
pole precisely when $n/2^h$ is a positive integer, namely for $0\le h\le\TwoAdicValuation(n)$.  Their
number is $1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$.
\end{proof}

\emph{Formal reciprocal version.}  In \path{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean}, define
\(
G_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)=\text{\texttt{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma}}(z)
=\prod_{h\ge0}\Gamma(1+2^{-h}z)^{-1}
\)
and define the Gamma-side representative
\(\text{\texttt{Fabius.dyadicGamma}}=G_{\mathrm{dy}}^{-1}\).
The reciprocal product is entire and normalized by
\leanPartx{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma_differentiable} and
\leanPartx{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma_zero}.  Its Gamma-side inverse is meromorphic by
\leanPartx{Fabius.dyadicGamma_meromorphic}, and the Mahler equation is the specialization of
\leanPartx{Fabius.geometricGamma_mahler} at \(q=1/2\).  In
\path{DyadicGammaOrder.lean},
\leanPartx{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma_eq_zero_iff} gives exactly the negative-integer zero
set; \leanPartx{Fabius.analyticOrderAt_dyadicReciprocalGamma_neg_nat} gives zero order
\(1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)\), and \leanPartx{Fabius.meromorphicOrderAt_dyadicGamma_neg_nat} records the
corresponding pole as meromorphic order \(-(1+\TwoAdicValuation(n))\).  Mathlib assigns
\leanPartx{Complex.Gamma} value zero at a classical pole and also totalizes \(0^{-1}=0\), so
the pole is encoded by this negative local order rather than an infinite point value.
Neither module proves that the raw product in \eqref{p2:eq:GammaDy-def} converges locally
uniformly off its poles or equals this meromorphic inverse there; that representation remains
frontier material.

\subsection{Reflection factorization of the Rvachev transform}

\begin{theorem}[Dyadic gamma factorization \New]
For every $z\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \Phi(z)=\frac{1}{\GammaDy(z)\GammaDy(-z)}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Phi-GammaDy}
\end{equation}
The equality is entire: at integer $z$, the poles of the gamma factors produce exactly the
zeros of $\Phi$ with their arithmetic multiplicities.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Euler's reflection formula in the shifted form, understood meromorphically and with the
removable value supplied at \(w=0\),
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma(1+w)\Gamma(1-w)=\frac{\pi w}{\sin(\pi w)}
 =\frac{1}{\SincRad(\pi w)}                                  \label{p2:eq:gamma-reflection}
\end{equation}
gives
\[
 \frac{1}{\Gamma(1+2^{-h}z)\Gamma(1-2^{-h}z)}
 =\SincRad(\pi z/2^h).
\]
Multiplication over $h\ge0$ yields the sinc product \eqref{p2:eq:Phi-sinc}.  Normal convergence
away from the poles and removable continuation at the integers complete the proof.
\end{proof}

\emph{Formal version.}  The pointwise total identity actually used by Lean is
\[
 \Gamma(1+w)^{-1}\Gamma(1-w)^{-1}=\SincRad(\pi w),
\]
namely \leanPartx{Fabius.shiftedReciprocalGamma_mul_neg}; unlike the middle quotient in
\eqref{p2:eq:gamma-reflection}, it includes \(w=0\) without a removable-value convention.
The entire dyadic identities \eqref{p2:eq:Phi-GammaDy} and
\eqref{p2:eq:Phi-Gdy} have formal analogues after replacing the raw
\(\Gamma_{\mathrm{dy}},G_{\mathrm{dy}}\) pair by Lean's totalized
\(\text{\texttt{Fabius.dyadicGamma}},
\text{\texttt{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma}}\) pair.  Those analogues are respectively
\leanPartx{Fabius.rvachevFourierProduct_eq_one_div_dyadicGamma_mul} and
\leanPartx{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma_mul_neg}; neither theorem identifies the raw
Gamma product with the totalized inverse away from its poles.

\begin{corollary}[Gamma integral representations of $\RvachevUp$ and $F$ \New]
For real $x$,
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\int_{\RealNumbers}
 \frac{\EulerE^{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit x\xi}}
 {\GammaDy(\xi)\GammaDy(-\xi)}\Differential\xi,                    \label{p2:eq:up-GammaDy-integral}
\end{equation}
and for real $x$,
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\frac12+\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty
 \frac{\sin\bigl(2\pi\xi(2x-1)\bigr)}
 {\xi\GammaDy(\xi)\GammaDy(-\xi)}\Differential\xi.              \label{p2:eq:F-GammaDy-integral}
\end{equation}
Both integrals are absolutely convergent after the usual removable interpretation at
$\xi=0$.
\end{corollary}

\emph{Frontier boundary.}  The reciprocal-Gamma factorization does not by itself provide an
exact public Lean declaration for either integral above.  Their convergence and their
Gamma-form integrands therefore remain unformalized.

\subsection{Canonical reciprocal product and power series}

Let $G_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)=1/\GammaDy(z)$.  Euler's canonical product for $1/\Gamma$ and
regrouping equal zeros give
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 G_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)
 =\EulerE^{2\gamma z}
 \prod_{n\ge1}\left[\left(1+\frac{z}{n}\right)
 \EulerE^{-z/n}\right]^{a_n}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Gdy-canonical}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=G_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)G_{\mathrm{dy}}(-z),          \label{p2:eq:Phi-Gdy}
\end{equation}
which splits the even zero divisor into a negative-integer factor and its reflection.
Equation \eqref{p2:eq:Phi-Gdy} has the formal analogue
\leanPartx{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma_mul_neg} after replacing raw
\(G_{\mathrm{dy}}\) by \texttt{Fabius.dyadicReciprocalGamma}; the arithmetic
Weierstrass regrouping \eqref{p2:eq:Gdy-canonical} is not formalized.
For $|z|<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \log\GammaDy(z)
 =-2\gamma z+
 \sum_{m\ge2}\frac{(-1)^m\zeta(m)}{m(1-2^{-m})}z^m.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:logGammaDy-series}
\end{equation}
The even part of \eqref{p2:eq:logGammaDy-series} is exactly
$-\tfrac12\log\Phi(z)$, while the odd part is new information lost in the even Fourier
product.

\emph{Frontier boundary.}  No public declaration at checkpoint
\path{71ab6f6728fceb753c88d8b0573077a59acf2682} proves
\eqref{p2:eq:Gdy-canonical} or \eqref{p2:eq:logGammaDy-series}.

\subsection{Dyadic digamma and Stieltjes resolvent}

Define
\begin{equation}
 \Psi_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)=\frac{\GammaDy'(z)}{\GammaDy(z)}.
\end{equation}
Termwise differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 \Psi_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)=\sum_{h\ge0}2^{-h}\psi(1+2^{-h}z),                 \label{p2:eq:dyadic-digamma}
\end{equation}
and the Fourier logarithmic derivative becomes
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}
 =\Psi_{\mathrm{dy}}(-z)-\Psi_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)
 =-2z\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{a_n}{n^2-z^2}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:digamma-resolvent}
\end{equation}
Thus the arithmetic Stieltjes resolvent is the odd part of a dyadic digamma function.
Differentiating repeatedly produces polygamma representations for every logarithmic jet of
$\Phi$.

\emph{Frontier boundary.}  The dyadic digamma, resolvent, and polygamma identities in this
subsection are not consequences exposed by the reciprocal-product API and remain
unformalized.

\subsection{Malmsten and heat-trace integrals}

For $\Re z>-1$, summing Malmsten's integral for $\log\Gamma(1+z/2^h)$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \log\GammaDy(z)=\int_0^\infty
 \left[
 2z\EulerE^{-t}-\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{h\ge0}
 (1-\EulerE^{-2^{-h}zt})}{\EulerE^t-1}
 \right]\frac{\Differential t}{t}.                               \label{p2:eq:GammaDy-Malmsten}
\end{equation}
The inner dyadic exponential sum has the entire expansion
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h\ge0}(1-\EulerE^{-2^{-h}w})
 =\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{m+1}w^m}{m!(1-2^{-m})}.       \label{p2:eq:dyadic-exponential-sum}
\end{equation}
A second integral makes the integer spectrum explicit.  From
\eqref{p2:eq:Gdy-canonical} and \eqref{p2:eq:L1},
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \log\GammaDy(z)
 =-2\gamma z+\int_0^\infty
 \bigl(zt-(1-\EulerE^{-zt})\bigr)L_1(t)\frac{\Differential t}{t},
 \qquad \Re z>-1.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:GammaDy-heat-integral}
\end{equation}
This identity is a direct analytic bridge between the gamma factor, Lambert series, and the
zero-divisor heat trace.

\emph{Frontier boundary.}  The Malmsten integral, dyadic exponential series, and heat-trace
integral in this subsection have no exact public Lean counterparts.

\subsection{Geometric-\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q} extension}

\begin{theorem}[Geometric gamma factorization \New]
Let $0<q<1$ and define
\begin{equation}
 \GammaGeom(z)=\prod_{h\ge0}\Gamma(1+q^hz),
 \qquad
 \Phi_q(z)=\prod_{h\ge0}\SincRad(\pi q^hz).                \label{p2:eq:q-geometric-gamma}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 \GammaGeom(z)&=\Gamma(1+z)\GammaGeom(qz),               \label{p2:eq:q-gamma-Mahler}\\
 \Phi_q(z)&=\frac{1}{\GammaGeom(z)\GammaGeom(-z)},       \label{p2:eq:q-gamma-reflection}\\
 \log\GammaGeom(z)
 &=-\frac{\gamma z}{1-q}
 +\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{(-1)^m\zeta(m)}{m(1-q^m)}z^m,
 \qquad |z|<1.                                           \label{p2:eq:q-gamma-series}
\end{align}
For $q=1/b$ with integer $b\ge2$, the pole at $-n$ has order
$1+\max\{h:b^h\mid n\}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The convergence argument is identical to the dyadic case, using
$\sum q^h$ and $\sum q^{2h}$.  Reflection is applied factor by factor, and the power series
follows by summing $\sum_hq^{hm}=(1-q^m)^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
$\GammaGeom$ is not the classical Jackson $q$-gamma function.  It is a geometric Mahler
product of ordinary gamma functions.  Its denominators $1-q^m$ nevertheless align exactly
with the $q$-series denominators already pervasive in the Fabius--Rvachev theory.
\end{remark}

\emph{Formal reciprocal version and boundary.}  The formal theorem is stronger in its base
hypothesis and narrower in analytic content: for every \(q\in\ComplexNumbers\) with
\(\lVert q\rVert<1\), including \(q=0\),
\leanPartx{Fabius.geometricReciprocalGamma_differentiable} proves that
\(G_q(z)=\prod_h\Gamma(1+q^hz)^{-1}\) is entire,
\leanPartx{Fabius.geometricReciprocalGamma_mahler} gives its reciprocal Mahler equation,
\leanPartx{Fabius.geometricReciprocalGamma_eq_zero_iff} gives the exact affine zero orbit,
\leanPartx{Fabius.geometricGamma_meromorphic} and
\leanPartx{Fabius.geometricGamma_mahler} give the meromorphic inverse and Gamma-side Mahler
equation, and \leanPartx{Fabius.geometricReciprocalGamma_mul_neg} gives the corresponding
entire reciprocal analogue of \eqref{p2:eq:q-gamma-reflection}.  The raw Gamma product in
\eqref{p2:eq:q-geometric-gamma} and the logarithmic series \eqref{p2:eq:q-gamma-series} are not
formalized.

The general integer-base statement is now half formalized, and the halves are worth separating.
Its \emph{zero set} is, at every integer base: for $b\ge2$,
\[
 G_{1/b}(z)=0
 \iff
 z\in\{-1,-2,-3,\dots\},
\]
independently of $b$
(\leanPartx{Fabius.geometricReciprocalGamma_inv_natCast_eq_zero_iff},
\path{ReciprocalIntegerGammaZeros.lean}).  \path{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean} states the zeros
in the \emph{affine} form $\exists n,m,\ 1+q^nz=-m$, which is the shape that stays safe at $q=0$
where no scale can be divided out; at reciprocal-integer base it collapses to the arithmetic one,
since $(m+1)b^n$ runs over the positive integers --- surjectively already at $n=0$.

The \emph{order} is not, and is not a corollary of the set.  At $b=2$ it is obtained by
transferring the integer-zero order of the Rvachev product through
$G_{1/2}(z)G_{1/2}(-z)=\Phi(z)$, and
the corpus has no $\Phi_b$ to transfer through at another base; a direct proof would have to
split off the finitely many vanishing factors --- the $h$ with $b^h\mid n$ --- and show the
remainder is an analytic unit.  Only the dyadic order has an exact counterpart, in
\path{DyadicGammaOrder.lean}.  The arithmetic side of the count is available at arbitrary base as
\leanPartx{Fabius.weightedScaleMultiplicity}, so what is missing is the analytic splitting, not
the combinatorics.

\section{Gaussian variance mixtures and the logistic dual}
\label{p2:sec:gaussian-mixture}

\subsection{The existing Thorin variable}

The audited frontier corpus already constructs, for $\tau>0$, a nonnegative random variable
$V_\tau$ with
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation\EulerE^{-wV_\tau}=\Acal(w)^{-\tau},                        \label{p2:eq:V-LT}
\end{equation}
and grouped gamma representation
\begin{equation}
 V_\tau\distPartx\sum_{n\ge1}H_{n,\tau},
 \qquad
 H_{n,\tau}\sim\operatorname{Gamma}(\tau a_n,
                 \text{rate }n^2),                       \label{p2:eq:V-gamma}
\end{equation}
with independent summands.  Its discrete Thorin measure is
\begin{equation}
 U_\tau=\tau\sum_{n\ge1}a_n\delta_{n^2}.                 \label{p2:eq:Thorin}
\end{equation}
The construction and its complete-Bernstein consequences are \Repo.  We now connect it to
the independently developed logistic dual.

\subsection{Equality in law}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian variance-mixture bridge \New]
Let
\begin{equation}
 T_\tau=\frac{V_\tau}{4\pi^2},                            \label{p2:eq:Ttau}
\end{equation}
and let $N\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ be independent of $T_\tau$.  Define
\begin{equation}
 Z_\tau=\sqrt{2T_\tau}\,N
 =\frac{\sqrt{V_\tau}}{\sqrt2\,\pi}N.                   \label{p2:eq:Ztau-mixture}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 \Expectation\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tZ_\tau}
 &=\Acal\left(\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2}\right)^{-\tau},        \label{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-A}\\
 &=\prod_{n\ge1}\left(1+\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2n^2}\right)^{-\tau a_n},
                                                               \label{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-canonical}\\
 &=\left[\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{t/2^j}{\sinh(t/2^j)}\right]^\tau,
                                                               \label{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-logistic}\\
 &=\left[\GammaDy\left(\frac{\ImaginaryUnit t}{2\pi}\right)
          \GammaDy\left(-\frac{\ImaginaryUnit t}{2\pi}\right)\right]^\tau.
                                                               \label{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-gamma}
\end{align}
For $\tau=1$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \sum_{j\ge1}\frac{\Lambda_j}{\pi2^j}
 \distPartx
 \frac{\sqrt{V_1}}{\sqrt2\,\pi}N,
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:logistic-mixture-law}
\end{equation}
where the $\Lambda_j$ are independent standard logistic variables.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Conditioning on $T_\tau$ gives
\[
 \Expectation(\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t\sqrt{2T_\tau}N}\mid T_\tau)=\EulerE^{-t^2T_\tau}.
\]
Taking expectation and applying \eqref{p2:eq:V-LT} at
$w=t^2/(4\pi^2)$ proves \eqref{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-A};
\eqref{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-canonical} follows from \eqref{p2:eq:A-def}.  Since
$\Acal(t^2/(4\pi^2))=M_Y(t)$, the reciprocal hyperbolic-sine product
\eqref{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-logistic} follows from \eqref{p2:eq:MY}.  Finally,
\eqref{p2:eq:Phi-GammaDy} at $z=\ImaginaryUnit t/(2\pi)$ gives
\eqref{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-gamma}.  At $\tau=1$, the right-hand side is exactly the characteristic
function of the repository's logistic series \eqref{p2:eq:logistic-Z}; uniqueness of
characteristic functions proves \eqref{p2:eq:logistic-mixture-law}.
\end{proof}

\emph{Formalization status.}  The reciprocal-Gamma/Rvachev factor used in the last rewrite is
formal, but the Gaussian variance-mixture theorem and its Gamma-form characteristic function
do not have exact public Lean counterparts at the audited checkpoint.

\begin{resultbox}
The bridge is not a restatement of generalized gamma convolution or of the logistic series.
It identifies two previously separate noncompact duals of the compact Rvachev law: one built
additively from logistic coordinates and the other obtained by subordinating a Gaussian by
the arithmetic Thorin variable.
\end{resultbox}

\subsection{Convolution semigroup, density, and moments}

The family $(Z_\tau)_{\tau>0}$ is a symmetric convolution semigroup:
\begin{equation}
 Z_{\tau_1}+Z'_{\tau_2}\distPartx Z_{\tau_1+\tau_2}.           \label{p2:eq:Z-semigroup}
\end{equation}
Its density is the exact normal scale mixture
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 p_\tau(x)=\Expectation\left[
 \frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi T_\tau}}
 \exp\left(-\frac{x^2}{4T_\tau}\right)
 \right].
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Z-density-mixture}
\end{equation}
The cumulants are
\begin{equation}
 \kappa_{2r}(Z_\tau)
 =\tau\frac{(2r)!}{r(4\pi^2)^r}
 \frac{\zeta(2r)}{1-4^{-r}},
 \qquad \kappa_{2r+1}(Z_\tau)=0.                        \label{p2:eq:Z-cumulants}
\end{equation}
In particular, $\Variance Z_\tau=\tau/9$.  Conditional Gaussian moments yield
\begin{equation}
 \Expectation Z_\tau^{2m}=2^m(2m-1)!!\,\Expectation T_\tau^m,               \label{p2:eq:Z-moment-T}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \kappa_r(T_\tau)=
 \tau(r-1)!\frac{\zeta(2r)}{(4\pi^2)^r(1-4^{-r})}.       \label{p2:eq:T-cumulants}
\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{p2:eq:Z-moment-T} and complete Bell polynomials give a second moment calculus for
the logistic/Appell dual.

\subsection{Closed Levy density and self-decomposability}

\begin{theorem}[Levy density of the dual semigroup \New]
The characteristic exponent of $Z_\tau$ is
\begin{equation}
 -\log\Expectation\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tZ_\tau}
 =2\tau\int_0^\infty(1-\cos(tx))L_1(2\pi x)\frac{\Differential x}{x}.  \label{p2:eq:Z-Levy-exponent}
\end{equation}
Consequently its symmetric Levy measure has density
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \nu_\tau(x)=\frac{\tau}{|x|}L_1(2\pi|x|)
 =\frac{\tau}{|x|}\sum_{h\ge0}
   \frac{1}{\EulerE^{2^{h+1}\pi|x|}-1},
 \qquad x\ne0.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Z-Levy-density}
\end{equation}
Every $Z_\tau$ is self-decomposable.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use
\[
 \log\left(1+\frac{t^2}{\lambda^2}\right)
 =2\int_0^\infty(1-\cos(tx))\EulerE^{-\lambda x}\frac{\Differential x}{x}
\]
in \eqref{p2:eq:Ztau-cf-canonical}, then sum over $n$.  This proves
\eqref{p2:eq:Z-Levy-exponent} and \eqref{p2:eq:Z-Levy-density}.  A symmetric infinitely divisible
law with Levy density $k(|x|)/|x|$ is self-decomposable when $k$ is decreasing.  Here
$k(x)=\tau L_1(2\pi x)$ is a positive sum of decreasing exponentials.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Complete monotonicity in the squared coordinate}

\begin{corollary}[Radial positivity in every dimension \New]
For every $\tau>0$, the function
\begin{equation}
 g_\tau(s)=p_\tau(\sqrt s),\qquad s>0,                    \label{p2:eq:g-density}
\end{equation}
is completely monotone:
\begin{equation}
 (-1)^m g_\tau^{(m)}(s)\ge0\qquad(m\ge0,s>0).            \label{p2:eq:CM-density}
\end{equation}
Consequently $x\mapsto p_\tau(\|x\|)$ is positive definite on $\RealNumbers^d$ for every
$d\ge1$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Formula \eqref{p2:eq:Z-density-mixture} writes
\[
 g_\tau(s)=\Expectation\left[(4\pi T_\tau)^{-1/2}
                    \EulerE^{-s/(4T_\tau)}\right],
\]
a positive mixture of exponentials in $s$.  Bernstein's theorem gives complete monotonicity,
and Schoenberg's theorem converts complete monotonicity of $g_\tau$ into radial positive
definiteness of $g_\tau(\|x\|^2)=p_\tau(\|x\|)$ in every dimension.
\end{proof}

\section{Jensen means, factorial products, and binary digit sums}
\label{p2:sec:jensen}

\subsection{Exact circular mean}

Define the Jensen mean
\begin{equation}
 J(r)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
 \log\left|\Phi(r\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})\right|\Differential\theta,
 \qquad r>0,                                             \label{p2:eq:J-def}
\end{equation}
with the improper interpretation when the circle passes through an integer zero.

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic Jensen formula \New]
Let $N=\Floor r$ and $N_j=\Floor{N/2^j}$.  Then
\begin{align}
 J(r)
 &=2\sum_{n\le N}a_n\log\frac{r}{n},                     \label{p2:eq:J-zero-sum}\\
 &=2\sum_{j\ge0}
 \left[N_j\log\frac{r}{2^j}-\log\Gamma(N_j+1)\right].   \label{p2:eq:J-factorial}
\end{align}
The second sum is finite.  Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \exp\left(\frac{J(r)}2\right)
 =\prod_{n\le N}\left(\frac{r}{n}\right)^{a_n}
 =\prod_{j\ge0}\frac{(r/2^j)^{N_j}}{N_j!}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:J-geometric-mean}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Jensen's formula for the entire function $\Phi$, normalized by $\Phi(0)=1$, says that the
mean is the sum of $\log(r/|\rho|)$ over zeros $\rho$ in the disk, counted with multiplicity.
The zeros occur at $\pm n$ with multiplicity $a_n$, giving \eqref{p2:eq:J-zero-sum}.  Expand
$a_n=\sum_{j\ge0}\IndicatorOf{2^j\mid n}$, write $n=2^jm$, and sum
$\log(r/(2^jm))$ for $1\le m\le N_j$.  This proves \eqref{p2:eq:J-factorial} and
\eqref{p2:eq:J-geometric-mean}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Digit-sum derivative}

\begin{corollary}[Binary slope law \New]
For $N<r<N+1$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 J'(r)=\frac{2A(N)}r
 =\frac{2(2N-\BinaryDigitSum(N))}{r}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:J-derivative}
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}
 \frac{rJ'(r)}2=2\Floor r-\BinaryDigitSum(\Floor r)                  \label{p2:eq:J-digit-recovery}
\end{equation}
recovers the binary digit sum directly from a circular mean of the Fourier image.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}
The zero-counting law $A(N)=2N-\BinaryDigitSum(N)$ was already in the repository.  The new point is that
it is the logarithmic radial derivative of a two-dimensional complex-analytic average, and
that the full mean has the factorial product \eqref{p2:eq:J-geometric-mean}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Asymptotic growth}

\begin{theorem}[Leading Jensen asymptotic \New]
As $r\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 J(r)=4r-\frac{(\log r)^2}{2\log2}+\BigO(\log r).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:J-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $N=\Floor r$.  Partial summation applied to \eqref{p2:eq:J-zero-sum} gives
\[
 \frac{J(r)}2=A(N)\log(r/N)
 +\int_1^N\frac{A(\Floor t)}{t}\Differential t.
\]
The first term is $\BigO(1)$.  Since
$A(n)=2n-\BinaryDigitSum(n)$, the linear part contributes $2N+\BigO(\log N)$.  The elementary
summatory digit estimate
\[
 \sum_{n\le x}\BinaryDigitSum(n)=\frac{x\log x}{2\log2}+\BigO(x)
\]
and another partial summation give
\[
 \int_1^N\frac{\BinaryDigitSum(\Floor t)}{t}\Differential t
 =\frac{(\log N)^2}{4\log2}+\BigO(\log N).
\]
Multiply by two and replace $N$ with $r$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Subunit circular energy}

For $0\le r<1$, Jensen's mean vanishes because the disk contains no zeros.  The angular
fluctuation nevertheless has an exact zeta-series energy.  Put
\begin{equation}
 c_m=\frac{\zeta(2m)}{m(1-4^{-m})}.                       \label{p2:eq:cm}
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{p2:eq:logPhi-series} and trigonometric orthogonality give
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
 \left(\log|\Phi(r\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})|\right)^2\Differential\theta
 =\frac12\sum_{m\ge1}c_m^2r^{4m}.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:J-energy}
\end{equation}
More generally, for $0\le r,s<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
 \log|\Phi(r\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})|
 \log|\Phi(s\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})|\Differential\theta
 =\frac12\sum_{m\ge1}c_m^2(rs)^{2m}.                   \label{p2:eq:J-covariance}
\end{equation}
This is a local angular covariance kernel for the Fourier determinant.

\section{Non-holonomicity from the arithmetic zero divisor}
\label{p2:sec:nonDfinite}

\begin{theorem}[The Rvachev transform is not D-finite \New]
The entire function $\Phi$ does not satisfy any nontrivial linear differential equation
\begin{equation}
 p_R(z)y^{(R)}+p_{R-1}(z)y^{(R-1)}+\cdots+p_0(z)y=0       \label{p2:eq:Dfinite-ODE}
\end{equation}
with polynomial coefficients $p_j\in\ComplexNumbers[z]$.  Equivalently, $\Phi$ is not holonomic over
$\ComplexNumbers(z)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose \eqref{p2:eq:Dfinite-ODE} holds with $p_R\ne0$.  At any ordinary point
$z_0$ with $p_R(z_0)\ne0$, uniqueness for the associated analytic initial-value problem
implies that a nonzero solution cannot have a zero of multiplicity $R$ or larger: if the
first $R$ derivatives vanish, the solution is identically zero near $z_0$.

The polynomial $p_R$ has only finitely many zeros.  But $\Phi$ has a zero at $2^k$ of
multiplicity $k+1$.  Choose $k\ge R$ such that $p_R(2^k)\ne0$.  This gives an ordinary zero
of multiplicity at least $R$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Coefficient and algebraic consequences \New]
The Maclaurin coefficient sequence of $\Phi$ is not P-recursive.  In particular, $\Phi$ is
not algebraic over $\ComplexNumbers(z)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
A power series is D-finite if and only if its coefficient sequence is P-recursive.  Every
algebraic power series is D-finite.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The dyadic gamma factors are not D-finite \New]
Neither $\GammaDy$ nor $G_{\mathrm{dy}}=1/\GammaDy$ is D-finite.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A D-finite germ has only finitely many possible finite singularities, whereas $\GammaDy$ has
poles at every negative integer.  The reciprocal $G_{\mathrm{dy}}$ is entire but has a zero
at $-2^k$ of multiplicity $k+1$, so the preceding multiplicity argument applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Non-D-finiteness is much weaker than differential transcendence.  The latter is stated only
as a conjecture in \cref{p2:sec:conjectures}.
\end{remark}

\emph{Formalization status.}  Exact dyadic zero and pole orders are now available in
\path{DyadicGammaOrder.lean}, but the repository still has no D-finite/P-recursive analytic
ODE framework proving this proposition.  It therefore remains a frontier result.

\section{Fourier representations of the bounded Fabius function}
\label{p2:sec:F-Fourier}

\subsection{The density transform}

Let $f_X=F'=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)$ on $[0,1]$ and zero outside.  A change of variables gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \FourierTwoPi{f_X}(\xi)=\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:fX-transform}
\end{equation}
Hence the compactly supported pieces
\begin{equation}
 F_0(x)=F(x)\IndicatorOf{[0,1]}(x),
 \qquad
 S_0(x)=(1-F(x))\IndicatorOf{[0,1]}(x)                      \label{p2:eq:F0S0}
\end{equation}
have, for $\xi\ne0$,
\begin{align}
 \FourierTwoPi{F_0}(\xi)
 &=\frac{\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2)-\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}}
 {2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi},                                         \label{p2:eq:F0-transform}\\
 \FourierTwoPi{S_0}(\xi)
 &=\frac{1-\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2)}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}.\label{p2:eq:S0-transform}
\end{align}
Both values at zero are $1/2$.  Inserting \eqref{p2:eq:Phi-sinc},
\eqref{p2:eq:Phi-canonical}, or \eqref{p2:eq:Phi-GammaDy} turns these into explicit product
representations of the Fourier image of the bounded CDF.

\subsection{The global CDF as a tempered distribution}

Let $H$ be the Heaviside distribution.  Since $F=H\ast f_X$,
\begin{equation}
 \FourierTwoPi{H}=\frac12\delta_0+\frac{1}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit}\pv\frac1\xi
\end{equation}
gives the distributional formula
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \FourierTwoPi{F}
 =\frac12\delta_0+
 \frac{1}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit}\pv\left(
 \frac{\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2)}{\xi}
 \right).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:F-distribution-transform}
\end{equation}
This is the correct Fourier image of the globally bounded, nonintegrable CDF.  Omitting the
Dirac mass or principal value changes the distribution.

\subsection{Sine integral and odd-harmonic series}

Since $F(x)=\tfrac12+\int_0^{2x-1}\RvachevUp(y)\Differential y$, Fourier inversion yields
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 F(x)=\frac12+\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty
 \Phi(\xi)\frac{\sin(2\pi\xi(2x-1))}{\xi}\Differential\xi.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:F-sine-integral}
\end{equation}
This holds for all real $x$ under the bounded-CDF convention.  Integrating the interval
cosine series \eqref{p2:eq:up-half-cosine} gives a second representation.

\begin{theorem}[Odd-harmonic Fabius series \New]
For $0\le x\le1$,
{\small
\begin{align}
 F'(x)
 &=1-2\sum_{k\ge0}\Phi\left(k+\frac12\right)
 \cos\bigl(2\pi(2k+1)x\bigr),                            \label{p2:eq:Fprime-series}\\
 \boxed{\quad
 F(x)=x-\frac1\pi\sum_{k\ge0}
 \frac{\Phi(k+1/2)}{2k+1}
 \sin\bigl(2\pi(2k+1)x\bigr).
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:F-sine-series}
\end{align}
}
Both series converge absolutely and uniformly together with every differentiated series
allowed by the displayed factors.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $t=2x-1$ into \eqref{p2:eq:up-half-cosine} and multiply by two.  Since
\[
 \cos((2k+1)\pi(2x-1))=-\cos(2\pi(2k+1)x),
\]
we obtain \eqref{p2:eq:Fprime-series}.  Integrating from $0$ to $x$ and using $F(0)=0$ gives
\eqref{p2:eq:F-sine-series}.  Rapid decay of the half-integer samples follows from
$\RvachevUp\in C_c^\infty$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Gamma versions}

The new factorization makes the preceding formulae explicitly gamma-theoretic:
\begin{align}
 \FourierTwoPi{f_X}(\xi)
 &=\frac{\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}}
 {\GammaDy(\xi/2)\GammaDy(-\xi/2)},                       \label{p2:eq:fX-gamma}\\
 F(x)
 &=\frac12+\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty
 \frac{\sin(2\pi\xi(2x-1))}
 {\xi\GammaDy(\xi)\GammaDy(-\xi)}\Differential\xi.              \label{p2:eq:F-gamma-again}
\end{align}
Thus ordinary gamma functions, assembled on a dyadic scale, provide direct integral and
Fourier images for the Fabius CDF.

\emph{Frontier boundary.}  Although the common reciprocal-Gamma factor in these displays is
formal, no public declaration states either Gamma-version Fourier formula above.  They remain
frontier claims together with the integral representations in
\eqref{p2:eq:up-GammaDy-integral}--\eqref{p2:eq:F-GammaDy-integral}.

\section{Quantile and inverse-Fabius transform representations}
\label{p2:sec:inverse-fourier}

\subsection{Quantile pushforward and product transforms}

Let $Q=F^{-1}$ be the clamped inverse.  The current formal exposition proves the support-free
quantile substitution theorem
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1\varphi(Q(y))\Differential y=\Expectation\varphi(X)                 \label{p2:eq:quantile-substitution}
\end{equation}
for a broad Banach-valued class of integrands.  Therefore
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-sQ(y)}\Differential y
 &=\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{1-\EulerE^{-s/2^j}}{s/2^j},             \label{p2:eq:Q-Laplace}\\
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{s(Q(y)-1/2)}\Differential y
 &=\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{\sinh(s/2^{j+1})}{s/2^{j+1}},     \label{p2:eq:Q-centered-MGF}\\
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi Q(y)}\Differential y
 &=\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2).                        \label{p2:eq:Q-characteristic}
\end{align}
For $0<\Re\alpha<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1Q(y)^\alpha\Differential y
 =\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty
 \left[1-\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{1-\EulerE^{-t/2^j}}{t/2^j}\right]
 t^{-\alpha-1}\Differential t.                                    \label{p2:eq:Q-fractional-moment}
\end{equation}
These are \Derived consequences of the now-formal quantile theorem.

\subsection{A nonlinear Fourier transform of the inverse}

\begin{theorem}[Inverse-transform identity \New]
For $\eta\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus\{0\}$,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1Q(y)\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta y}\Differential y
 &=\int_0^1xF'(x)\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta F(x)}\Differential x,            \label{p2:eq:Q-transform-substitution}\\
 &=\frac{\displaystyle\int_0^1\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta F(x)}\Differential x
       -\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta}}
 {2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta}.                                        \label{p2:eq:Q-transform-nonlinear}
\end{align}
The value at $\eta=0$ is $1/2$ by continuity.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply $y=F(x)$, $\Differential y=F'(x)\Differential x$, and $Q(F(x))=x$ to obtain
\eqref{p2:eq:Q-transform-substitution}.  Since
\(
 \Differential\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta F(x)}
 =-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta F'(x)\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta F(x)}\Differential x,
\)
integration by parts gives \eqref{p2:eq:Q-transform-nonlinear}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sine series of the inverse in terms of \texorpdfstring{$F$}{F}}

\begin{theorem}[Nonlinear inverse-Fabius sine series \New]
Put
\begin{equation}
 c_n=\frac{1}{\pi n}\int_0^1\cos\bigl(2\pi nF(x)\bigr)\Differential x,
 \qquad n\ge1.                                           \label{p2:eq:Q-sine-coeff}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 Q(y)=y+\sum_{n\ge1}c_n\sin(2\pi ny),
 \qquad 0\le y\le1.
 \quad}                                                  \label{p2:eq:Q-sine-series}
\end{equation}
The series converges in $L^2(0,1)$ and pointwise at every $y\in[0,1]$.  Moreover
$c_n=\LittleO(1/n)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The function $g(y)=Q(y)-y$ is continuous, vanishes at both endpoints, and is of bounded
variation.  The symmetry $Q(1-y)=1-Q(y)$ gives $g(1-y)=-g(y)$, so only the modes
$\sin(2\pi ny)$ occur.  Their coefficients are
\[
 2\int_0^1g(y)\sin(2\pi ny)\Differential y.
\]
For the $Q$ term, use $y=F(x)$ and integrate by parts:
\begin{align*}
 \int_0^1Q(y)\sin(2\pi ny)\Differential y
 &=\int_0^1xF'(x)\sin(2\pi nF(x))\Differential x\\
 &=-\frac1{2\pi n}+\frac1{2\pi n}
   \int_0^1\cos(2\pi nF(x))\Differential x.
\end{align*}
Since $\int_0^1y\sin(2\pi ny)\Differential y=-1/(2\pi n)$, the difference is exactly
\eqref{p2:eq:Q-sine-coeff}.  Dirichlet--Jordan convergence applies to the bounded-variation
function $g$.  Finally,
\[
 \int_0^1\cos(2\pi nF(x))\Differential x
 =\int_0^1\cos(2\pi ny)Q'(y)\Differential y
\]
with $Q'\in L^1(0,1)$; the Riemann--Lebesgue lemma gives $c_n=\LittleO(1/n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Formula \eqref{p2:eq:Q-sine-series} is not an asymptotic reversion.  It is a global interval
representation of the inverse in which every coefficient is a nonlinear oscillatory integral
of the original CDF.  It therefore complements the repository's local Lambert--Bell inverse
expansions.
\end{remark}
