\section{Numerical experiments and reproducibility}
\label{p2:sec:numerics}

The package contains \texttt{numerical\_experiments.py}, written to be run without network
access.  It uses double precision, a fixed seed \texttt{20260827}, vectorized dyadic products,
and explicit tail diagnostics.  The program creates the figures in this section and writes a
plain-text audit trail to \texttt{figures/numerical\_results.txt}.

\subsection{Dyadic gamma factorization}

Three complex test points were evaluated with sixty product layers.  The absolute errors in
\eqref{p2:eq:Phi-GammaDy} were between $1.8\times10^{-15}$ and
$3.6\times10^{-15}$; the Mahler functional equation \eqref{p2:eq:GammaDy-Mahler} was verified at
comparable accuracy.  The finite-layer identity is exact mathematically, so the plateau in
\cref{p2:fig:gamma-check} is floating-point accumulation rather than truncation error.
The reciprocal identity and the totalized Gamma-denominator form are now proved independently
in \path{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean}; this figure remains a reproducibility check, not the
formal justification.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{assets/fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier/figures/gamma_factorization_convergence.png}
\caption{Finite-layer check of the dyadic-gamma reflection factorization.}
\label{p2:fig:gamma-check}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Jensen mean}

Angular trapezoidal quadrature with $8192$ nodes was compared with the exact zero-divisor
formula on $76$ radii.  The maximum absolute discrepancy was
$7.11\times10^{-15}$.  Representative values are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
$r$ & angular quadrature & exact formula & error \\
\midrule
$0.6$ & $-3.6082\times10^{-16}$ & $0$ & $-3.61\times10^{-16}$ \\
$1.4$ & $0.6729444732424255$ & $0.6729444732424258$ & $-3.33\times10^{-16}$ \\
$3.7$ & $5.496849257625430$ & $5.496849257625430$ & $0$ \\
$10.3$ & $26.15154490220755$ & $26.15154490220754$ & $3.55\times10^{-15}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{assets/fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier/figures/jensen_mean_comparison.png}
\caption{Jensen's angular mean versus the exact factorial/zero-divisor expression.}
\label{p2:fig:jensen-check}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Logistic series versus Gaussian mixture}

The simulation used $100{,}000$ samples.  The logistic series retained sixteen dyadic layers;
its omitted standard deviation was $5.09\times10^{-6}$.  The gamma mixture retained the first
$250$ spectral sites and added the exact mean of the positive tail.  The tail standard
deviation was $2.07\times10^{-4}$, negligible compared with the exact standard deviation
$1/3$ of $Z_1$.  The two-sample Kolmogorov--Smirnov statistic was $0.00347$ with p-value
$0.5825$.  Sample variances were $0.1114725$ and $0.1110627$, compared with the exact
$1/9=0.1111111\ldots$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{assets/fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier/figures/gaussian_mixture_characteristic_function.png}
\caption{The exact characteristic function and two independent simulations of the law in
\eqref{p2:eq:logistic-mixture-law}.}
\label{p2:fig:mixture-check}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Fabius sine series}

The series \eqref{p2:eq:F-sine-series} was compared with the independent Fourier integral
\eqref{p2:eq:F-sine-integral}.  At $x=0.25$, for example,
\[
 F(0.25)=0.06944444445136150\quad\text{(integral)},
 \qquad
 0.06944444444444434\quad\text{(128 harmonics)}.
\]
The discrepancy is governed by the numerical quadrature floor after roughly thirty-two
harmonics.  The rapid early convergence is visible in \cref{p2:fig:F-series-check}.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{assets/fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier/figures/fabius_sine_series_convergence.png}
\caption{Maximum error of the odd-harmonic Fabius series against Fourier inversion on ten
sample points.}
\label{p2:fig:F-series-check}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Reproduction command}

From the directory containing this consolidated report, run
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pythonstyle]
python3 assets/fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier/numerical_experiments.py
\end{lstlisting}
The script creates no hidden cache and writes all outputs beneath
\path{assets/fabius_rvachev_representation_frontier/figures/}.  The source comments explain
the product truncations, gamma-tail correction, and quadrature splitting at integer zeros.

\section{Conjectures and testable frontier statements}
\label{p2:sec:conjectures}

\begin{conjecture}[Differential transcendence of the Fourier determinant \Conj]
The functions $\Phi$, $G_{\mathrm{dy}}$, and $\GammaDy$ are differentially transcendental
over $\ComplexNumbers(z)$: none satisfies a nonzero algebraic differential equation with coefficients in
$\ComplexNumbers(z)$.
\end{conjecture}

The non-D-finite theorem is evidence but does not approach the full differential-algebraic
claim.  A promising route is to combine the Mahler equation
\eqref{p2:eq:GammaDy-Mahler} with differential Galois theory for mixed differential--difference
systems and the nonperiodic pole multiplicities.

\begin{conjecture}[Full Delange expansion of the Jensen mean \Conj]
There exist explicit continuous one-periodic functions $P_1,P_0$ such that
\begin{equation}
 J(r)=4r-\frac{(\log r)^2}{2\log2}
      +(\log r)P_1(\log_2r)+P_0(\log_2r)+\LittleO(1),      \label{p2:eq:J-Delange-conj}
\end{equation}
away from a controlled boundary layer around the integers.  The functions should be
computable from the Delange Fourier series for the binary digit sum and from the fractional
part $r-\Floor r$.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{conjecture}[Asymptotics of inverse Fourier coefficients \Conj]
Let $c_n$ be defined by \eqref{p2:eq:Q-sine-coeff}.  Then there is a bounded, nonconstant,
phase-dependent function $P$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \pi n c_n
 =Q(1/n)\left(P(\log_2\lambda_n)+\LittleO(1)\right),      \label{p2:eq:cn-conj}
\end{equation}
where $\lambda_n$ is the exact lower-Lambert saddle phase for $F(1/n)$.  In particular,
$\log|nc_n|$ should have leading scale
$-\sqrt{2\log2\,\log n}$ with the same periodic modulation that governs the inverse endpoint.
\end{conjecture}

This statement is testable by computing the oscillatory integral in
\eqref{p2:eq:Q-sine-coeff} with the repository's certified endpoint saddle machinery.  It would
turn the local inverse asymptotic into a global spectral law for $Q$.

\begin{conjecture}[Hyperbolic complete monotonicity of the mixture density \Conj]
For every $\tau>0$, the function $s\mapsto p_\tau(\sqrt s)$ is hyperbolically completely
monotone, not merely completely monotone.  Equivalently, the normal variance mixture may lie
in the Bondesson class with a stronger multiplicative total-positivity structure inherited
from the discrete Thorin measure.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{conjecture}[Arithmetic special values of the dyadic gamma function \Conj]
At rational arguments with odd denominator, suitable products of
$\GammaDy(r)$, powers of $2\pi$, and classical Barnes multiple-gamma constants reduce to a
finite family of dyadic $L$-values and logarithmic $q$-products.  The functional equation
\eqref{p2:eq:GammaDy-Mahler} should produce explicit distribution relations indexed by the
binary orbit of $r$.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{conjecture}[Optimal rational approximation from the Stieltjes resolvent \Conj]
Diagonal Pad\'e approximants obtained from
\(
 \Psi_{\mathrm{dy}}(-z)-\Psi_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)
\)
provide alternating, globally sharp rational bounds for $\Phi(\ImaginaryUnit y)$ and for the
characteristic function of $Z_\tau$ on $y>0$, with error constants controlled explicitly by
the next unused spectral atom.
\end{conjecture}

\section{Promising research directions}
\label{p2:sec:future}

\subsection{Formalization roadmap}

The shortest Lean route to the new results is modular.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2.3em]
\item \emph{Reciprocal tranche delivered.}  \path{ScaledInfiniteProducts.lean},
      \path{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean}, and \path{DyadicGammaOrder.lean} construct the
      entire reciprocal product, prove its generic Mahler/zero/reflection laws, identify its
      dyadic reflection with \(\Phi\), and give exact dyadic zero and pole orders.  Still open
      here is the distinct raw-product claim: construct
      \(\prod_h\Gamma(1+2^{-h}z)\) locally uniformly away from its poles and identify it there
      with the formal meromorphic inverse.
\item \emph{First-jet/tower follow-up delivered.}
      \path{ReciprocalGammaJets.lean} proves the exact first jet, simple analytic
      order, and punctured local coefficient of reciprocal Gamma at every
      nonpositive integer.  \path{ThueMorseGammaTower.lean} transfers those jets
      to every trivial zero of the entire Dirichlet--Mellin continuation and
      proves the Mellin/integral GammaLog, dyadic log/tower, and level-zero
      master-product ratio laws.  Its definitions are total in the real
      parameter, while the theorems use positive parameters; the remaining
      parameter-differential ladder requires differentiation under the Mellin
      integral.
\item Reuse the formal zero-multiplicity theorem to prove non-D-finiteness in a small
      analytic-ODE module.
\item Build $Z_\tau$ by conditioning a standard Gaussian on the already developed
      $V_\tau$, then identify characteristic functions.
\item Formalize Jensen's formula for the canonical product, followed by the finite factorial
      regrouping.  The digit-sum derivative then becomes arithmetic simplification.
\item Derive the compact and distributional Fourier transforms of $F$ from the existing
      transform of $\RvachevUp$ and a formal Heaviside distribution library.
\item Use the support-free quantile theorem and bounded-variation Fourier convergence to
      formalize \eqref{p2:eq:Q-transform-nonlinear} and \eqref{p2:eq:Q-sine-series}.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Spectral geometry and complex analysis}

The exact Jensen mean suggests treating $\Phi$ as an arithmetic spectral determinant in the
complex plane, not only on the real axis.  Natural next objects include logarithmic capacity,
angular large deviations of $\log|\Phi|$, Nevanlinna characteristics refined by binary digit
sums, and de Branges spaces generated by the reflected factors
$G_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)$ and $G_{\mathrm{dy}}(-z)$.

\subsection{Probability and semigroups}

The family $Z_\tau$ gives a canonical subordinate-Brownian semigroup attached to the Rvachev
zero divisor.  Its transition density, fractional generator, entropy production, and small-
time scaling should be expressible through the two heat kernels $L_1$ and $K$.  The explicit
Levy density \eqref{p2:eq:Z-Levy-density} makes simulation, self-decomposability, and potential
theory accessible without reconstructing the gamma series term by term.

\subsection{Base-\texorpdfstring{$b$}{b} and geometric-\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q} families}

The entire reciprocal form of the geometric factorization
\eqref{p2:eq:q-gamma-reflection} is delivered for every complex \(q\) with
\(\lVert q\rVert<1\), in parallel with the repository's base-generic Gaussian-binomial
interpolation.  The remaining comparison concerns raw Gamma products and exact analytic
collision orders: for integer \(b\), the claimed order is a whole-base valuation, whereas for
nonintegral \(q^{-1}\) the pole orbits are typically noncoincident.  Only the dyadic order is
currently formal.  Comparing the remaining regimes may separate which phenomena are
genuinely dyadic from those caused only by geometric scaling.

\subsection{Inverse spectral analysis}

The coefficients \eqref{p2:eq:Q-sine-coeff} provide a concrete meeting point for global Fourier
analysis and endpoint Lambert asymptotics.  Certified steepest-descent evaluation of these
coefficients could reveal periodic modulation, sign patterns, and optimal approximation
rates for the inverse.  A parallel study of
\(
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta F(x)}\Differential x
\)
would produce a nonlinear Fourier theory of the CDF itself.

\subsection{Computer-assisted certification}

The existing Python checks are exploratory.  A rigorous package should combine interval
arithmetic for gamma products, exact rational moment recurrences, certified quadrature split
at the integer zero set, and explicit tail bounds from the rapid Fourier decay.  Such a tool
could test the conjectural Jensen fluctuation and inverse-coefficient phases at hundreds of
dyadic scales without confusing truncation error with genuine periodic oscillation.

\section{Conclusion}

The Fabius--Rvachev system is unusually rich because the same object can be read as a smooth
compact bump, a CDF, an infinite uniform convolution, a Thue--Morse spline limit, an entire
sinc determinant, an arithmetic spectrum, a $q$-series, and a Lambert-saddle problem.  The
source audit shows that many attractive formulae already coexist in the repository; treating
them as separate discoveries would obscure rather than advance the subject.

The new results in this report add bridges rather than isolated identities.  The dyadic gamma
function splits the even Fourier determinant into reflected arithmetic gamma factors.  The
Gaussian-mixture theorem joins the logistic/Appell dual to the Thorin/gamma dual and exposes a
new Levy semigroup.  Jensen's formula turns binary digit sums into a radial complex-analytic
observable.  Unbounded multiplicities settle non-holonomicity in one stroke.  Finally, the
bounded CDF and its inverse acquire transform formulae that are global, explicit, and
compatible with the existing endpoint theory.  Each bridge supplies both a theorem and a
research program, with a comparatively short path to formal verification.

The reciprocal-product core of the first bridge is now formal at checkpoint
\path{71ab6f6728fceb753c88d8b0573077a59acf2682}: it includes generic complex contractions,
Mahler and zero-orbit laws, reflection, the dyadic Rvachev identity, and exact dyadic
zero/pole orders.  The raw Gamma-product realization and the canonical, logarithmic,
digamma, integral, heat-trace, D-finite, derivative-tower, and general-base extensions retain
their frontier status.

The current overlay sharpens two of those historical boundaries:
commit \path{0ba35abd4} supplies all reciprocal-Gamma first jets and the
first eight continuation GammaLog/tower declarations, and the current
integrated tree adds the level-zero master-product ratio.  It also closes the
zero set of \(G_{1/b}\) at every integer base \(b\ge2\) in
\path{ReciprocalIntegerGammaZeros.lean}.  Thus the
Mellin/integral GammaLog and dyadic log/tower layers are formal at every natural
level.  Only the parameter-differential and iterated differential ladder remains
frontier within that tower; the raw Gamma product and the canonical,
logarithmic/digamma, Malmsten/heat-trace, and D-finite extensions remain frontier,
as does the collision \emph{order} at general integer base.

\section{Selected derivations in one line}
\label{p2:app:one-line}

For convenient reuse, here are the central bridges in compressed form:
{\footnotesize
\begin{align*}
 \Gamma(1+w)^{-1}\Gamma(1-w)^{-1}&=\SincRad(\pi w)
 &&\Longrightarrow\quad
 \begin{aligned}
  \Phi(z)&=G_{\mathrm{dy}}(z)G_{\mathrm{dy}}(-z)\\
          &=1/[\GammaDy(z)\GammaDy(-z)],
 \end{aligned}\\
 \Expectation\EulerE^{-wV_\tau}&=\Acal(w)^{-\tau},\quad
 \Expectation(\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t\sqrt{2T}N}\mid T)=\EulerE^{-t^2T}
 &&\Longrightarrow\quad Z_1\distPartx\sqrt{2T_1}N,\\
 \operatorname{zeros}(\Phi)&=\{\pm n\text{ with multiplicity }a_n\}
 &&\Longrightarrow\quad J(r)=2\sum_{n\le r}a_n\log(r/n),\\
 a_{2^k}&=k+1\to\infty
 &&\Longrightarrow\quad \Phi\text{ is not D-finite},\\
 F'(x)&=2\RvachevUp(2x-1),\quad \FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}=\Phi
 &&\Longrightarrow\quad \FourierTwoPi{F'}(\xi)=\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2),\\
 y&=F(x),\quad Q(F(x))=x
 &&\Longrightarrow\quad
 \FourierTwoPi{Q}(\eta)=\frac{\int_0^1\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta F(x)}\Differential x-
 \EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta}}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\eta}.
\end{align*}
}

\part{Dyadic Multiresolution and Product-Series Representations in the Fabius--Rvachev System}
% Absorbed from Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Report/fabius_rvachev_multiresolution_report.tex




\begin{abstract}
The existing \repo{} corpus develops the Fabius function, its inverse, Rvachev's
compactly supported up-function, the Thue--Morse sequence, exact dyadic values,
$q$-binomial formulas, infinite sinc products, Bell and Bernoulli polynomial
machinery, and phase-resolved Lambert--$W$ endpoint asymptotics in considerable
depth.  This report first organizes those results into a representation atlas and
then develops a complementary multiresolution layer that was not located in the
audited LaTeX corpus.

Let $p=F'$ and let
$\Delta_{N,k}=F((k+1)2^{-N})-F(k2^{-N})$ be the exact dyadic cell masses.
The same rational projective array produces four different finite models.  It
produces a fixed-cell uniform mixture for $p$ and $\RvachevUp$, an exact rational
unnormalized Haar expansion of $p$, a rational Faber--Schauder expansion of $F$,
and the finite Walsh--Hadamard transform of $p$.  Their Fourier images yield
locally uniform limits of rational exponential polynomials multiplied by sinc
windows.  The Walsh transform of every basis function is itself a finite product
of sines and cosines.  Consequently the infinite sinc product admits a new
series whose summands are finite trigonometric products and whose coefficients
are exact Walsh transforms of dyadic Fabius masses.

A Walsh mode indexed by $m$ has exactly $\BinaryDigitSum(m)$ vanishing algebraic moments.
Writing $\sigma(m)=\sum_jj b_j$ for the positions of the nonzero binary digits,
its first nonzero moment is
\[
  \int_0^1x^{\BinaryDigitSum(m)}w_m(x)\Differential x
  =(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(m)}\BinaryDigitSum(m)!\,2^{-2\BinaryDigitSum(m)-\sigma(m)}.
\]
Summing over a fixed binary weight produces the Gaussian binomial coefficient
$\GaussianBinomial{N}{r}{1/2}$.  Higher Walsh moments are encoded by a finite
Bell-polynomial expression whose logarithmic coefficients are explicit
Bernoulli-number sums.  This gives a direct bridge among Walsh cancellation,
Thue--Morse signs, $q$-binomial coefficients, and the Bell--Bernoulli structure
of the Fabius moments.

A second positive approximation is obtained by differentiating the Bernstein
polynomial of $F$: $p$ is the uniform limit of finite mixtures of beta densities
with rational weights $\Delta_{N,k}$.  Its moment-generating and Fourier
transforms are therefore limits of positive rational mixtures of confluent
hypergeometric functions.  A dual construction uses dyadic values of the inverse
$G=F^{-1}$: equal probability cells with variable spatial widths give a finite
sinc-mixture formula for $\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}$, as well as polynomial limits for all
moments.

Finally, the exact Haar detail energy $E_N$ is rational and has an all-orders
continuum expansion
\[
 E_N\sim \frac{h^2}{16}\|p'\|_2^2-
             \frac{h^4}{384}\|p''\|_2^2+
             \frac{h^6}{20480}\|p'''\|_2^2-\cdots,
 \qquad h=2^{-N}.
\]
The complete coefficient sequence is generated by $\SincRad^4$, hence by complete
Bell polynomials in Bernoulli numbers.  The beyond-all-orders aliasing remainder
provides a new place where the known log-periodic Fourier decay should reappear.
The report distinguishes repository input, results proved here, numerical checks,
and conjectures; ``new'' always means new relative to the audited repository
corpus, not an assertion of worldwide priority.
\end{abstract}

\subsection{Pinned corpus and cumulative audit}

The documentation tree
\begin{center}
\url{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt/tree/main/Analysis/FabiusFunction/docs}
\end{center}
was audited recursively.  The Git tree contained $89$ files with extension
\texttt{.tex} at the source-level inventory used in the audit.  During this
investigation the branch advanced; the report is pinned to commit
\nolinkurl{924652f4a9a656fd957d2936b2f053747c6f6c47}.  The five-commit delta from
the preceding pin modified the primary exposition, its coverage ledger, and an
integral-transform report, but added no new TeX path.  The delta was checked
before the novelty boundary below was frozen.

The audit is cumulative rather than a naive count of duplicated revisions.
Earlier source-level passes covered the complete path inventory; the present
pass reread the canonical primary exposition, current audit ledgers, active
frontier syntheses, and every recent report capable of overlapping the proposed
results.  Historical copies under \texttt{archive/} were normalized against
their live successors.  Imported papers were used as literature baselines, not
counted as independent repository discoveries.

The current primary exposition and Lean modules include the probability law of
weighted uniform series, finite atomic and spline approximants, exact dyadic
values, quantile transport, sinc and cosine products, Fourier inversion and
Poisson summation, moment recurrences, a Fourier--Legendre expansion, normalized
integer Volterra calculus, inverse-Fabius infrastructure, and the proved
Lambert-phase endpoint machinery.  These are inputs below.  Repackaging one of
those statements with different notation is not counted as a new result.

\section{Normalization and the function triad}
\label{p3:sec:normalization}

\subsection{Bounded Fabius function, density, and Rvachev function}

Throughout, $F:\RealNumbers\to[0,1]$ is the bounded Fabius distribution function,
\[
 F(x)=0\quad(x\le0),\qquad F(x)=1\quad(x\ge1),
\]
and $\RvachevUp$ is Rvachev's even $C^\infty$ function supported on $[-1,1]$.
On their common fundamental intervals,
\begin{align}
 F(x)&=\RvachevUp(x-1), &&0\le x\le1,\label{p3:eq:F-up-shift}\\
 F'(x)&=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)=:p(x), &&0<x<1,\label{p3:eq:p-def}\\
 F(x)+F(1-x)&=1, &&0\le x\le1,\label{p3:eq:F-symmetry}\\
 \RvachevUp(x)&=1-F(x)=F(1-x), &&0\le x\le1.\label{p3:eq:up-survival}
\end{align}
The density $p$, extended by zero outside $[0,1]$, is a compactly supported
$C^\infty$ function satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:p-symmetry}
 p(x)=p(1-x),\qquad \int_0^1p(x)\Differential x=1.
\end{equation}
The ordinary inverse of $F|_{[0,1]}$ is denoted
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:G-def}
 G=F^{-1}:[0,1]\longrightarrow[0,1],
 \qquad G(1-u)=1-G(u).
\end{equation}
The signed global extension $\FabiusGlobal$, important in the repository's
repeated-integration theory, is not needed for the multiresolution proofs; it
appears only in the baseline atlas.

\subsection{Fourier convention}

For an integrable function $f$ we use angular frequency,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Fourier-convention}
 \FourierAngular{f}(t)=\int_\RealNumbers f(x)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential x,
 \qquad
 f(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\RealNumbers\FourierAngular{f}(t)\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential t.
\end{equation}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Phi-M-def}
 \Phi(t)=\FourierAngular{\RvachevUp}(t),\qquad
 M(z)=\int_{-1}^1\RvachevUp(x)\EulerE^{zx}\Differential x=\Phi(\ImaginaryUnit z).
\end{equation}
The compact-interval Fourier image of the bounded Fabius function is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Fhat-def}
 \mathscr F_F(t)=\int_0^1F(x)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential x.
\end{equation}
This notation avoids the meaningless full-line Fourier transform of a function
that equals one on $[1,\infty)$.

\subsection{Probabilistic model}

Let $U_1,U_2,\ldots$ be independent uniform random variables on $[-1,1]$ and
let $V_j=(U_j+1)/2$, uniform on $[0,1]$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:random-series}
 Y=\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}U_j,\qquad
 X=\frac{Y+1}{2}=\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}V_j.
\end{equation}
The law of $Y$ has density $\RvachevUp$, and $F$ is the distribution function of $X$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:quantile-push}
 X\overset d=G(U),\qquad
 Y\overset d=2G(U)-1,
 \qquad U\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1].
\end{equation}

\section{A representation atlas from the existing theory}
\label{p3:sec:atlas}

This section collects a broad common baseline.  Most formulas are already
present, sometimes in stronger form, in the audited repository.  They are
included so that the genuinely new representations later can be compared
against one normalization without forcing the reader to move among several
manuscripts.

\subsection{Infinite convolution, cube integrals, and finite heads}

Let $\nu_a$ denote the uniform probability density on $[-a,a]$.
Independence in \eqref{p3:eq:random-series} gives the infinite convolution
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:infinite-convolution}
 \RvachevUp=\nu_{1/2}*\nu_{1/4}*\nu_{1/8}*\cdots.
\end{equation}
In distributional notation this is the infinite-cube integral
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:delta-cube}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\int_{[-1,1]^{\NaturalNumbers}}
 \delta\!\left(x-\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}u_j\right)
 \prod_{j\ge1}\frac{\Differential u_j}{2},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:F-cube}
 F(x)=\int_{[0,1]^{\NaturalNumbers}}
 \IndicatorOf{\{\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}v_j\le x\}}
 \prod_{j\ge1}\Differential v_j.
\end{equation}

For the finite head
\[
 Y_N=\sum_{j=1}^N2^{-j}U_j,
 \qquad A_N=\sum_{j=1}^N2^{-j}=1-2^{-N},
\]
write $B_N$ for its density.  Inclusion--exclusion for a box spline gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:BN-truncated-power}
 B_N(x)=\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \sum_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^N}(-1)^{|\varepsilon|}
 \left(x+A_N-2\sum_{j=1}^N\varepsilon_j2^{-j}\right)_+^{N-1}.
\end{equation}
The signs $(-1)^{|\varepsilon|}$ are the finite Thue--Morse signs.  Likewise,
for $X_N=\sum_{j=1}^N2^{-j}V_j$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:FN-truncated-power}
 \Probability(X_N\le x)=\frac{2^{N(N+1)/2}}{N!}
 \sum_{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^N}(-1)^{|\varepsilon|}
 \left(x-\sum_{j=1}^N\varepsilon_j2^{-j}\right)_+^N.
\end{equation}
Taking $N\to\infty$ gives $B_N\to\RvachevUp$ and the finite CDFs tend to $F$.

The tail is an independent scaled copy:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:head-tail}
 X\overset d=X_N+2^{-N}X',\qquad
 Y\overset d=Y_N+2^{-N}Y'.
\end{equation}
Hence the exact finite-scale identities
\begin{align}
 F(x)&=\Expectation\,F\!\left(2^N(x-X_N)\right),\label{p3:eq:F-head-tail}\\
 \RvachevUp(x)&=2^N\int_\RealNumbers B_N(y)\RvachevUp\!\left(2^N(x-y)\right)\Differential y.\label{p3:eq:up-head-tail}
\end{align}
These formulas combine a finite Thue--Morse spline with a shrunken copy of the
same limiting function.

\subsection{Sinc, hyperbolic-sinc, and renormalization products}

The Fourier and moment-generating products are
\begin{align}
 \Phi(t)&=\prod_{j=1}^\infty\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right),
 &\SincRad z&=\frac{\sin z}{z},\label{p3:eq:Phi-sinc-product}\\
 M(z)&=\prod_{j=1}^\infty\frac{\sinh(z/2^j)}{z/2^j}.
 \label{p3:eq:M-sinh-product}
\end{align}
They converge locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$, and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:renormalization-products}
 \Phi(2t)=\SincRad(t)\Phi(t),\qquad
 M(2z)=\frac{\sinh z}{z}M(z).
\end{equation}
Iteration at any finite depth gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:finite-tail-product}
 \Phi(t)=\Phi(t/2^N)\prod_{j=1}^N\SincRad(t/2^j),
\end{equation}
which is the transform counterpart of \eqref{p3:eq:up-head-tail}.

Euler's product for sine yields two canonical products:
\begin{align}
 \Phi(t)
 &=\prod_{m=1}^\infty
   \left(\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2m^2};\frac14\right)_\infty,
 \label{p3:eq:qpoch-Phi}\\
 &=\prod_{n=1}^\infty
   \left(1-\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2n^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}.
 \label{p3:eq:zero-product}
\end{align}
Thus $2\pi n$ is a zero of multiplicity $1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$.  For $|t|<2\pi$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:log-Phi-zeta}
 \log\Phi(t)
 =-\sum_{r=1}^\infty
 \frac{\zeta(2r)}{r\pi^{2r}(4^r-1)}t^{2r}.
\end{equation}
The Wick-rotated determinant
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:wick-product}
 \mathcal A(s)=\Phi(\ImaginaryUnit\sqrt{s})
 =\prod_{n=1}^\infty
 \left(1+\frac{s}{4\pi^2n^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}
\end{equation}
underlies the spectral-zeta, total-positivity, and generalized-gamma-convolution
results already developed in the corpus.

\subsection{Moments, cumulants, Bell polynomials, and Bernoulli numbers}

Write
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:central-moments}
 \mu_n=\int_{-1}^1x^n\RvachevUp(x)\Differential x=\Expectation Y^n.
\end{equation}
Odd moments vanish.  The even cumulants are
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:up-cumulants}
 \kappa_{2r}(Y)=\frac{2^{2r}B_{2r}}{2r(2^{2r}-1)},
 \qquad \kappa_{2r+1}(Y)=0.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Bell-moments}
 \mu_n=Y_n\bigl(0,\kappa_2,0,\kappa_4,\ldots,\kappa_n\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $Y_n$ is the complete exponential Bell polynomial, and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Phi-Taylor}
 \Phi(t)=\sum_{r=0}^\infty\frac{(-1)^r\mu_{2r}}{(2r)!}t^{2r}.
\end{equation}
For $m_n=\Expectation X^n$ the smoothing equation
$X\overset d=(V+X')/2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Fabius-moment-recurrence}
 (2^n-1)m_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom nk\frac{m_k}{n-k+1},
 \qquad m_0=1.
\end{equation}
All $m_n$ are rational, and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:central-one-sided-moment}
 \mu_{2r}=\frac{2m_{2r+1}}{2r+1}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fourier inversion, twisted Poisson summation, and cosine series}

Since $\Phi$ is rapidly decreasing on the real axis,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:up-Fourier-inversion}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\RealNumbers\Phi(t)\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential t
 =\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty\Phi(t)\cos(tx)\Differential t.
\end{equation}
The distribution function of the centered law gives, for $0\le x\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:F-GilPelaez}
 F(x)=\frac12+\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty
 \Phi(t)\frac{\sin((2x-1)t)}{t}\Differential t.
\end{equation}
Combining this with $F(x)=\RvachevUp(x-1)$ produces the integral identity
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:sine-cosine-duality}
 \int_0^\infty\Phi(t)\cos((1-x)t)\Differential t
 =\frac\pi2+\int_0^\infty\Phi(t)
   \frac{\sin((2x-1)t)}{t}\Differential t.
\end{equation}

The partition of unity $\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\RvachevUp(x-k)=1$ kills all nonzero integer
Fourier modes.  Twisting by $(-1)^k$ leaves the half-integer modes:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:twisted-Poisson}
 \sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}(-1)^k\RvachevUp(x-k)
 =\sum_{m\in\IntegerNumbers}\Phi((2m+1)\pi)\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit(2m+1)\pi x}.
\end{equation}
For $x\in[0,1]$ the left side is $1-2F(x)$.  With
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:A_m}
 A_m=\Phi((2m+1)\pi),
\end{equation}
we obtain the rapidly convergent series
\begin{align}
 \RvachevUp(x)&=\frac12+\sum_{m=0}^\infty A_m
          \cos((2m+1)\pi x), &&|x|\le1,\label{p3:eq:up-half-series}\\
 F(x)&=\frac12-\sum_{m=0}^\infty A_m
          \cos((2m+1)\pi x), &&0\le x\le1,\label{p3:eq:F-half-series}\\
 p(x)&=\sum_{m=0}^\infty A_m(2m+1)\pi
          \sin((2m+1)\pi x).\label{p3:eq:p-half-series}
\end{align}
Every derivative may be taken termwise.

\subsection[Fourier images of p, F, and 1-F]{Fourier images of $p$, $F$, and $1-F$}

The affine relation between $p$ and $\RvachevUp$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:p-transform}
 \FourierAngular{p}(t)=\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t/2}\Phi(t/2).
\end{equation}
Integration by parts then yields the exact compact-interval transforms
\begin{align}
 \mathscr F_F(t)
 &=\frac{\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t/2}\Phi(t/2)-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t}}{\ImaginaryUnit t},
 \label{p3:eq:F-transform-product}\\
 \int_0^1(1-F(x))\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential x
 &=\frac{1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t/2}\Phi(t/2)}{\ImaginaryUnit t}.
 \label{p3:eq:survival-transform-product}
\end{align}
The singularities at $t=0$ are removable.  After Wick rotation,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{zx}F(x)\Differential x
 &=\frac{\EulerE^z-\EulerE^{z/2}M(z/2)}{z},\label{p3:eq:F-Laplace}\\
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{zx}(1-F(x))\Differential x
 &=\frac{\EulerE^{z/2}M(z/2)-1}{z}.
 \label{p3:eq:survival-Laplace}
\end{align}

\subsection{Quantile, Stieltjes, and correlation representations}

Quantile transport \eqref{p3:eq:quantile-push} gives
\begin{align}
 \Phi(t)&=\int_0^1\exp\bigl(-\ImaginaryUnit t(2G(u)-1)\bigr)\Differential u,
 \label{p3:eq:Phi-quantile}\\
 M(z)&=\int_0^1\exp\bigl(z(2G(u)-1)\bigr)\Differential u,
 \label{p3:eq:M-quantile}\\
 \mu_n&=\int_0^1(2G(u)-1)^n\Differential u.\label{p3:eq:moments-quantile}
\end{align}
For $z\notin[-1,1]$, the Cauchy transform is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Cauchy-quantile}
 \mathcal S(z)=\int_{-1}^1\frac{\RvachevUp(x)}{z-x}\Differential x
 =\int_0^1\frac{\Differential u}{z-(2G(u)-1)}.
\end{equation}
If $\Im z>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Cauchy-Fourier}
 \mathcal S(z)=-\ImaginaryUnit\int_0^\infty\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tz}\Phi(t)\Differential t.
\end{equation}
The autocorrelation and convolution powers are
\begin{align}
 \int_\RealNumbers\RvachevUp(x)\RvachevUp(x+y)\Differential x
 &=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\RealNumbers\Phi(t)^2\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit ty}\Differential t,
 \label{p3:eq:autocorrelation}\\
 \RvachevUp^{*r}(x)&=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\RealNumbers\Phi(t)^r\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential t,
 \qquad r\in\NaturalNumbers.\label{p3:eq:convolution-powers}
\end{align}

\subsection{Legendre and Chebyshev coefficient integrals}

The repository contains a Fourier--Legendre expansion.  Fourier inversion gives
a useful transform-side form of its coefficients.  If
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Legendre-expansion}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}\lambda_nP_n(x),
 \qquad
 \lambda_n=\frac{2n+1}{2}\int_{-1}^1\RvachevUp(x)P_n(x)\Differential x,
\end{equation}
then odd coefficients vanish and, with $j_n$ the spherical Bessel function,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Legendre-Bessel}
 \lambda_{2r}=\frac{4r+1}{\pi}(-1)^r
 \int_0^\infty\Phi(t)j_{2r}(t)\Differential t.
\end{equation}
Indeed,
$\int_{-1}^1\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tx}P_n(x)\Differential x=2\ImaginaryUnit^n j_n(t)$.

A parallel Chebyshev representation, apparently not stated in the audited
corpus, is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Chebyshev-expansion}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\frac{a_0}{2}+\sum_{n\ge1}a_nT_n(x),
 \qquad
 a_n=\frac2\pi\int_0^\pi\RvachevUp(\cos\theta)\cos(n\theta)\Differential\theta.
\end{equation}
The odd coefficients vanish and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Chebyshev-Bessel}
 a_{2r}=\frac{2(-1)^r}{\pi}
 \int_0^\infty\Phi(t)J_{2r}(t)\Differential t.
\end{equation}
The function $\theta\mapsto\RvachevUp(\cos\theta)$ is smooth and periodic, so the
Chebyshev coefficients decrease faster than every power.

\section{The projective dyadic mass array}
\label{p3:sec:dyadic-masses}

\subsection{Definition and exact algebra}

Put
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:nN-hN}
 n_N=2^N,
 \qquad h_N=2^{-N},
 \qquad I_{N,k}=[kh_N,(k+1)h_N),
\end{equation}
with the last interval closed at $1$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Delta-def}
 \Delta_{N,k}
 =F((k+1)h_N)-F(kh_N),
 \qquad 0\le k<n_N.
\end{equation}
These are the probabilities that $X$ lies in the dyadic cells.

\begin{theorem}[Projective rational mass array]
\label{p3:thm:projective-masses}
For every $N\ge0$, put
\[
\Delta_N=(\Delta_{N,0},\ldots,\Delta_{N,n_N-1}).
\]
This vector has the following properties:
\begin{align}
 \Delta_{N,k}&\in\RationalNumbers_{>0},\label{p3:eq:mass-rational}\\
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}&=1,\label{p3:eq:mass-sum}\\
 \Delta_{N,n_N-1-k}&=\Delta_{N,k},\label{p3:eq:mass-symmetry}\\
 \Delta_{N,k}&=\Delta_{N+1,2k}+\Delta_{N+1,2k+1}.
 \label{p3:eq:mass-projective}
\end{align}
Consequently the mass polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:mass-polynomial}
 D_N(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}z^k
\end{equation}
has rational coefficients, satisfies $D_N(1)=1$, and is palindromic:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:D-palindromic}
 D_N(z)=z^{n_N-1}D_N(z^{-1}).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Rationality is the exact dyadic-value theorem from the repository.  Positivity
comes from strict increase of $F$ on $(0,1)$.  The sum telescopes.  Reflection
\eqref{p3:eq:mass-symmetry} follows from $F(1-x)=1-F(x)$, and
\eqref{p3:eq:mass-projective} is additivity after bisecting a cell.  The polynomial
statements are immediate.
\end{proof}

The array is therefore a rational projective system of probability vectors.
It is useful to regard it as the common discrete skeleton behind all subsequent
representations.  No approximation has yet been made.

\subsection{Fixed-cell uniform mixtures}

Define the dyadic conditional-expectation density
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pN-def}
 p_N(x)=n_N\Delta_{N,k},\qquad x\in I_{N,k}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pN-mixture}
 p_N(x)\Differential x
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}
   \operatorname{Unif}(I_{N,k}).
\end{equation}
This is a finite mixture of equal-width uniform laws with variable rational
weights.  On the centered $\RvachevUp$ scale, put
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:up-cell-centers}
 u_{N,k}=\frac{2k+1}{n_N}-1.
\end{equation}
The corresponding approximation is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:upN-def}
 \RvachevUp_N(u)=\frac12p_N\!\left(\frac{u+1}{2}\right)
 =\frac{n_N}{2}\Delta_{N,k},
 \qquad |u-u_{N,k}|<\frac1{n_N}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Martingale and uniform convergence]
\label{p3:thm:martingale-convergence}
Let $\mathcal D_N$ be the dyadic sigma-algebra on $[0,1]$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:conditional-expectation}
 p_N=\Expectation[p\mid\mathcal D_N].
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pN-uniform}
 \|p_N-p\|_\infty\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \|p_N-p\|_{L^q(0,1)}\longrightarrow0
 \quad(0<q<\infty),
\end{equation}
and $\RvachevUp_N\to\RvachevUp$ in the corresponding centered norms.
If $p\in C^1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pN-simple-rate}
 \|p_N-p\|_\infty\le h_N\|p'\|_\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The average of $p$ on $I_{N,k}$ is
$h_N^{-1}\int_{I_{N,k}}p=n_N\Delta_{N,k}$, proving
\eqref{p3:eq:conditional-expectation}.  Uniform continuity gives uniform
convergence of cell averages; the displayed bound follows from the mean-value
theorem.  Finite-measure $L^q$ convergence follows from uniform convergence.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Although $p_N$ is only piecewise constant, all its coefficients are exact.  It
is not the finite-head spline $B_N$ of \eqref{p3:eq:BN-truncated-power}: $B_N$ is
obtained by truncating the random series, whereas $p_N$ is obtained by
conditioning the limiting density on a spatial dyadic partition.  The two
approximations therefore probe different structures.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Rational exponential-polynomial limits for the transforms}

The transform of \eqref{p3:eq:pN-mixture} can be written directly in terms of the
mass polynomial.

\begin{theorem}[Primal dyadic transform representations]
\label{p3:thm:primal-transform}
For every $z\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pN-transform-polynomial}
 \FourierAngular{p_N}(z)
 =\frac{n_N(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/n_N})}{\ImaginaryUnit z}
   D_N(\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/n_N}).
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{align}
 \Phi_N^{\mathrm{cell}}(t)
 &:=\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}\FourierAngular{p_N}(2t)\label{p3:eq:PhiN-cell-def}\\
 &=\SincRad(t/n_N)
   \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}
   \exp\!\left[-\ImaginaryUnit t\left(\frac{2k+1}{n_N}-1\right)\right].
 \label{p3:eq:PhiN-cell}
\end{align}
The limits
\begin{align}
 \FourierAngular{p}(z)&=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{n_N(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/n_N})}{\ImaginaryUnit z}
 D_N(\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/n_N}),\label{p3:eq:p-transform-mass-limit}\\
 \Phi(t)&=\lim_{N\to\infty}\SincRad(t/n_N)
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t u_{N,k}}
 \label{p3:eq:Phi-cell-limit}
\end{align}
are locally uniform on $\ComplexNumbers$.  Thus the infinite sinc product is a locally
uniform limit of rational exponential polynomials times one elementary sinc
window.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Integrating $p_N$ cell by cell gives
\[
 \int_{kh_N}^{(k+1)h_N}n_N\Delta_{N,k}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit zx}\Differential x
 =\Delta_{N,k}\frac{n_N(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/n_N})}{\ImaginaryUnit z}
  \EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit zk/n_N},
\]
which sums to \eqref{p3:eq:pN-transform-polynomial}.  The affine change from $p$
to $\RvachevUp$ gives \eqref{p3:eq:PhiN-cell}.  If $K\subset\ComplexNumbers$ is compact, then
\[
 \sup_{z\in K}|\FourierAngular{p_N}(z)-\FourierAngular{p}(z)|
 \le \EulerE^{\sup_{z\in K}|\Im z|}\|p_N-p\|_1,
\]
so convergence is locally uniform.  The same argument applies after the affine
change of variables.
\end{proof}

Deleting the sinc window gives the midpoint-atomic representation
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Phi-atomic-limit}
 \Phi(t)=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t u_{N,k}}.
\end{equation}
The cell version is usually more accurate because it integrates the test
function across each cell rather than concentrating the mass at its midpoint.

After Wick rotation one obtains the moment-generating limit
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:M-cell-limit}
 M(z)=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{\sinh(z/n_N)}{z/n_N}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}
 \exp\!\left[z\left(\frac{2k+1}{n_N}-1\right)\right].
\end{equation}
Equations \eqref{p3:eq:Phi-cell-limit} and \eqref{p3:eq:M-cell-limit} are the same
finite mixture on the imaginary and real axes.

\subsection{A second-order quadrature principle}

The conditional-expectation property gives better weak accuracy than the
pointwise bound \eqref{p3:eq:pN-simple-rate} suggests.

\begin{proposition}[Cell-average quadrature]
\label{p3:prop:cell-quadrature}
If $p,g\in C^1[0,1]$, then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:quadrature-bound}
 \left|\int_0^1g(x)(p(x)-p_N(x))\Differential x\right|
 \le h_N^2\|g'\|_\infty\|p'\|_\infty.
\end{equation}
In particular, on each compact set $|z|\le R$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:transform-O4N}
 \FourierAngular{p_N}(z)-\FourierAngular{p}(z)=\BigO_R(4^{-N}),
 \qquad
 \Phi_N^{\mathrm{cell}}(z)-\Phi(z)=\BigO_R(4^{-N}).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On a cell $I$ choose any $x_I\in I$.  Since
$\int_I(p-p_N)=0$,
\[
 \int_Ig(p-p_N)=\int_I(g-g(x_I))(p-p_N).
\]
Now $\osc_Ig\le h_N\|g'\|_\infty$ and
$\int_I|p-p_N|\le h_N\osc_Ip\le h_N^2\|p'\|_\infty$.
Summing $n_N=h_N^{-1}$ cells proves \eqref{p3:eq:quadrature-bound}.
For exponential test functions, their derivatives are uniformly bounded on
compact subsets of $\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{proof}

The $4^{-N}$ behavior is visible in the numerical experiment of
\cref{p3:sec:numerics}.  The atomic midpoint formula is also second order for a
smooth density and smooth test function, but the sinc-window formula has a
clearer probabilistic interpretation: it is exactly the transform of the
conditional-expectation density.

\subsection[Moments, Lq norms, and discrete entropies]{Moments, $L^q$ norms, and discrete entropies}

For $q>0$, uniform convergence gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Lq-mass-limit}
 \int_0^1p(x)^q\Differential x
 =\lim_{N\to\infty}n_N^{q-1}
  \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}^q.
\end{equation}
For $q=2$ this is an exact rational approximation to the collision integral,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:up-L2-masses}
 \int_{-1}^1\RvachevUp(x)^2\Differential x
 =\frac12\lim_{N\to\infty}n_N
  \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}^2.
\end{equation}
Differentiating \eqref{p3:eq:Lq-mass-limit} at $q=1$ gives the Shannon asymptotic
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:mass-entropy}
 -\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}\log\Delta_{N,k}
 =\log n_N-\int_0^1p(x)\log p(x)\Differential x+o(1).
\end{equation}
Here $p\log p$ extends continuously by zero at the endpoints.  Thus the
continuous entropy of the Fabius density is the constant term in the entropy of
its exact rational dyadic mass vectors.

For ordinary moments, integrating a monomial over each uniform cell gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:moment-cell-limit-clean}
 m_r=\int_0^1x^rp(x)\Differential x
 =\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{1}{(r+1)n_N^{r}}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}
 \bigl((k+1)^{r+1}-k^{r+1}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Every finite approximant is rational.

\section{Haar and Faber--Schauder representations}
\label{p3:sec:haar-schauder}

\subsection{Rational unnormalized Haar increments}

Let
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:eta-haar}
 \eta_{n,k}
 =\IndicatorOf{I_{n+1,2k}}-\IndicatorOf{I_{n+1,2k+1}},
 \qquad 0\le k<2^n,
\end{equation}
and let $h_{n,k}=2^{n/2}\eta_{n,k}$ be the orthonormal Haar functions.
Define
\begin{align}
 \gamma_{n,k}
 &=2^n\bigl(\Delta_{n+1,2k}-\Delta_{n+1,2k+1}\bigr)\in\RationalNumbers,
 \label{p3:eq:gamma-rational}\\
 c_{n,k}
 &=\langle p,h_{n,k}\rangle
 =2^{n/2}\bigl(\Delta_{n+1,2k}-\Delta_{n+1,2k+1}\bigr)
 =2^{-n/2}\gamma_{n,k}.
 \label{p3:eq:c-orthonormal}
\end{align}
Thus the coefficients of the unnormalized Haar representation are rational,
while the orthonormal coefficients lie in $2^{n/2}\RationalNumbers$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact Haar expansion]
\label{p3:thm:Haar-expansion}
For every $N\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pN-Haar}
 p_N(x)=1+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 \gamma_{n,k}\eta_{n,k}(x)
 =1+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 c_{n,k}h_{n,k}(x).
\end{equation}
The power-of-two partial sums converge uniformly and in every finite $L^q$:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:p-Haar-limit}
 p(x)=1+\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}\gamma_{n,k}\eta_{n,k}(x).
\end{equation}
Reflection gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Haar-antisymmetry}
 \gamma_{n,2^n-1-k}=-\gamma_{n,k},
 \qquad
 c_{n,2^n-1-k}=-c_{n,k}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The difference $p_{n+1}-p_n$ is constant with opposite signs on the two
children of each parent cell.  On the left child it equals
$2^n(\Delta_{n+1,2k}-\Delta_{n+1,2k+1})=\gamma_{n,k}$,
so $p_{n+1}-p_n=\sum_k\gamma_{n,k}\eta_{n,k}$.  Telescoping from
$p_0\equiv1$ proves \eqref{p3:eq:pN-Haar}.  Convergence is
\cref{p3:thm:martingale-convergence}; antisymmetry follows from
\eqref{p3:eq:mass-symmetry}.
\end{proof}

The coefficients can also be read directly from three dyadic values:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:c-three-values}
 c_{n,k}=2^{n/2}\left[
  2F\!\left(\frac{2k+1}{2^{n+1}}\right)
  -F\!\left(\frac{k}{2^n}\right)
  -F\!\left(\frac{k+1}{2^n}\right)
 \right].
\end{equation}
This is a discrete second derivative of $F$ at a dyadic midpoint.

\subsection{Fourier image of the Haar expansion}

A direct integration gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Haar-transform}
 \FourierAngular{h_{n,k}}(z)
 =2^{n/2}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit zk/2^n}
 \frac{(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2^{n+1}})^2}{\ImaginaryUnit z}.
\end{equation}
Therefore:

\begin{theorem}[Haar product-series transform]
\label{p3:thm:Haar-transform-series}
Locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:p-Haar-transform}
 \FourierAngular{p}(z)=\frac{1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z}}{\ImaginaryUnit z}
 +\sum_{n=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 c_{n,k}2^{n/2}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit zk/2^n}
 \frac{(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2^{n+1}})^2}{\ImaginaryUnit z}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Phi-Haar-transform}
 \Phi(t)=\SincRad(t)
 +\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}\sum_{n=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 c_{n,k}2^{n/2}\EulerE^{-2\ImaginaryUnit tk/2^n}
 \frac{(1-\EulerE^{-2\ImaginaryUnit t/2^{n+1}})^2}{2\ImaginaryUnit t}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Transform the finite identity \eqref{p3:eq:pN-Haar} and let $N\to\infty$.
Local uniform convergence follows from uniform $L^1$ convergence of $p_N$ to
$p$.  Absolute convergence by scale also follows directly: smoothness gives
$c_{n,k}=\BigO(2^{-3n/2})$ uniformly in $k$, while
$\FourierAngular{h_{n,k}}(z)=\BigO_K(2^{-3n/2})$ on a compact $K$; summing $2^n$
locations leaves a convergent $\BigO_K(4^{-n})$ scale contribution.
\end{proof}

The factor $(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2^{n+1}})^2$ is the frequency signature of one
vanishing moment.  In \cref{p3:sec:walsh} it will be replaced by a product with as
many sine factors as there are nonzero binary digits in the Walsh index.

\subsection{Faber--Schauder series of the Fabius function}

Let $\psi_{n,k}$ be the peak-one tent supported on $I_{n,k}$, with apex at
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Schauder-midpoint}
 m_{n,k}=\frac{2k+1}{2^{n+1}}.
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{align}
 d_{n,k}
 &=F(m_{n,k})-\frac12F(k2^{-n})-\frac12F((k+1)2^{-n})
 \label{p3:eq:d-Schauder-def}\\
 &=\frac12\bigl(\Delta_{n+1,2k}-\Delta_{n+1,2k+1}\bigr)
 =2^{-n/2-1}c_{n,k}\in\RationalNumbers.
 \label{p3:eq:d-mass}
\end{align}

\begin{theorem}[Rational Faber--Schauder representation]
\label{p3:thm:Faber-Schauder-F}
The piecewise-linear interpolant $L_NF$ through the dyadic values
$F(k2^{-N})$ is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:LN-F-Schauder}
 L_NF(x)=x+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 d_{n,k}\psi_{n,k}(x).
\end{equation}
It converges uniformly to $F$, hence
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:F-Schauder-series}
 F(x)=x+\sum_{n=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 d_{n,k}\psi_{n,k}(x),
\end{equation}
where the series is understood by complete dyadic levels.  The coefficients are
rational and obey
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:d-antisymmetry}
 d_{n,2^n-1-k}=-d_{n,k}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:F-interpolation-error}
 \|F-L_NF\|_\infty
 \le\frac{\|F''\|_\infty}{8}\,4^{-N}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The classical Faber--Schauder construction says that the coefficient added at a
new midpoint is its value minus the chord average, giving
\eqref{p3:eq:d-Schauder-def}.  Equation \eqref{p3:eq:d-mass} follows by expanding the
two child masses.  Uniform convergence follows from continuity; the displayed
error is the standard linear-interpolation bound on intervals of length
$2^{-N}$.  Symmetry follows from $F(1-x)=1-F(x)$.
\end{proof}

Because $F$ is convex on $[0,1/2]$ and concave on $[1/2,1]$, one also has
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:d-sign}
 d_{n,k}<0\quad(0\le k<2^{n-1}),
 \qquad
 d_{n,k}>0\quad(2^{n-1}\le k<2^n),
\end{equation}
with the $n=0$ coefficient zero by symmetry.

\subsection[A sinc-squared series for the Fourier image of F]{A sinc-squared series for the Fourier image of $F$}

The transform of a peak-one tent of width $2^{-n}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:tent-transform}
 \int_0^1\psi_{n,k}(x)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit zx}\Differential x
 =\frac{2^{-n}}{2}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z m_{n,k}}
 \SincRad^2\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{n+2}}\right).
\end{equation}
Since
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:x-transform}
 \int_0^1x\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit zx}\Differential x
 =\frac{\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z}(1+\ImaginaryUnit z)-1}{z^2},
\end{equation}
we obtain a representation of the Fourier image of the Fabius function itself.

\begin{corollary}[Schauder--sinc representation of $\mathscr F_F$]
\label{p3:cor:Fhat-Schauder}
Locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Fhat-Schauder}
 \mathscr F_F(z)
 =\frac{\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z}(1+\ImaginaryUnit z)-1}{z^2}
 +\sum_{n=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 d_{n,k}\,2^{-n-1}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z m_{n,k}}
 \SincRad^2\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{n+2}}\right).
\end{equation}
All coefficients $d_{n,k}$ are rational.  Equation
\eqref{p3:eq:Fhat-Schauder} is identical, after summation, to the compact product
formula \eqref{p3:eq:F-transform-product}.
\end{corollary}

The identity equates a single infinite sinc product to a doubly indexed sum of
translated $\SincRad^2$ atoms.  It is a time--frequency multiresolution counterpart
of the canonical zero product \eqref{p3:eq:zero-product}.

\section{Walsh--Hadamard and Thue--Morse product representations}
\label{p3:sec:walsh}

\subsection{Paley Walsh functions and the finite Hadamard transform}

Let
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Rademacher}
 r_j(x)=(-1)^{\Floor{2^{j+1}x}},
 \qquad j\ge0,
\end{equation}
away from dyadic endpoints.  If
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:m-bits}
 m=\sum_{j\ge0}b_j2^j,
 \qquad b_j\in\{0,1\},
\end{equation}
then the Paley Walsh function is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Walsh-def}
 w_m(x)=\prod_{j\ge0}r_j(x)^{b_j}.
\end{equation}
Write
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:weight-sigma}
 r(m)=\BinaryDigitSum(m)=\sum_jb_j,
 \qquad
 \sigma(m)=\sum_jj b_j.
\end{equation}
The top mode $w_{2^N-1}$ on a depth-$N$ partition is the signed
Thue--Morse step function.

Define the Walsh coefficient of the Fabius density by
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:a_m-def}
 a_m=\int_0^1p(x)w_m(x)\Differential x.
\end{equation}
If $m<n_N$, then $w_m$ is constant on every depth-$N$ cell.  With
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:epsilon-mNk}
 \varepsilon_{m,N}(k)=w_m\!\left(\frac{k+1/2}{n_N}\right)\in\{\pm1\},
\end{equation}
we have the exact finite formula
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:a-Hadamard-masses}
 a_m=\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\varepsilon_{m,N}(k)\Delta_{N,k}.
\end{equation}
Thus every $a_m$ is rational.

Let $H_N=(\varepsilon_{m,N}(k))_{0\le m,k<n_N}$.  In vector notation,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Hadamard-pair}
 a^{(N)}=H_N\Delta_N,
 \qquad
 \Delta_N=n_N^{-1}H_Na^{(N)},
 \qquad H_NH_N^{\mathsf T}=n_NI.
\end{equation}
This is an exact rational Walsh--Hadamard transform of the dyadic mass vector.

\begin{theorem}[Walsh expansion and finite Parseval]
\label{p3:thm:Walsh-expansion}
For every $N$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pN-Walsh}
 p_N(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{n_N-1}a_mw_m(x).
\end{equation}
Consequently the power-of-two Walsh partial sums converge uniformly:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:p-Walsh}
 p(x)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{n_N-1}a_mw_m(x).
\end{equation}
At each finite level,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Walsh-Parseval-finite}
 \sum_{m=0}^{n_N-1}a_m^2
 =n_N\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}^2
 =\|p_N\|_2^2.
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Walsh-Parseval-limit}
 \|p\|_2^2=\sum_{m=0}^\infty a_m^2,
 \qquad
 \|\RvachevUp\|_2^2=\frac12\sum_{m=0}^\infty a_m^2.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On a depth-$N$ cell, \eqref{p3:eq:pN-Walsh} is exactly the inverse Hadamard
formula in \eqref{p3:eq:Hadamard-pair}.  Uniform convergence is
\cref{p3:thm:martingale-convergence}.  Parseval follows from orthonormality of the
Walsh functions or directly from $H_NH_N^{\mathsf T}=n_NI$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Reflection selection rules}

Binary reflection complements every Rademacher sign, modulo a null set:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Walsh-reflection}
 w_m(1-x)=(-1)^{r(m)}w_m(x).
\end{equation}
Since $p(1-x)=p(x)$, this gives a sharp parity filter.

\begin{proposition}[Hamming-parity selection]
\label{p3:prop:Walsh-selection}
The density coefficients satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:a-odd-vanish}
 a_m=0\qquad\text{whenever }r(m)\text{ is odd}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:b_m-F}
 b_m=\int_0^1\left(F(x)-\frac12\right)w_m(x)\Differential x,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:b-even-vanish}
 b_m=0\qquad\text{whenever }r(m)\text{ is even}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $x\mapsto1-x$.  For $a_m$ the integral is multiplied by
$(-1)^{r(m)}$; for $b_m$, the antisymmetry
$F(1-x)-1/2=-(F(x)-1/2)$ contributes one additional minus sign.
\end{proof}

Thus the Walsh support of the density occupies only even Hamming layers, while
the centered distribution function occupies only odd layers.  This is a
digital analogue of the disappearance of odd ordinary Fourier and polynomial
moments under reflection symmetry.

\subsection{Finite-product Fourier transform of a Walsh mode}

Set
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Wm-def}
 W_m(z)=\int_0^1w_m(x)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit zx}\Differential x.
\end{equation}
For $m>0$, let
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Nm-def}
 N(m)=1+\Floor{\log_2m}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Walsh finite-product transform]
\label{p3:thm:Walsh-product-transform}
For $m=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:W0}
 W_0(z)=\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2}\frac{2\sin(z/2)}{z}.
\end{equation}
For $m>0$, with $N=N(m)$ and $r=r(m)$,
\begin{align}
 W_m(z)
 &=\frac{1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2^N}}{\ImaginaryUnit z}
   \prod_{j=0}^{N-1}
   \left(1+(-1)^{b_j}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2^{j+1}}\right)
 \label{p3:eq:Wm-exponential-product}\\
 &=\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2}\frac{2^{N+1}\ImaginaryUnit^r}{z}
   \sin\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{N+1}}\right)
   \prod_{b_j=0}\cos\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{j+2}}\right)
   \prod_{b_j=1}\sin\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{j+2}}\right).
 \label{p3:eq:Wm-trig-product}
\end{align}
Both right sides have removable singularities and define the same entire
function.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At depth $N$, integrate the constant value of $w_m$ on each cell.  The common
cell integral is $(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2^N})/(\ImaginaryUnit z)$, and the signed geometric sum
factorizes according to the binary digits of the cell index:
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{2^N-1}\varepsilon_{m,N}(k)q^k
 =\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(1+(-1)^{b_j}q^{2^{N-1-j}}\right),
 \qquad q=\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2^N}.
\]
This proves \eqref{p3:eq:Wm-exponential-product}.  Pairing exponentials into sines
and cosines gives \eqref{p3:eq:Wm-trig-product}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A series of finite products for the infinite sinc product}

Transforming the Walsh partial sums gives a representation of $\Phi$ that is
qualitatively different from the canonical product: the coefficients encode
Fabius arithmetic, while each basis transform is a finite product.

\begin{theorem}[Walsh product-series for $\Phi$]
\label{p3:thm:Phi-Walsh-product-series}
Locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Phi-Walsh-basic}
 \Phi(t)=\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}\sum_{m=0}^\infty a_mW_m(2t),
\end{equation}
where the sum is taken through complete blocks $m<2^N$.  More explicitly,
\begin{align}
 \Phi(t)=\SincRad(t)
 +\sum_{\substack{m\ge1\\r(m)\ \mathrm{even}}}
 &a_m\frac{2^{N(m)}(-1)^{r(m)/2}}{t}
 \sin\!\left(\frac{t}{2^{N(m)}}\right)
 \label{p3:eq:Phi-Walsh-trig}\\[-0.3em]
 &\times
 \prod_{b_j=0}\cos\!\left(\frac{t}{2^{j+1}}\right)
 \prod_{b_j=1}\sin\!\left(\frac{t}{2^{j+1}}\right).
 \nonumber
\end{align}
Each coefficient $a_m$ is rational and is given exactly by
\eqref{p3:eq:a-Hadamard-masses}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $p_N\to p$ in $L^1$ and all functions are supported in $[0,1]$, their
Fourier transforms converge locally uniformly.  Substitute the finite Walsh
expansion \eqref{p3:eq:pN-Walsh} and use
$\Phi(t)=\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}\FourierAngular{p}(2t)$.  Equation
\eqref{p3:eq:Phi-Walsh-trig} follows from
\cref{p3:thm:Walsh-product-transform} and the parity filter
\eqref{p3:eq:a-odd-vanish}.
\end{proof}

The corresponding hyperbolic product-series is obtained by $t\mapsto\ImaginaryUnit z$:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:M-Walsh}
 M(z)=\EulerE^{-z}\sum_{m=0}^\infty a_mW_m(2\ImaginaryUnit z),
\end{equation}
so each term becomes a finite product of $\sinh$ and $\cosh$ factors.
Equations \eqref{p3:eq:Phi-sinc-product} and \eqref{p3:eq:Phi-Walsh-trig} therefore
give a product-versus-product-series duality for the same entire function.

\subsection{Exact Hamming-weight cancellation}

The number of sine factors in \eqref{p3:eq:Wm-trig-product} is not cosmetic: it is
the exact number of vanishing algebraic moments.

\begin{theorem}[Walsh cancellation order and first moment]
\label{p3:thm:Walsh-moment-cancellation}
For $m>0$, put $r=r(m)$ and $\sigma=\sigma(m)$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Walsh-vanishing-moments}
 \int_0^1x^qw_m(x)\Differential x=0,
 \qquad 0\le q<r,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Walsh-first-moment}
 \int_0^1x^rw_m(x)\Differential x
 =(-1)^rr!\,2^{-2r-\sigma}.
\end{equation}
Thus $W_m$ has a zero of exact order $r(m)$ at the origin.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Extract the small factors from \eqref{p3:eq:Wm-trig-product}.  One obtains
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Wm-factorized-origin}
 W_m(z)=\ImaginaryUnit^r2^{-2r-\sigma}z^rR_m(z),
 \qquad R_m(0)=1,
\end{equation}
where $R_m$ is entire.  On the other hand,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Wm-moment-Taylor}
 W_m(z)=\sum_{q=0}^\infty\frac{(-\ImaginaryUnit z)^q}{q!}
 \int_0^1x^qw_m(x)\Differential x.
\end{equation}
Comparing the first nonzero Taylor coefficient proves both statements.
\end{proof}

For the top Thue--Morse mode, $m=2^N-1$, one has $r=N$ and
$\sigma=N(N-1)/2$.  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Thue-Morse-moment}
 \int_0^1x^qw_{2^N-1}(x)\Differential x=0\quad(q<N),
 \qquad
 \int_0^1x^Nw_{2^N-1}(x)\Differential x
 =(-1)^NN!2^{-N(N+3)/2}.
\end{equation}
This is the continuous, normalized Prouhet cancellation attached to the
Thue--Morse sign pattern.

\subsection{The Gaussian-binomial cancellation spectrum}

The first nonzero moments aggregate exactly by Hamming weight.

\begin{theorem}[$q=1/2$ Gaussian-binomial aggregation]
\label{p3:thm:qbinom-Walsh}
For $0\le r\le N$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:sigma-qbinom}
 \sum_{\substack{0\le m<2^N\\r(m)=r}}2^{-\sigma(m)}
 =2^{-r(r-1)/2}\GaussianBinomial{N}{r}{1/2}.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:first-moment-qbinom}
 \sum_{\substack{0\le m<2^N\\r(m)=r}}
 \int_0^1x^rw_m(x)\Differential x
 =(-1)^rr!\,2^{-2r-r(r-1)/2}\GaussianBinomial{N}{r}{1/2}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The finite $q$-binomial theorem gives
\[
 \prod_{j=0}^{N-1}(1+y2^{-j})
 =\sum_{r=0}^N2^{-r(r-1)/2}\GaussianBinomial{N}{r}{1/2}y^r.
\]
The coefficient of $y^r$ on the left is the sum of $2^{-\sigma(m)}$ over
all subsets of $r$ bit positions.  Combine with
\eqref{p3:eq:Walsh-first-moment}.
\end{proof}

The appearance of $q=1/2$, rather than the $q=1/4$ common in the even-moment
and scale-energy theory, has a simple origin: it records bit \emph{positions},
not squared dyadic lengths.  Squaring amplitudes or passing to even cumulants
naturally changes $1/2$ to $1/4$.

\subsection{All higher Walsh moments: a Bell--Bernoulli formula}

The product formula encodes every moment, not only the first nonzero one.
Define the even entire factor
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Em-def}
 E_m(z)=
 \SincRad\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{N+1}}\right)
 \prod_{b_j=0}\cos\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{j+2}}\right)
 \prod_{b_j=1}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{z}{2^{j+2}}\right),
\end{equation}
where $N=N(m)$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:R-m-E}
 R_m(z)=\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2}E_m(z)
\end{equation}
in \eqref{p3:eq:Wm-factorized-origin}.  Write
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:E-coefficients}
 E_m(z)=\sum_{j=0}^\infty e_{m,j}z^{2j}.
\end{equation}

For $q\ge1$, set
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:lambda-mq}
 \lambda_{2q}(m)
 =-\frac{2^{2q-1}|B_{2q}|}{q}
 \left[
  2^{-2q(N+1)}
  +\sum_{b_j=1}2^{-2q(j+2)}
  +(2^{2q}-1)\sum_{b_j=0}2^{-2q(j+2)}
 \right].
\end{equation}
The classical expansions of $\log\SincRad$ and $\log\cos$ show that
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:log-Em}
 \log E_m(z)=\sum_{q=1}^\infty
 \lambda_{2q}(m)\frac{z^{2q}}{(2q)!}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:e-Bell}
 e_{m,j}=\frac1{(2j)!}
 Y_{2j}\bigl(0,\lambda_2(m),0,\lambda_4(m),\ldots,
              \lambda_{2j}(m)\bigr).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Bell--Bernoulli formula for Walsh moments]
\label{p3:thm:Walsh-Bell-moments}
Let $m>0$, $r=r(m)$, and $\ell\ge0$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Walsh-higher-moments}
 \int_0^1x^{r+\ell}w_m(x)\Differential x
 =(-1)^r\frac{(r+\ell)!}{2^{2r+\sigma(m)}}
 \sum_{j=0}^{\Floor{\ell/2}}
 \frac{(-1)^je_{m,j}}
      {2^{\ell-2j}(\ell-2j)!},
\end{equation}
with $e_{m,j}$ given explicitly by \eqref{p3:eq:e-Bell} and
\eqref{p3:eq:lambda-mq}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use
$W_m(z)=\ImaginaryUnit^r2^{-2r-\sigma}z^r\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z/2}E_m(z)$,
expand the exponential and the even series \eqref{p3:eq:E-coefficients}, and
compare with \eqref{p3:eq:Wm-moment-Taylor}.  Formula \eqref{p3:eq:e-Bell} is the
standard exponential Bell-polynomial coefficient formula applied to
\eqref{p3:eq:log-Em}.
\end{proof}

This theorem places the Bernoulli numbers in the \emph{basis moments}, while
the rational coefficients $a_m$ carry the Fabius-specific arithmetic.  The
ordinary Fabius moments can therefore be reconstructed as
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Fabius-moment-Walsh}
 m_q=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \sum_{m=0}^{2^N-1}a_m\int_0^1x^qw_m(x)\Differential x.
\end{equation}
Only modes with even $r(m)\le q$ contribute.  For example, since
$m_2=5/18$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:weight-two-sum-rule}
 \sum_{\substack{m\ge1\\r(m)=2}}
 2^{-\sigma(m)}a_m=-\frac49.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the constant Walsh mode contributes $\int_0^1x^2\Differential x=1/3$, and
\eqref{p3:eq:Walsh-first-moment} contributes
$2^{-3-\sigma(m)}a_m$ from every weight-two mode.

\begin{remark}
Equation \eqref{p3:eq:Fabius-moment-Walsh} is naturally ordered by dyadic blocks,
not by arbitrary rearrangement.  Blockwise convergence follows immediately
from uniform convergence of the Walsh conditional expectations.  Establishing
absolute convergence after regrouping by Hamming weight is a separate and
interesting coefficient-decay problem.
\end{remark}

\section{Bernstein polynomials and positive beta-mixture limits}
\label{p3:sec:bernstein}

\subsection{Rational Bernstein approximants}

For $n=n_N=2^N$, define the Bernstein polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Bernstein-F}
 \mathcal B_NF(x)
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}F(k/n)\binom nkx^k(1-x)^{n-k}.
\end{equation}
Every coefficient in the Bernstein basis is a rational dyadic Fabius value.
The power-basis form is also rational:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Bernstein-power-basis}
 \mathcal B_NF(x)
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom nj
 \left(\sum_{k=0}^j(-1)^{j-k}\binom jkF(k/n)\right)x^j.
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:F-Bernstein-limit}
 F(x)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal B_NF(x)
\end{equation}
uniformly, as a limit of explicit rational polynomials.

The dyadic Faber interpolants of \cref{p3:sec:haar-schauder} converge at order
$4^{-N}$ because they exploit the $C^2$ smoothness on a mesh of width $2^{-N}$.
The Bernstein approximants use a binomial cloud of width $n^{-1/2}$ and have the
classical expansion
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Voronovskaya}
 \mathcal B_NF(x)-F(x)
 =\frac{x(1-x)}{2n}F''(x)+\BigO(n^{-2})
\end{equation}
under the available smoothness.  Their advantage is positivity and the
probabilistic mixture structure of the derivative.

\subsection{Derivative as a beta-density mixture}

Differentiating \eqref{p3:eq:Bernstein-F} gives
\begin{align}
 p_N^{\mathrm{B}}(x)
 &:=(\mathcal B_NF)'(x)\\
 &=n\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\Delta_{N,k}
   \binom{n-1}{k}x^k(1-x)^{n-1-k}\\
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\Delta_{N,k}
   \frac{x^k(1-x)^{n-1-k}}{B(k+1,n-k)}.
 \label{p3:eq:pB-beta-density}
\end{align}

\begin{theorem}[Positive rational beta-mixture representation]
\label{p3:thm:beta-mixture}
The functions $p_N^{\mathrm B}$ are probability densities and are finite
mixtures of $\mathrm{Beta}(k+1,n-k)$ densities with the exact rational weights
$\Delta_{N,k}$.  Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pB-uniform}
 p_N^{\mathrm B}\longrightarrow p
 \quad\text{uniformly on }[0,1].
\end{equation}
Consequently $p$ is a uniform limit of positive polynomial densities whose
mixture weights are exactly the dyadic Fabius masses.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Formula \eqref{p3:eq:pB-beta-density} proves positivity and unit mass.  The
classical derivative theorem for Bernstein polynomials states that
$(\mathcal B_n f)'\to f'$ uniformly when $f\in C^1[0,1]$.
\end{proof}

This representation is structurally different from the uniform-cell mixture:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
model & component family & weights\\
\midrule
$p_N$ & disjoint equal-width uniforms & $\Delta_{N,k}$\\
$p_N^{\mathrm B}$ & overlapping beta densities & $\Delta_{N,k}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The same rational vector is therefore a mixing law for both a local and a
global positive basis.

\subsection{Hypergeometric transform representations}

The moment-generating function of a $\mathrm{Beta}(a,b)$ variable is
${}_1F_1(a;a+b;z)$.  Hence:

\begin{theorem}[Confluent-hypergeometric mixture limits]
\label{p3:thm:hypergeometric-mixture}
Locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1p(x)\EulerE^{zx}\Differential x
 &=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \Delta_{N,k}\,{}_1F_1(k+1;n_N+1;z),
 \label{p3:eq:p-MGF-beta}\\
 \Phi(t)
 &=\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \Delta_{N,k}\,{}_1F_1(k+1;n_N+1;-2\ImaginaryUnit t),
 \label{p3:eq:Phi-beta}\\
 M(z)
 &=\EulerE^{-z}\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \Delta_{N,k}\,{}_1F_1(k+1;n_N+1;2z).
 \label{p3:eq:M-beta}
\end{align}
Thus the infinite sinc and hyperbolic-sinc products are limits of positive
rational mixtures of confluent hypergeometric functions.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Integrate \eqref{p3:eq:pB-beta-density} against $\EulerE^{zx}$ and use uniform
convergence.  The affine transform relations
$\Phi(t)=\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}\FourierAngular{p}(2t)$ and
$M(z)=\EulerE^{-z}\int_0^1p(x)\EulerE^{2zx}\Differential x$ give the remaining formulas.
\end{proof}

The beta moments give another rational moment limit:
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:moment-beta-limit}
 m_r=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\Delta_{N,k}
 \frac{(k+1)_r}{(n_N+1)_r},
 \qquad r\in\NaturalNumbers,
\end{equation}
where $(a)_r$ is the rising factorial.  Comparison of
\eqref{p3:eq:moment-beta-limit} with the cell formula
\eqref{p3:eq:moment-cell-limit-clean} gives two positive rational quadrature
schemes for the same exact Fabius moments.

\section{Dual quantile cells and equal-weight sinc mixtures}
\label{p3:sec:quantile-cells}

\subsection{The inverse dyadic width array}

The primal mass array fixes spatial widths and varies probabilities.  The
inverse construction fixes probabilities and varies spatial widths.  Put
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:gNk-qNk}
 g_{N,k}=G(k/n_N),
 \qquad
 q_{N,k}=g_{N,k+1}-g_{N,k}>0,
 \qquad 0\le k<n_N.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}q_{N,k}&=1,\label{p3:eq:q-sum}\\
 q_{N,n_N-1-k}&=q_{N,k},\label{p3:eq:q-symmetry}\\
 q_{N,k}&=q_{N+1,2k}+q_{N+1,2k+1}.
 \label{p3:eq:q-projective}
\end{align}
Unlike $\Delta_{N,k}$, these widths are not expected to be rational in general.
They are exact inverse-Fabius data.

Let $G_N=L_NG$ be the piecewise-linear interpolant through the points
$(k/n_N,g_{N,k})$.  Pushing uniform measure forward by $G_N$ gives the density
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:pQN}
 p_N^{\mathrm Q}(x)
 =\frac{1}{n_Nq_{N,k}},
 \qquad g_{N,k}<x<g_{N,k+1}.
\end{equation}
Each interval has probability $1/n_N$.  Thus $p_N^{\mathrm Q}$ is a finite
mixture of variable-width uniforms with \emph{equal} weights.

\begin{resultboxPartl}[Primal--dual cell dictionary]
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
 & spatial interval & probability weight\\
\midrule
primal Fabius cells & fixed width $1/n_N$ & variable $\Delta_{N,k}$\\
dual quantile cells & variable width $q_{N,k}$ & fixed $1/n_N$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The two projective arrays are interchanged by $F$ and $G$: the first samples
increments of $F$ on a uniform spatial grid, the second samples increments of
$G$ on a uniform probability grid.
\end{resultboxPartl}

\subsection{Exact chord integration and the Fourier image}

On the $k$th probability cell, $G_N$ moves linearly from $g_{N,k}$ to
$g_{N,k+1}$.  Integrating an exponential along that chord gives a sinc factor.

\begin{theorem}[Inverse-quantile chord representation]
\label{p3:thm:quantile-chord}
Locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \Phi(t)
 &=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1{n_N}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t(g_{N,k}+g_{N,k+1}-1)}
 \SincRad\!\left(tq_{N,k}\right),
 \label{p3:eq:Phi-quantile-chord}\\
 M(z)
 &=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1{n_N}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \EulerE^{z(g_{N,k}+g_{N,k+1}-1)}
 \frac{\sinh(zq_{N,k})}{zq_{N,k}}.
 \label{p3:eq:M-quantile-chord}
\end{align}
Equivalently, $\Phi$ is the locally uniform limit of the Fourier transforms of
finite equal-weight uniform mixtures on the centered intervals
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:centered-quantile-intervals}
 [2g_{N,k}-1,\,2g_{N,k+1}-1].
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Uniform continuity of $G$ gives $G_N\to G$ uniformly.  Therefore
\[
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t(2G_N(u)-1)}\Differential u
 \longrightarrow
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t(2G(u)-1)}\Differential u=\Phi(t)
\]
locally uniformly in $t$.  On a probability cell of length $1/n_N$, direct
integration of the affine phase gives
\[
 \frac1{n_N}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t(g_{N,k}+g_{N,k+1}-1)}
 \SincRad(tq_{N,k}).
\]
Summing proves \eqref{p3:eq:Phi-quantile-chord}; Wick rotation gives
\eqref{p3:eq:M-quantile-chord}.
\end{proof}

Removing the chord average gives the simpler midpoint Riemann representation
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Phi-quantile-midpoint}
 \Phi(t)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1{n_N}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \exp\!\left[-\ImaginaryUnit t\left(2G\!\left(\frac{k+1/2}{n_N}\right)-1\right)\right].
\end{equation}
The chord formula is preferable when inverse values at endpoints are already
available, because it integrates the linear interpolant exactly.

\subsection{Moment and Laplace limits from inverse dyadic values}

For the uncentered law, exact integration of powers along each chord yields:

\begin{corollary}[Inverse-dyadic moment polygons]
\label{p3:cor:inverse-moment-polygons}
For every integer $r\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 m_r
 &=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1{n_N(r+1)}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \frac{g_{N,k+1}^{r+1}-g_{N,k}^{r+1}}
      {g_{N,k+1}-g_{N,k}}
 \label{p3:eq:moment-quantile-chord}\\
 &=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1{n_N(r+1)}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}\sum_{j=0}^r
 g_{N,k}^{j}g_{N,k+1}^{r-j}.
 \label{p3:eq:moment-quantile-homogeneous}
\end{align}
For arbitrary $z\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:p-MGF-quantile-chord}
 \int_0^1p(x)\EulerE^{zx}\Differential x
 =\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1{n_N}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}
 \EulerE^{z(g_{N,k}+g_{N,k+1})/2}
 \sinhc\!\left(\frac{zq_{N,k}}2\right),
\end{equation}
where $\sinhc w=\sinh(w)/w$.
\end{corollary}

The first line is a limit of complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials in
adjacent inverse-dyadic values.  It complements the repository's local inverse
series and exact inverse values at special dyadic levels: local inverse
aithmetic enters a global moment formula through a simple polygonal limit.

\subsection[Dual Lq spectra and entropy constants]{Dual $L^q$ spectra and entropy constants}

Because $q_{N,k}=\int_{k/n_N}^{(k+1)/n_N}G'(u)\Differential u$, the slope
$n_Nq_{N,k}$ is the dyadic conditional expectation of $G'$.  This gives a
discrete counterpart of the inverse-derivative norm threshold.

\begin{theorem}[Primal--dual R\'enyi spectra]
\label{p3:thm:dual-Renyi}
For $s>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:primal-Renyi}
 n_N^{s-1}\sum_k\Delta_{N,k}^s
 \longrightarrow\int_0^1p(x)^s\Differential x.
\end{equation}
For $0<s<1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:dual-Renyi}
 n_N^{s-1}\sum_kq_{N,k}^s
 \longrightarrow\int_0^1G'(u)^s\Differential u
 =\int_0^1p(x)^{1-s}\Differential x.
\end{equation}
At $s=1$ both normalized sums equal one.  For $s>1$, the dual normalized
sum tends to $+\infty$, consistently with $G'\notin L^s(0,1)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The primal statement is \eqref{p3:eq:Lq-mass-limit}.  On each probability cell,
$n_Nq_{N,k}=\Expectation[G'\mid\mathcal D_N]$.  The dyadic martingale converges to $G'$
in $L^1$.  For $0<s<1$, the inequality
$|a^s-b^s|\le|a-b|^s$ and H\"older's inequality give convergence of the
$s$th powers in $L^1$.  The change of variables $u=F(x)$ gives
\[
 \int_0^1G'(u)^s\Differential u
 =\int_0^1p(x)^{1-s}\Differential x.
\]
For $s>1$, conditional-expectation $L^s$ norms increase to the infinite
$L^s$ norm of $G'$.
\end{proof}

The one-sided derivatives at $s=1$ give entropy constants.  In addition to
\eqref{p3:eq:mass-entropy}, the endpoint asymptotics imply $\log p\in L^1(0,1)$,
and
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:quantile-entropy}
 -\sum_{k=0}^{n_N-1}q_{N,k}\log q_{N,k}
 =\log n_N+\int_0^1\log p(x)\Differential x+o(1).
\end{equation}
Thus the two exact partitions retain different continuous entropies:
\begin{align}
 H(\Delta_N)-\log n_N&\longrightarrow-\int p\log p,
 \label{p3:eq:entropy-primal-constant}\\
 H(q_N)-\log n_N&\longrightarrow\int\log p.
 \label{p3:eq:entropy-dual-constant}
\end{align}
The second constant is sensitive to endpoint flatness, whereas the first is
weighted by the probability density itself.

\section[Haar scale energies and the sinc-fourth-power continuum expansion]{Haar scale energies and the $\SincRad^4$ continuum expansion}
\label{p3:sec:haar-energy}

\subsection{Three exact forms of the energy}

Define the detail energy
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:En-def}
 E_n=\|p_{n+1}-p_n\|_2^2
 =\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}c_{n,k}^2.
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{p3:eq:c-orthonormal},
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:En-masses}
 E_n=2^n\sum_{k=0}^{2^n-1}
 \bigl(\Delta_{n+1,2k}-\Delta_{n+1,2k+1}\bigr)^2\in\RationalNumbers.
\end{equation}
Thus a sum of squares of generally quadratic Haar coefficients is rational.
Orthogonality gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:L2-energy-telescope}
 \|p_N\|_2^2=1+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}E_n,
 \qquad
 \|p\|_2^2=1+\sum_{n=0}^\infty E_n.
\end{equation}

The Haar and Walsh filtrations are the same nested dyadic subspaces.  Hence:

\begin{proposition}[Haar--Walsh shell identity]
\label{p3:prop:Haar-Walsh-shell}
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Haar-Walsh-energy}
 E_n=\sum_{m=2^n}^{2^{n+1}-1}a_m^2.
\end{equation}
Only even-Hamming-weight indices contribute to the right side.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Both sides are the squared norm of the orthogonal projection of $p$ onto
$V_{n+1}\ominus V_n$, where $V_n$ is the space of functions constant on
depth-$n$ dyadic cells.  The Haar basis organizes this complement by location;
the Walsh basis organizes it by sequency.
\end{proof}

Thus the same rational $E_n$ is simultaneously a local curvature statistic of
$F$, a sibling-mass contrast, and a Walsh spectral-shell energy.

\subsection{Local Taylor expansion of a Haar coefficient}

Let $h=2^{-n}$ and let $m_{n,k}=(k+1/2)h$ be the center of a parent cell.
Flatness of $p$ at the endpoints permits uniform Taylor expansion to arbitrary
order.  Direct integration of odd Taylor terms gives
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:c-local-asymptotic}
 c_{n,k}\sim-
 \sum_{r=0}^\infty
 \frac{h^{2r+3/2}}{2^{2r+1}(2r+2)!}
 p^{(2r+1)}(m_{n,k}).
\end{equation}
The first terms are
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:c-local-first}
 c_{n,k}=-h^{3/2}\left[
 \frac{p'(m_{n,k})}{4}
 +\frac{h^2p'''(m_{n,k})}{192}
 +\frac{h^4p^{(5)}(m_{n,k})}{23040}
 +\BigO(h^6)
 \right].
\end{equation}
This explains the scale $E_n\asymp4^{-n}$ despite the infinite smoothness of
$p$: the Haar wavelet has only one vanishing moment.  Higher-order multiwavelets
would expose the superalgebraic smoothness more efficiently.

\subsection{A frequency multiplier and a superalgebraic alias remainder}

Let
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Kh-def}
 K_h(x)=h^{-1/2}
 \bigl(\IndicatorOf{[-h/2,0]}(x)-\IndicatorOf{[0,h/2]}(x)\bigr).
\end{equation}
Its squared Fourier modulus is
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:Kh-transform}
 |\FourierAngular{K_h}(\xi)|^2
 =\frac{16}{h\xi^2}\sin^4\!\left(\frac{h\xi}{4}\right).
\end{equation}
The coefficients $c_{n,k}$ are samples of a translate of $p*K_h$ at the cell
centers.  Poisson summation for the squared samples separates a zero alias from
all nonzero aliases.

\begin{theorem}[$\SincRad^4$ multiplier formula]
\label{p3:thm:sinc4-energy}
For $h=2^{-n}$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:En-multiplier}
 E_n=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\RealNumbers|\FourierAngular{p}(\xi)|^2
 \frac{h^2\xi^2}{16}\SincRad^4\!\left(\frac{h\xi}{4}\right)\Differential\xi
 +R(h),
\end{equation}
where for every $M>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{p3:eq:alias-superalgebraic}
 R(h)=\BigO_M(h^M)\qquad(h\downarrow0).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply Poisson summation to the samples of $|p*K_h|^2$ on the shifted lattice
$h(\IntegerNumbers+1/2)$.  The zero Fourier mode is
\[
 \frac1h\|p*K_h\|_2^2
 =\frac1{2\pi h}\int|\FourierAngular{p}(\xi)|^2
 |\FourierAngular{K_h}(\xi)|^2\Differential\xi,
\]
which simplifies to the integral in \eqref{p3:eq:En-multiplier}.  Every nonzero
alias contains a factor $\FourierAngular{p}(\xi+2\pi\ell/h)$ or its conjugate.
Because the zero extension of $p$ is $C_c^\infty(\RealNumbers)$,
$\FourierAngular{p}$ is Schwartz; the nonzero aliases are therefore smaller than every
power of $h$ after summation over $\ell\ne0$.
\end{proof}
