\part{Unit-Circle, Bessel, and Spectral--Monodromy Representations}
% Absorbed from rvachev_fabius_representations_2026/rvachev-fabius-representations.tex




\begin{abstract}
This report audits the mathematical \LaTeX{} corpus under
\url{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt/tree/main/Analysis/FabiusFunction/docs},
including its subdirectories, source inventories, historical snapshots, imported papers,
and living frontier reports. It first consolidates the repository's established
probabilistic, convolutional, spline, Fourier, product, moment, resolvent, continued-fraction,
multiresolution, integral-transform, and inverse-function representations. It then develops
a repo-relative new coordinate system based on the probability--Chebyshev moments
\[
 c_n=\int_{-1}^{1}T_n(x)\RvachevUp(x)\Differential x.
\]
These moments lift the Rvachev probability measure to a symmetric probability measure on the
unit circle. The lift yields an exact Carath\'eodory function, a Joukowski representation of
the Cauchy transform, weighted Chebyshev boundary series for \(\RvachevUp\), phase series for the
Fabius function and its inverse, rational Schur--Verblunsky parameters, a CMV resolvent,
Toeplitz determinants, and a Szeg\H{o} product.

Jacobi--Anger expansions produce entire Bessel-series identities equal to the infinite sinc
product and its canonical dyadic zero product, while modified Bessel series equal the
hyperbolic moment-generating product. A Laplace--Bessel--Joukowski chain unifies these
identities with the Cauchy transform. For the signed and unsigned Thue--Morse sequences, we
derive argument-principle formulas from the multiplicities of the integer zeros of the
Fourier image, local residue recurrences, a logarithmic-monodromy recovery formula for
finite blocks, and a smooth Abel--Poisson regularization of the Rademacher sign product.

Every theorem marked ``repo-relative new'' is proved here and was not located by exact-term
or structural search in the audited repository. This phrase is deliberately narrower than
a claim of worldwide priority. Conjectures and a research program are stated separately.
The accompanying Python program uses exact rational arithmetic and high-precision numerics
to verify the coefficient pipelines and analytic identities.
\end{abstract}

\begin{statusboxPartc}
The archive includes the full \LaTeX{} source, exact coefficient CSVs, generated tables,
three convergence/parameter figures, a commented Python program, a numerical verification
log, and a source-overlap ledger. The direct Schur algorithm and the independent Geronimus
reconstruction agree exactly through the checked orders; the spectral Thue--Morse formulas
pass for \(0\le n\le256\); and three independent Cauchy-transform evaluations agree to the
precision recorded in \cref{p7:sec:numerics}.
\end{statusboxPartc}


\clearpage

\section{Scope, conventions, and nonduplication protocol}
\label{p7:sec:scope}

\subsection{Normalized objects}

We use the Rvachev probability density
\[
 \rho(x):=\RvachevUp(x),\qquad \rho\ge0,\qquad
 \SupportOperator\rho=[-1,1],\qquad
 \int_{-1}^{1}\rho(x)\Differential x=1,
\]
which is even and \(C^\infty\), with every derivative flat at the endpoints. Its associated
random variable admits the convergent series
\begin{equation}
 X=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}U_k,
 \qquad U_k\stackrel{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\operatorname{Unif}[-1,1].
 \label{p7:eq:X-random-series}
\end{equation}
Set
\[
 Y=\frac{X+1}{2},\qquad
 F(u)=\Probability(Y\le u)=\int_{-1}^{2u-1}\rho(x)\Differential x,
 \qquad I(v)=F^{-1}(v).
\]
Thus, on \(0<u<1\),
\begin{equation}
 F'(u)=2\rho(2u-1).
 \label{p7:eq:Fprime-rho}
\end{equation}

The Fourier convention is
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(\xi)=\int_{-1}^{1}\rho(x)\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi x}\Differential x,
 \qquad
 \SincPi z=\frac{\sin(\pi z)}{\pi z},\quad \SincPi 0=1.
 \label{p7:eq:fourier-convention}
\end{equation}
The moment-generating and Cauchy transforms are
\begin{equation}
 M(t)=\Expectation\EulerE^{tX},
 \qquad
 R(z)=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\rho(x)}{z-x}\Differential x,
 \qquad z\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1].
 \label{p7:eq:M-R-defs}
\end{equation}
For the Thue--Morse sequence we use
\begin{equation}
 \epsilon_n=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)},\qquad
 \tau_n=\frac{1-\epsilon_n}{2}\in\{0,1\},
 \label{p7:eq:TM-conventions}
\end{equation}
where \(\BinaryDigitSum(n)\) is the sum of the binary digits of \(n\).

\subsection{Corpus audit}

The current repository manifest organizes the documentation into Fourier-decay,
Thue--Morse, exponent and \(q\)-series, spectral/arithmetic, integration and transform,
inverse/sampling, representation, and frontier campaigns. The repository's own pinned
corpus inventory enumerates sixty-seven source documents plus later living additions, while
the multiresolution report records a source-level audit of eighty-nine \LaTeX{} paths at
commit \texttt{924652f4a9a656fd957d2936b2f053747c6f6c47}. The consolidated frontier volume,
the current manifest, and the overlap-sensitive living reports were used jointly so that
historical duplicates did not masquerade as independent novelty.

The following families are already present and are therefore treated as baseline rather
than novelty: the infinite sinc product; the canonical zero product; finite box-convolution
splines; finite Thue--Morse truncated-power formulas; Cauchy and logarithmic resolvents;
moment/cumulant and Bell--Bernoulli formulas; transforms of \(F\) and \(F^{-1}\); digamma,
Hurwitz-zeta, and cotangent logarithmic derivatives; Fredholm determinants; Jacobi and
Stieltjes continued fractions; Haar, Schauder, Walsh, and beta-mixture expansions;
fractional Volterra integrals; Mellin/Newton moments; inverse endpoint asymptotics; the
half-integer sign formula; dyadic cardinal reproduction; and the non-holonomicity theorem
from unbounded dyadic zero multiplicities.

The exact terms and structures \emph{Carath\'eodory function, OPUC, Verblunsky coefficient,
CMV matrix, Joukowski transform, probability--Chebyshev moment, Bessel product-series,
argument-principle Thue--Morse formula}, and \emph{logarithmic-monodromy encoding} were not
found in the current consolidated volume or in the relevant living reports. In particular,
the multiresolution report's density-Chebyshev coefficients are different from the
probability-Chebyshev moments introduced in \cref{p7:sec:cheb}.

\begin{baselinebox}
The audit is documented in \texttt{corpus\_audit.txt}. Throughout the report, ``new'' means
``not located in the audited \repoPartc{} subtree.'' Standard external machinery---Jacobi--Anger
expansions, the Schur algorithm, Geronimus relations, Szeg\H{o}'s theorem, CMV matrices,
Shannon sampling, and the argument principle---is cited rather than claimed as new.
\end{baselinebox}

\subsection{Proof-status convention}

A theorem or proposition without qualification is proved in this report. A boxed
``repository baseline'' records known material used as input. Conjectures are numbered and
visually separated. Numerical evidence is never promoted to a proof; exact rational
calculations are identified as such.

\section{Established representation atlas}
\label{p7:sec:baseline}

This section compresses the repository's existing representation families into one
normalization. It is included both for completeness and to make the genuinely new bridges
in later sections transparent.

\subsection{Random series, convolutions, and refinement}

From \eqref{p7:eq:X-random-series}, \(X\) satisfies the distributional fixed point
\begin{equation}
 X\ \stackrel d=\ \frac{X+U}{2},
 \qquad U\sim\operatorname{Unif}[-1,1],\quad U\perp X.
 \label{p7:eq:distribution-fixed-point}
\end{equation}
Consequently \(\rho\) is an infinite convolution of normalized boxes,
\begin{equation}
 \rho=\underset{k\ge1}{*}\left(2^{k-1}\IndicatorOf{[-2^{-k},2^{-k}]}\right),
 \label{p7:eq:infinite-convolution}
\end{equation}
and the finite truncation \(X_N=\sum_{k=1}^{N}2^{-k}U_k\) has a compactly supported
polynomial-spline density \(\rho_N\). The refinement and differential identities can be
written
\begin{align}
 \rho(x)&=\int_{-1}^{1}\rho(2x-u)\Differential u,
 \label{p7:eq:rho-refinement}\\
 \rho'(x)&=2\bigl(\rho(2x+1)-\rho(2x-1)\bigr).
 \label{p7:eq:rho-differential}
\end{align}
They imply the familiar Fabius functional-differential relations after the affine change
\(x=2u-1\).

\subsection{Finite Thue--Morse splines}

Let
\[
 a_{N,j}=\frac{2^N-1-2j}{2^N},\qquad 0\le j<2^N.
\]
The finite convolution density has the exact truncated-power form
\begin{equation}
 \rho_N(x)=\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^N-1}\epsilon_j\,(x+a_{N,j})_+^{N-1}.
 \label{p7:eq:finite-spline}
\end{equation}
The cancellation of all polynomial pieces below degree \(N\) is a Prouhet--Thue--Morse
phenomenon. Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 P_N(z):=\prod_{r=0}^{N-1}(1-z^{2^r})
 =\sum_{j=0}^{2^N-1}\epsilon_j z^j.
 \label{p7:eq:finite-TM-product}
\end{equation}
The corresponding unsigned block has
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^N-1}\tau_j z^j
 =\frac12\left(\frac{1-z^{2^N}}{1-z}-P_N(z)\right).
 \label{p7:eq:unsigned-finite-product}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fourier, hyperbolic, and canonical products}

Independence in \eqref{p7:eq:X-random-series} gives
\begin{align}
 \Phi(\xi)
 &=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\SincPi\!\left(\frac{\xi}{2^j}\right),
 \label{p7:eq:phi-sinc-product}\\
 M(t)
 &=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sinh(t/2^k)}{t/2^k}.
 \label{p7:eq:M-hyperbolic-product}
\end{align}
Rearranging Euler products yields
\begin{align}
 \Phi(\xi)
 &=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}
 \left(1-\frac{\xi^2}{m^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(m)},
 \label{p7:eq:phi-canonical-product}\\
 M(t)
 &=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}
 \left(1+\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2m^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(m)}.
 \label{p7:eq:M-canonical-product}
\end{align}
Thus \(\Phi\) has a zero at every nonzero integer \(m\), of multiplicity
\begin{equation}
 d_m=1+\TwoAdicValuation(|m|).
 \label{p7:eq:zero-multiplicity}
\end{equation}
Its logarithmic derivative is the normally convergent meromorphic series
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}
 =-2z\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+\TwoAdicValuation(m)}{m^2-z^2}.
 \label{p7:eq:phi-log-derivative}
\end{equation}
The audited repository develops several equivalent cotangent, tangent, digamma, and
Hurwitz-zeta rearrangements of this expression.

\subsection{Moments, cumulants, Bell polynomials, and Bernoulli numbers}

Write \(\mu_n=\Expectation X^n\). Symmetry gives \(\mu_{2m+1}=0\). Applying
\eqref{p7:eq:distribution-fixed-point} gives the exact triangular recurrence
\begin{equation}
 \mu_n=\frac{1}{2^n-1}
 \sum_{\substack{2\le k\le n\\k\ \mathrm{even}}}
 \binom{n}{k}\frac{\mu_{n-k}}{k+1}
 \qquad(n\ge2\ \mathrm{even}).
 \label{p7:eq:moment-recurrence}
\end{equation}
The cumulants are
\begin{equation}
 \kappa_{2m}=\frac{2^{2m-1}B_{2m}}{m(2^{2m}-1)},
 \qquad \kappa_{2m+1}=0,
 \label{p7:eq:cumulants}
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
 \mu_n=Y_n(\kappa_1,\ldots,\kappa_n),
 \label{p7:eq:Bell-moments}
\end{equation}
where \(Y_n\) is the complete exponential Bell polynomial. These formulas make all moments
rational and expose the Bernoulli and dyadic denominator structure.

\subsection{Cauchy, logarithmic, determinant, and continued-fraction forms}

For \(z\notin[-1,1]\), the Cauchy transform has the moment expansion
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_n}{z^{n+1}},\qquad |z|>1,
 \label{p7:eq:R-moment-series}
\end{equation}
and, for \(\Re z>1\), the Laplace form
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\int_0^{\infty}\EulerE^{-zt}M(t)\Differential t.
 \label{p7:eq:R-Laplace}
\end{equation}
Differentiating under the integral or using \eqref{p7:eq:rho-differential} gives
\begin{equation}
 R'(z)=2\bigl(R(2z+1)-R(2z-1)\bigr).
 \label{p7:eq:R-differential}
\end{equation}
The finite spline transform possesses the logarithmic formula
\begin{equation}
 R_N(z)=\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \sum_{j=0}^{2^N-1}\epsilon_j
 (z+a_{N,j})^{N-1}\PrincipalLogarithm(z+a_{N,j}),
 \label{p7:eq:finite-log-resolvent}
\end{equation}
with polynomial ambiguities canceled by Prouhet identities. The living representation
report also gives Fredholm determinant and Jacobi/Stieltjes continued-fraction forms. If
\(P_n\) are the monic symmetric orthogonal polynomials,
\begin{equation}
 P_{n+1}(x)=xP_n(x)-\beta_nP_{n-1}(x),\qquad \beta_n>0,
 \label{p7:eq:Jacobi-recurrence}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 R(z)=\cfrac{1}{z-\cfrac{\beta_1}{z-\cfrac{\beta_2}{z-\ddots}}}.
 \label{p7:eq:Jacobi-CF}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fabius and inverse-function transform forms}

The pushforward identity \(Y=(X+1)/2\) yields
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi u}\Differential F(u)
 =\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2).
 \label{p7:eq:F-measure-transform}
\end{equation}
For \(\xi\ne0\), integration by parts gives
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1F(u)\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi u}\Differential u
 =\frac{\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2)-\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}}
 {2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}.
 \label{p7:eq:F-function-transform}
\end{equation}
Since \(I(V)\) has distribution \(F\) when \(V\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi I(v)}\Differential v
 =\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\Phi(\xi/2).
 \label{p7:eq:inverse-pushforward-transform}
\end{equation}
The repository further contains finite dyadic antiderivatives, complex powers, fractional
Volterra operators, Mellin/Newton moments, inverse primitives and higher derivatives,
Stieltjes/log-potential transforms, autocorrelation identities, and endpoint asymptotics.

\begin{baselinebox}
Equations \eqref{p7:eq:infinite-convolution}--\eqref{p7:eq:inverse-pushforward-transform} form the
input atlas. Later sections do not claim these identities as new; they reorganize them
through a unit-circle probability lift and derive consequences absent from the audited
subtree.
\end{baselinebox}

\section{Probability--Chebyshev coordinates}
\label{p7:sec:cheb}

\begin{definition}
For \(n\ge0\), define the \emph{probability--Chebyshev moment}
\begin{equation}
 c_n:=\Expectation[T_n(X)]=\int_{-1}^{1}T_n(x)\rho(x)\Differential x,
 \label{p7:eq:cndef}
\end{equation}
where \(T_n(\cos\theta)=\cos(n\theta)\).
\end{definition}

Because \(\rho\) is even, \(c_{2m+1}=0\). These numbers are not the unweighted Chebyshev
coefficients of the density: the integration measure in \eqref{p7:eq:cndef} is
\(\rho(x)\Differential x\), not \(\Differential x/\sqrt{1-x^2}\).

\begin{theorem}[Exact Bell--Bernoulli formula]
\label{p7:thm:c-exact}
For \(m\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 c_{2m}=m\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-k}
 \frac{(m+k-1)!}{(m-k)!(2k)!}\,2^{2k}\mu_{2k}.
 \label{p7:eq:c-moment-formula}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, with \(\kappa_{2j}\) as in \eqref{p7:eq:cumulants},
\begin{equation}
 c_{2m}=m\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-k}
 \frac{(m+k-1)!}{(m-k)!(2k)!}\,2^{2k}
 Y_{2k}(0,\kappa_2,0,\kappa_4,\ldots,\kappa_{2k}).
 \label{p7:eq:c-Bell-Bernoulli}
\end{equation}
In particular every \(c_n\) is rational.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(m\ge1\),
\[
 T_{2m}(x)=m\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-k}
 \frac{(m+k-1)!}{(m-k)!(2k)!}(2x)^{2k}.
\]
Integrating termwise against \(\rho(x)\Differential x\) proves \eqref{p7:eq:c-moment-formula}; substituting
\eqref{p7:eq:Bell-moments} and \eqref{p7:eq:cumulants} proves \eqref{p7:eq:c-Bell-Bernoulli}.
\end{proof}

The first terms are
\begin{align*}
 c_0&=1,&c_2&=-\frac79,&c_4&=\frac{227}{675},&c_6&=-\frac{11237}{297675},\\
 c_8&=-\frac{8241761}{227721375},&
 c_{10}&=\frac{171586753}{8135075025},&
 c_{12}&=-\frac{472381023339557}{45472425490366875}.
\end{align*}
The repeated negative sign at \(c_6,c_8\) warns against a naive alternating-sign conjecture.

\begin{proposition}[Generating function]
\label{p7:prop:G-cheb}
For \(|w|<1\),
\begin{align}
 G(w)&:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_nw^n
 =\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1-wx}{1-2wx+w^2}\rho(x)\Differential x,
 \label{p7:eq:G-cheb}\\
 &=1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}w^{2m}.
 \label{p7:eq:G-even}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Integrate the standard generating function
\(\sum_{n\ge0}T_n(x)w^n=(1-wx)/(1-2wx+w^2)\) against the probability measure.
\end{proof}

For any \(0<r<1\), Cauchy coefficient extraction gives
\begin{equation}
 c_n=\frac{1}{2\pi r^n}\int_0^{2\pi}G(re^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})
 \EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit n\theta}\Differential\theta.
 \label{p7:eq:c-Cauchy-coefficient}
\end{equation}
This will become an ordinary circle Fourier coefficient in the next section.

\begin{novelbox}
The sequence \(c_n\) is one rational coordinate system controlling a positive-real
Carath\'eodory function, the Joukowski pullback of \(R\), a weighted Chebyshev expansion of
\(\rho\), phase series for \(F\) and \(F^{-1}\), Bessel expansions of \(\Phi\) and \(M\),
and rational OPUC/CMV data. This unification is the central structural result of the report.
\end{novelbox}

\section{Unit-circle lift and Carath\'eodory--Joukowski bridge}
\label{p7:sec:circle}

\subsection{A canonical probability measure on the circle}

Define a probability measure \(\nu\) on \(\T\) by
\begin{equation}
 \Differential\nu(\theta)=\frac12\rho(\cos\theta)|\sin\theta|\Differential\theta,
 \qquad 0\le\theta<2\pi.
 \label{p7:eq:circle-lift}
\end{equation}
The map \(\theta\mapsto x=\cos\theta\) pushes \(\nu\) forward to \(\rho(x)\Differential x\). Moreover,
\(\nu\) is invariant under conjugation and the half-turn \(\zeta\mapsto-\zeta\).

\begin{proposition}[Fourier moments of the lift]
\label{p7:prop:circle-moments}
For every \(n\in\IntegerNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\T}\zeta^{-n}\Differential\nu(\zeta)=c_{|n|}.
 \label{p7:eq:nu-moments}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Imaginary parts cancel under conjugation, while
\(\cos(n\arccos x)=T_n(x)\). The two preimages of each \(x\in(-1,1)\) contribute equally.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
The Carath\'eodory function of \(\nu\) is
\begin{equation}
 C(w)=\int_{\T}\frac{\zeta+w}{\zeta-w}\Differential\nu(\zeta),
 \qquad w\in\D.
 \label{p7:eq:C-def}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}
It is analytic in \(\D\), satisfies \(C(0)=1\), and has positive real part.

\begin{theorem}[Carath\'eodory representation]
\label{p7:thm:C-representation}
For \(|w|<1\),
\begin{align}
 C(w)
 &=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1-w^2}{1-2wx+w^2}\rho(x)\Differential x,
 \label{p7:eq:C-real-integral}\\
 &=1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_nw^n
 =1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}w^{2m},
 \label{p7:eq:C-series}\\
 &=2G(w)-1.
 \label{p7:eq:C-G}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Pair \(e^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta}\) and \(e^{-\ImaginaryUnit\theta}\) in \eqref{p7:eq:C-def}; their average kernel is
\((1-w^2)/(1-2w\cos\theta+w^2)\). The geometric expansion
\((\zeta+w)/(\zeta-w)=1+2\sum_{n\ge1}w^n\zeta^{-n}\) and
\cref{p7:prop:circle-moments} give the series.
\end{proof}

The positive harmonic regularization
\begin{equation}
 W_r(\theta):=\Re C(re^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})
 =1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}r^{2m}\cos(2m\theta)
 \label{p7:eq:Wr}
\end{equation}
preserves positivity for every \(0\le r<1\), unlike an arbitrary boundary truncation.

\subsection{Joukowski uniformization}

Let
\begin{equation}
 \Jouk(w)=\frac12(w+w^{-1}),\qquad 0<|w|<1,
 \label{p7:eq:Joukowski}
\end{equation}
with inverse branch
\begin{equation}
 w(z)=z-\sqrt{z^2-1},
 \qquad \sqrt{z^2-1}\sim z\quad(z\to\infty).
 \label{p7:eq:Joukowski-inverse}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Exact Joukowski bridge]
\label{p7:thm:Joukowski-bridge}
For \(0<|w|<1\),
\begin{equation}
 C(w)=\frac{1-w^2}{2w}R(\Jouk(w)).
 \label{p7:eq:C-R-Jouk}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for \(z\in\ComplexNumbers\setminus[-1,1]\),
\begin{align}
 R(z)&=\frac{2w(z)}{1-w(z)^2}C(w(z)),
 \label{p7:eq:R-C-w}\\
 &=\frac{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}w(z)^{2m}}
 {\sqrt{z^2-1}}.
 \label{p7:eq:R-Cheb-series}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since \(\Jouk(w)-x=(1-2wx+w^2)/(2w)\), compare \eqref{p7:eq:C-real-integral} with the
definition of \(R\). On the chosen branch, \(\sqrt{z^2-1}=(1-w^2)/(2w)\).
\end{proof}

The ordinary moment series is centered at infinity in powers of \(z^{-1}\). By contrast,
\eqref{p7:eq:R-Cheb-series} uses the conformal coordinate of the slit plane, so it remains
geometrically adapted even near the support.

\subsection[Boundary recovery of rho]{Boundary recovery of \(\rho\)}

Set
\begin{equation}
 W(\theta)=\pi\rho(\cos\theta)|\sin\theta|.
 \label{p7:eq:W-def}
\end{equation}
This is the density of \(\nu\) relative to \(\Differential\theta/(2\pi)\). Endpoint flatness makes it a
\(C^\infty\), \(\pi\)-periodic function.

\begin{theorem}[Weighted Chebyshev boundary series]
\label{p7:thm:boundary-rho}
For every \(\theta\),
\begin{equation}
 W(\theta)=\Re C(e^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})
 =1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}\cos(2m\theta),
 \label{p7:eq:W-Fourier}
\end{equation}
with convergence in \(C^k\) for every fixed \(k\). Consequently, for \(|x|<1\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \rho(x)=\frac{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}T_{2m}(x)}
 {\pi\sqrt{1-x^2}}.}
 \label{p7:eq:rho-weighted-Cheb}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The boundary theorem for Poisson integrals gives \(\Re C(e^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})=W(\theta)\). Smoothness
implies uniform convergence of all fixed derivatives. Substitute \(x=\cos\theta\).
\end{proof}

The harmonic conjugate is
\begin{align}
 \Im C(e^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta})
 &=2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}\sin(2m\theta),
 \label{p7:eq:C-imag-series}\\
 &=\PV\int_0^{2\pi}\cot\!\left(\frac{\theta-\varphi}{2}\right)\Differential\nu(\varphi).
 \label{p7:eq:C-Hilbert}
\end{align}
Thus one disk-analytic function packages the density and its circular Hilbert transform.

\subsection[Phase series for F and inverse F]{Phase series for \(F\) and \(F^{-1}\)}

For \(0<u<1\), put
\begin{equation}
 \theta(u)=\arccos(2u-1)\in(0,\pi).
 \label{p7:eq:theta-u}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Fabius phase series]
\label{p7:thm:F-phase}
For \(0\le u\le1\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F(u)=1-\frac{\theta(u)}{\pi}
 -\frac1\pi\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{2m}}{m}\sin(2m\theta(u)).}
 \label{p7:eq:F-phase}
\end{equation}
For \(0<u<1\),
\begin{equation}
 F'(u)=\frac{\Re C(e^{\ImaginaryUnit\theta(u)})}{\pi\sqrt{u(1-u)}}
 =\frac{1+2\sum_{m\ge1}c_{2m}T_{2m}(2u-1)}{\pi\sqrt{u(1-u)}}.
 \label{p7:eq:Fprime-C}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute \(x=\cos\varphi\) in \(F(u)=\int_{-1}^{2u-1}\rho(x)\Differential x\):
\(F(u)=\pi^{-1}\int_{\theta(u)}^\pi W(\varphi)\Differential\varphi\). Integrate
\eqref{p7:eq:W-Fourier} termwise and differentiate.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Inverse phase equation]
\label{p7:cor:inverse-phase}
For each \(v\in(0,1)\), let \(\Theta(v)\in(0,\pi)\) be the unique solution of
\begin{equation}
 v=1-\frac{\Theta}{\pi}
 -\frac1\pi\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{2m}}{m}\sin(2m\Theta).
 \label{p7:eq:Theta-implicit}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 I(v)&=\frac{1+\cos\Theta(v)}{2},
 \label{p7:eq:I-phase}\\
 I'(v)&=\frac{\pi\sqrt{I(v)(1-I(v))}}{\Re C(e^{\ImaginaryUnit\Theta(v)})}.
 \label{p7:eq:Iprime-C}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

The explicit factors \(1/\sqrt{u(1-u)}\) do not create endpoint singularities in \(F'\): the
numerator \(W\) is flat enough to cancel every algebraic divergence. In the inverse formula,
\(1/W\) isolates the true rapid endpoint growth and is compatible with the Lambert--\(W\)
asymptotics in the repository.

\subsection{Disk form of the resolvent differential equation}

Set
\begin{equation}
 A(w)=\frac{2wC(w)}{1-w^2}=R(\Jouk(w))
 \label{p7:eq:A-disk}
\end{equation}
and, as germs at \(w=0\),
\begin{equation}
 q_{\pm}(w)=2\Jouk(w)\pm1-
 \sqrt{(2\Jouk(w)\pm1)^2-1},
 \label{p7:eq:qpm}
\end{equation}
choosing square roots so that \(q_\pm(w)\to0\).

\begin{proposition}[Disk composition equation]
\label{p7:prop:disk-composition}
Near \(w=0\),
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential w}\left(\frac{2wC(w)}{1-w^2}\right)
 =4\Jouk'(w)\left[
 \frac{q_+(w)C(q_+(w))}{1-q_+(w)^2}
 -\frac{q_-(w)C(q_-(w))}{1-q_-(w)^2}
 \right].
 \label{p7:eq:disk-composition}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate \(A(w)=R(\Jouk(w))\), apply \eqref{p7:eq:R-differential}, and use
\(R(\Jouk(q))=2qC(q)/(1-q^2)\) for each inverse branch.
\end{proof}

\section{Bessel series equal to the dyadic products}
\label{p7:sec:bessel}

The Jacobi--Anger expansions
\begin{align}
 \EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit z\cos\theta}
 &=J_0(z)+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-\ImaginaryUnit)^nJ_n(z)\cos(n\theta),
 \label{p7:eq:JA-ordinary}\\
 \EulerE^{z\cos\theta}
 &=I_0(z)+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}I_n(z)\cos(n\theta)
 \label{p7:eq:JA-modified}
\end{align}
turn the Fourier moments of \(\nu\) into Bessel coefficients.

\begin{theorem}[Bessel--sinc--canonical product identity]
\label{p7:thm:Bessel-Phi}
For every \(\xi\in\ComplexNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Phi(\xi)=J_0(2\pi\xi)
 +2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(-1)^m c_{2m}J_{2m}(2\pi\xi).}
 \label{p7:eq:Phi-Bessel}
\end{equation}
The series is locally uniformly convergent, and
\begin{align}
 J_0(2\pi\xi)+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(-1)^mc_{2m}J_{2m}(2\pi\xi)
 &=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\SincPi\!\left(\frac{\xi}{2^j}\right)
 \label{p7:eq:Bessel-sinc}\\
 &=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{\xi^2}{n^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}.
 \label{p7:eq:Bessel-canonical}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under \(\nu\), \(X=\cos\Theta\). Integrate \eqref{p7:eq:JA-ordinary} with
\(z=2\pi\xi\); odd moments vanish and \((-\ImaginaryUnit)^{2m}=(-1)^m\). The product equalities are
\eqref{p7:eq:phi-sinc-product} and \eqref{p7:eq:phi-canonical-product}.
\end{proof}

For every \(r\ge0\), termwise differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 \Phi^{(r)}(\xi)=(2\pi)^r\left[J_0^{(r)}(2\pi\xi)
 +2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(-1)^mc_{2m}J_{2m}^{(r)}(2\pi\xi)\right].
 \label{p7:eq:Phi-Bessel-derivatives}
\end{equation}
At an integer zero \(N\ne0\), the first \(1+\TwoAdicValuation(N)\) equations in this derivative ladder
become exact Bessel cancellation identities. In particular,
\begin{equation}
 J_0(2\pi N)+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(-1)^mc_{2m}J_{2m}(2\pi N)=0.
 \label{p7:eq:Bessel-integer-zero}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Modified-Bessel--hyperbolic identity]
\label{p7:thm:Bessel-M}
For every \(t\in\ComplexNumbers\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 M(t)=I_0(t)+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}I_{2m}(t).}
 \label{p7:eq:M-Bessel}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{align}
 I_0(t)+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}I_{2m}(t)
 &=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sinh(t/2^k)}{t/2^k}
 \label{p7:eq:IBessel-hyperbolic}\\
 &=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2n^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}.
 \label{p7:eq:IBessel-canonical}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Integrate \eqref{p7:eq:JA-modified} against \(\nu\) and invoke the product formulas for \(M\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Laplace--Bessel--Joukowski chain}

For \(\Re z>1\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^{\infty}\EulerE^{-zt}I_n(t)\Differential t
 =\frac{(z-\sqrt{z^2-1})^n}{\sqrt{z^2-1}}.
 \label{p7:eq:Laplace-In}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Three-level transform identity]
\label{p7:thm:Laplace-Bessel-Jouk}
For \(\Re z>1\), with \(w=z-\sqrt{z^2-1}\),
\begin{align}
 R(z)
 &=\int_0^{\infty}\EulerE^{-zt}
 \prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sinh(t/2^k)}{t/2^k}\Differential t
 \label{p7:eq:R-Laplace-product-new}\\
 &=\int_0^{\infty}\EulerE^{-zt}\left[I_0(t)+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}I_{2m}(t)\right]\Differential t
 \label{p7:eq:R-Laplace-Bessel}\\
 &=\frac{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}c_{2m}w^{2m}}{\sqrt{z^2-1}}.
 \label{p7:eq:R-Laplace-Jouk}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Insert \eqref{p7:eq:M-hyperbolic-product} and \eqref{p7:eq:M-Bessel} into
\eqref{p7:eq:R-Laplace}; use dominated convergence and \eqref{p7:eq:Laplace-In}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Fabius and inverse-Fabius transforms}

For \(\xi\ne0\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1F(u)\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi u}\Differential u
 =\frac{\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}
 \left[J_0(\pi\xi)+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(-1)^mc_{2m}J_{2m}(\pi\xi)\right]
 -\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}.
 \label{p7:eq:F-Bessel-transform}
\end{equation}
The value at \(\xi=0\) is \(1/2\). For the inverse pushforward,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1\EulerE^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi I(v)}\Differential v
 =\EulerE^{-\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\left[J_0(\pi\xi)+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(-1)^mc_{2m}J_{2m}(\pi\xi)\right].
 \label{p7:eq:I-Bessel-transform}
\end{equation}

\begin{novelbox}
Equations \eqref{p7:eq:Bessel-sinc}, \eqref{p7:eq:Bessel-canonical},
\eqref{p7:eq:IBessel-hyperbolic}, and \eqref{p7:eq:IBessel-canonical} are direct series--product
bridges: the same rational sequence \(c_{2m}\) expands both the Fourier image and the moment
generating function in Bessel bases. No corresponding bridge was located in the audited
repository.
\end{novelbox}

\section{Schur, Verblunsky, CMV, and Toeplitz representations}
\label{p7:sec:opuc}

The Carath\'eodory function places the Rvachev measure in the theory of orthogonal
polynomials on the unit circle (OPUC) \cite{p7:SimonOPUC1,p7:SimonOneFoot}. This creates a rational
coefficient system distinct from, but explicitly coupled to, the repository's Jacobi
coefficients.

\subsection{Schur algorithm and rationality}

Define
\begin{equation}
 f_0(w)=\frac{C(w)-1}{w(C(w)+1)},
 \qquad C(w)=\frac{1+wf_0(w)}{1-wf_0(w)}.
 \label{p7:eq:Schur-first}
\end{equation}
Recursively set
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_n=f_n(0),\qquad
 f_{n+1}(w)=\frac{f_n(w)-\alpha_n}{w(1-\alpha_nf_n(w))}.
 \label{p7:eq:Schur-recursion}
\end{equation}
All functions and coefficients are real under the present symmetries.

\begin{theorem}[Rational Verblunsky coefficients]
\label{p7:thm:rational-alpha}
The Schur parameters \(\alpha_n\) are the Verblunsky coefficients of \(\nu\). They satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_n\in\RationalNumbers\cap(-1,1),\qquad \alpha_{2n}=0\quad(n\ge0).
 \label{p7:eq:alpha-rational-parity}
\end{equation}
The odd coefficients are obtained by exact rational operations from the moments \(c_{2m}\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first assertion is the standard Carath\'eodory--Schur correspondence. Rationality follows
because the Taylor coefficients of \(C\) are rational and every Schur step uses rational
operations. The half-turn invariance \(\nu(E)=\nu(-E)\) forces all odd circle moments and,
equivalently, every even-index Verblunsky coefficient to vanish. Infinite support gives
\(|\alpha_n|<1\).
\end{proof}

The first nonzero values are
\begin{align*}
 \alpha_1&=-\frac79,&
 \alpha_3&=-\frac{17}{25},&
 \alpha_5&=-\frac{1849}{3087},\\
 \alpha_7&=-\frac{3569379}{6492691},&
 \alpha_9&=-\frac{445076883510763}{886612917691413},&
 \alpha_{11}&=-\frac{594580974953911037169143}{1270428317522023221443375}.
\end{align*}
The exact program computes much longer coefficients without floating-point contamination.

\subsection{Wall--Schur continued fraction}

Solving \eqref{p7:eq:Schur-recursion} for \(f_n\) gives
\begin{equation}
 f_n(w)=\frac{\alpha_n+w f_{n+1}(w)}{1+\alpha_n w f_{n+1}(w)}.
 \label{p7:eq:inverse-Schur-step}
\end{equation}
Iterating this Möbius recursion and inserting it into \eqref{p7:eq:Schur-first} produces a
Wall--Schur continued fraction for \(C\). Truncating after \(N\) parameters by setting
\(f_N=0\) gives a rational \(C_N\) with positive real part in \(\D\). Therefore
\begin{equation}
 R_N^{\mathrm{Schur}}(z)=\frac{C_N(w(z))}{\sqrt{z^2-1}}
 \label{p7:eq:R-Schur-approximant}
\end{equation}
is a positivity-preserving rational/conformal approximation to the Cauchy transform. It is
geometrically different from truncating the real-line Jacobi fraction
\eqref{p7:eq:Jacobi-CF}.

\subsection{Geronimus bridge to Jacobi coefficients}

For the symmetric pushforward under \(x=(\zeta+\zeta^{-1})/2\), the Geronimus relation
simplifies because \(\alpha_{2n}=0\).

\begin{theorem}[Exact Jacobi--Verblunsky conversion]
\label{p7:thm:Geronimus}
With \(\alpha_{-1}=-1\), the monic Jacobi coefficients satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \beta_{n+1}=\frac14(1-\alpha_{2n-1})(1+\alpha_{2n+1})}
 \qquad(n\ge0).
 \label{p7:eq:Geronimus}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_{2n+1}=\frac{4\beta_{n+1}}{1-\alpha_{2n-1}}-1.
 \label{p7:eq:alpha-from-beta}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The first values
\begin{equation}
 \beta_1=\frac19,\quad
 \beta_2=\frac{32}{225},\quad
 \beta_3=\frac{619}{3675},\quad
 \beta_4=\frac{835232}{4640643}
 \label{p7:eq:first-betas}
\end{equation}
reproduce \(\alpha_1,\alpha_3,\alpha_5,\alpha_7\). In the accompanying code, the
\(\beta_n\) are generated by exact Gram--Schmidt from \(\mu_n\), while a direct formal Schur
algorithm independently generates \(\alpha_n\); the pipelines agree exactly through the
checked indices.

\subsection{CMV resolvent}

Let \(\CMV\) be the CMV matrix associated with \((\alpha_n)_{n\ge0}\) on
\(\ell^2(\NaturalNumbers)\), and let \(e_0=(1,0,\ldots)\). Its spectral measure at \(e_0\) is \(\nu\).

\begin{theorem}[CMV representation]
\label{p7:thm:CMV}
For \(|w|<1\),
\begin{equation}
 C(w)=\left\langle e_0,(\CMV+w)(\CMV-w)^{-1}e_0\right\rangle.
 \label{p7:eq:C-CMV}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 R(\Jouk(w))=\frac{2w}{1-w^2}
 \left\langle e_0,(\CMV+w)(\CMV-w)^{-1}e_0\right\rangle.
 \label{p7:eq:R-CMV}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply the spectral theorem to multiplication by \(\zeta\) in \(L^2(\nu)\), then express that
unitary operator in the CMV basis. The second formula follows from
\eqref{p7:eq:C-R-Jouk}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Toeplitz determinants and Szeg\H{o} product}

Let
\begin{equation}
 D_N=\det[c_{j-k}]_{j,k=0}^{N-1},\qquad c_{-n}=c_n.
 \label{p7:eq:Toeplitz-D}
\end{equation}
These determinants are positive rationals.

\begin{proposition}[Exact determinant product]
\label{p7:prop:Toeplitz-product}
For \(N\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 D_N=\prod_{j=0}^{N-2}(1-\alpha_j^2)^{N-1-j}.
 \label{p7:eq:Toeplitz-alpha}
\end{equation}
Only odd \(j\) contribute.
\end{proposition}

The weight \(W(\theta)\) is positive almost everywhere and
\(\log W\in L^1(\T)\). The latter follows from the established endpoint asymptotics:
although \(\rho(\cos\theta)\) is superflat, its logarithm grows only polylogarithmically in
\(1/|\theta|\), which is integrable against \(\Differential\theta\). Szeg\H{o}'s theorem gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \prod_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-\alpha_n^2)
 =\exp\left(\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}\log W(\theta)\Differential\theta\right).}
 \label{p7:eq:Szego-product}
\end{equation}
Rakhmanov's theorem also yields \(\alpha_n\to0\), because \(W>0\) almost everywhere.

\begin{novelbox}
Equations \eqref{p7:eq:C-CMV}, \eqref{p7:eq:R-CMV}, \eqref{p7:eq:Toeplitz-alpha}, and
\eqref{p7:eq:Szego-product} give operator, determinant, and entropy-product representations of
the Rvachev law. The associated CMV coefficients are exact rationals and are linked to the
known Jacobi coefficients by \eqref{p7:eq:Geronimus}.
\end{novelbox}

\section{Spectral, contour, and monodromy formulas for Thue--Morse}
\label{p7:sec:TM}

This section derives representations for both \(\epsilon_n\) and \(\tau_n\) by treating the
integer zeros of \(\Phi\) as spectral data. These formulas differ from the repository's
coefficient, digit, half-integer-sign, trigonometric, and finite-product formulas.

\subsection{Zero-counting encodes the full binary digit sum}

Let
\begin{equation}
 N_\Phi(n):=\sum_{m=1}^{n}\bigl(1+\TwoAdicValuation(m)\bigr),\qquad N_\Phi(0)=0.
 \label{p7:eq:Nphi-def}
\end{equation}
This is the number of positive zeros of \(\Phi\) in \((0,n+1/2)\), counted with multiplicity.

\begin{theorem}[Spectral digit-sum identity]
\label{p7:thm:spectral-digit-sum}
For every \(n\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 N_\Phi(n)=n+\TwoAdicValuation(n!)=2n-\BinaryDigitSum(n).
 \label{p7:eq:Nphi-digit}
\end{equation}
Thus the complete binary digit sum, not only its parity, is recovered by
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle \BinaryDigitSum(n)=2n-N_\Phi(n).}
 \label{p7:eq:digit-from-spectrum}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first equality follows from \eqref{p7:eq:zero-multiplicity}. Legendre's formula for the
2-adic valuation gives \(\TwoAdicValuation(n!)=n-\BinaryDigitSum(n)\).
\end{proof}

Let \(\Gamma_n\) be any positively oriented contour enclosing precisely the positive integer
zeros \(1,\ldots,n\), and no other zeros of \(\Phi\).

\begin{corollary}[Argument-principle representations]
\label{p7:cor:argument-TM}
For \(n\ge0\),
\begin{align}
 \BinaryDigitSum(n)
 &=2n-\frac{1}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit}\oint_{\Gamma_n}\frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}\Differential z,
 \label{p7:eq:digit-contour}\\
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \epsilon_n=\exp\left(\frac12\oint_{\Gamma_n}
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}\Differential z\right),}
 \label{p7:eq:epsilon-contour}\\
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \tau_n=\sin^2\left(\frac{1}{4\ImaginaryUnit}\oint_{\Gamma_n}
 \frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}\Differential z\right).}
 \label{p7:eq:tau-contour}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The argument principle gives the contour integral as \(2\pi\ImaginaryUnit N_\Phi(n)\). Since
\(N_\Phi(n)\equiv \BinaryDigitSum(n)\pmod2\), exponentiation gives \(\epsilon_n\), while
\(\sin^2(\pi N_\Phi(n)/2)\) is its unsigned parity.
\end{proof}

Substitution of \eqref{p7:eq:phi-log-derivative} turns the contour formulas into explicit
meromorphic series integrals. Locally at a positive integer,
\begin{equation}
 \ResidueOperator_{z=n}\frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}=1+\TwoAdicValuation(n),
 \label{p7:eq:local-residue}
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\epsilon_n}{\epsilon_{n-1}}
 =\exp\left(\pi\ImaginaryUnit\ResidueOperator_{z=n}\frac{\Phi'(z)}{\Phi(z)}\right)
 =(-1)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)}.
 \label{p7:eq:local-TM-ratio}
\end{equation}
Thus the local zero multiplicity records the carry chain when passing from \(n-1\) to \(n\).

\subsection{Logarithmic monodromy of finite resolvents}

The finite resolvent \eqref{p7:eq:finite-log-resolvent} has branch points
\begin{equation}
 z_{N,j}=-a_{N,j},\qquad 0\le j<2^N.
 \label{p7:eq:branch-points}
\end{equation}
Let \(\Delta_jR_N\) denote the change after one positive analytic continuation around
\(z_{N,j}\), holding all other logarithmic branches fixed.

\begin{theorem}[Monodromy recovery of a finite Thue--Morse word]
\label{p7:thm:monodromy-TM}
For \(z\) near \(z_{N,j}\),
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_jR_N(z)=2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\frac{2^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N-1)!}
 \epsilon_j(z-z_{N,j})^{N-1}.
 \label{p7:eq:monodromy-jump}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \epsilon_j=\frac{1}{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\,2^{N(N-1)/2}}
 \left.\frac{\Differential^{N-1}}{\Differential z^{N-1}}\Delta_jR_N(z)\right|_{z=z_{N,j}},}
 \label{p7:eq:epsilon-monodromy}
\end{equation}
and \(\tau_j=(1-\epsilon_j)/2\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Only the \(j\)-th logarithm changes, by \(2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\), around \(z_{N,j}\). This proves
\eqref{p7:eq:monodromy-jump}. Differentiating \(N-1\) times at the branch point cancels
\((N-1)!\) and isolates \(\epsilon_j\).
\end{proof}

The finite Thue--Morse word is therefore the normalized monodromy representation of a single
multivalued analytic function. This interpretation is stronger than saying that
\(\epsilon_j\) merely occurs as an algebraic coefficient: the word is recovered from the
analytic continuation operators themselves.

\subsection{Abel--Poisson regularization of the Rademacher product}

Define, for \(0<r<1\),
\begin{align}
 H_r(\theta)
 &:=\frac{4}{\pi}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{r^{2\ell+1}}{2\ell+1}\sin((2\ell+1)\theta)
 \label{p7:eq:Hr-series}\\
 &=\frac{2}{\pi}\operatorname{atan2}
 \bigl(2r\sin\theta,1-r^2\bigr).
 \label{p7:eq:Hr-atan}
\end{align}
This is the Abel/Poisson regularization of \(\Sign(\sin\theta)\), and
\(H_r(\theta)\to\Sign(\sin\theta)\) away from multiples of \(\pi\).

For \(n\ge1\), put \(J_n=\Floor{\log_2(n+1/2)}\); for \(n=0\), interpret the product
below as empty.

\begin{theorem}[Smooth finite-product representation]
\label{p7:thm:Abel-TM}
For every \(n\ge0\),
\begin{align}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \epsilon_n=\lim_{r\uparrow1}
 \prod_{j=0}^{J_n}H_r\!\left(\frac{\pi(n+1/2)}{2^j}\right),}
 \label{p7:eq:epsilon-Abel}\\
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \tau_n=\frac12\left[1-
 \lim_{r\uparrow1}\prod_{j=0}^{J_n}H_r\!\left(\frac{\pi(n+1/2)}{2^j}\right)\right].}
 \label{p7:eq:tau-Abel}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The signed Thue--Morse sequence has the Rademacher sign product
\[
 \epsilon_n=\prod_{j\ge0}\Sign\sin\!\left(\frac{\pi(n+1/2)}{2^j}\right).
\]
For \(j>J_n\), the angle lies in \((0,\pi)\), so the factor is \(+1\); only finitely many
factors matter. Replace each by its Abel limit.
\end{proof}

The accompanying computation shows uniform convergence over the first 64 symbols: the
maximum errors for \(r=0.9,0.99,0.999,0.9999\) are approximately
\(0.937,0.237,0.0257,0.00259\), respectively. The formula is especially useful when a smooth
analytic surrogate is preferable to a discontinuous sign function.

\subsection{A cardinal Bessel--Thue--Morse expansion}

Since \(\rho\) is supported in \([-1,1]\), the Paley--Wiener/Shannon formula at spacing
\(1/2\) is
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\Phi(k/2)\SincPi(2z-k).
 \label{p7:eq:Shannon-Phi}
\end{equation}
All nonzero integer samples vanish. If
\(A_n=|\Phi(n+1/2)|\), the existing half-integer sign law
\(\Sign\Phi(n+1/2)=\epsilon_n\) therefore yields
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\SincPi(2z)+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\epsilon_nA_n
 \left[\SincPi(2z-(2n+1))+\SincPi(2z+(2n+1))\right].
 \label{p7:eq:Shannon-TM}
\end{equation}
This formula is included as a derived synthesis rather than a primary novelty claim, because
the latest repository report already develops a broad cardinal-reproduction ladder.

\begin{novelbox}
The contour formulas recover the full digit sum and both Thue--Morse normalizations from the
zero divisor of \(\Phi\). The monodromy formula recovers each finite word from analytic
continuation of \(R_N\), while the Abel formula replaces discontinuous Rademacher signs by
finite products of harmonic functions.
\end{novelbox}

\section{Exact computations and numerical experiments}
\label{p7:sec:numerics}

The file \texttt{numerical\_experiments.py} is deliberately self-contained. It uses
\path{fractions.Fraction} for exact moments, Chebyshev moments, Jacobi coefficients, and
Verblunsky coefficients; \texttt{mpmath} for 70-digit analytic checks; and
\texttt{matplotlib} for the figures. The code follows two independent coefficient routes:

\begin{enumerate}
\item ordinary moments \(\to\) exact monic Gram--Schmidt \(\to\beta_n\to\alpha_n\) through
Geronimus;
\item ordinary moments \(\to c_n\to C(w)\to\alpha_n\) through a formal exact Schur algorithm.
\end{enumerate}

The two routes agree exactly through \(\alpha_{15}\) in the automated assertion. The script
also verifies \(N_\Phi(n)=2n-\BinaryDigitSum(n)\) for \(0\le n\le256\), the monodromy normalization for
\(1\le N\le9\), and all positivity/unit-disk conditions for the computed coefficients.

\input{assets/rvachev_fabius_representations_2026/generated_tables.tex}

The figure is plotted from exact fractions, not from a fitted or floating-point recurrence.
The largest fractions contain hundreds of digits; the CSV files preserve their exact
numerators and denominators.

\subsection{Reproducibility boundaries}

The experiments verify identities already proved symbolically; they do not establish the
conjectures in \cref{p7:sec:conjectures}. Product truncation and quadrature errors are not given
as formal interval bounds, so their decimal residuals should be read as consistency checks.
A natural next step is to port the same recurrences to Arb or MPFI interval arithmetic.

\section{Conjectures and frontier research program}
\label{p7:sec:conjectures}

\begin{conjecturebox}
The statements in this section are unproved unless explicitly described as conditional.
They are motivated by exact data, standard OPUC theory, and the known endpoint asymptotics,
but they should not be cited as theorems.
\end{conjecturebox}

\begin{conjecture}[Sign and monotonicity of odd Verblunsky coefficients]
\label{p7:conj:alpha-monotone}
For every \(n\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 -1<\alpha_{2n+1}<\alpha_{2n+3}<0.
 \label{p7:eq:conj-alpha-monotone}
\end{equation}
Thus the odd subsequence approaches zero strictly from below.
\end{conjecture}

The first twenty odd coefficients support the conjecture. A proof may be accessible through
total positivity of Toeplitz minors, a comparison principle for the Schur algorithm, or a
special sign-regularity property of the circle weight \(W\). Ordinary positivity of \(W\) is
not enough; a genuinely stronger structural argument is needed.

\begin{conjecture}[Logarithmic edge law]
\label{p7:conj:alpha-asymptotic}
There is a constant \(A>0\) such that
\begin{equation}
 \alpha_{2n-1}\sim-A\frac{\log n}{n}.
 \label{p7:eq:alpha-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
If the Jacobi edge law has the form
\begin{equation}
 \frac14-\beta_{n+1}\sim C\frac{(\log n)^2}{n^2},
 \label{p7:eq:beta-edge-conj}
\end{equation}
then the Geronimus relation predicts \(A=2\sqrt C\).
\end{conjecture}

Indeed, writing \(\gamma_n=-\alpha_{2n-1}\),
\[
 \frac14-\beta_{n+1}
 =\frac14\bigl(\gamma_{n+1}-\gamma_n+\gamma_n\gamma_{n+1}\bigr).
\]
For \(\gamma_n\asymp(\log n)/n\), the product term dominates the discrete derivative.
This turns the real-line Jacobi edge problem into a unit-circle Schur asymptotic problem.

\begin{conjecture}[Refined Szeg\H{o}--Toeplitz asymptotics]
\label{p7:conj:Toeplitz}
The Toeplitz determinants \(D_N\) admit a strong asymptotic expansion whose first correction
to the Szeg\H{o} linear term is governed by the logarithmic endpoint-flatness law of
\(\rho\), and hence ultimately by the Lambert--\(W\) expansion appearing in the endpoint
analysis.
\end{conjecture}

The weight is positive and smooth but has infinite-order zeros. It lies outside the most
naive strictly-positive smooth-weight regime, making it a promising test case for extending
Fisher--Hartwig and strong Szeg\H{o} methods to logarithmically superflat weights.

\begin{conjecture}[Arithmetic structure of the Szeg\H{o} constant]
\label{p7:conj:Szego-constant}
The entropy integral
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal S_\rho=\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
 \log\bigl(\pi\rho(\cos\theta)|\sin\theta|\bigr)\Differential\theta
 \label{p7:eq:Szego-constant}
\end{equation}
has a representation in terms of the dyadic cumulant data, perhaps as a rapidly convergent
series involving Bernoulli numbers, dyadic zeta factors, or a binary Barnes-type constant.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{conjecture}[Bessel zero hierarchy has a finite recursive basis]
\label{p7:conj:Bessel-basis}
The derivative cancellation identities obtained from the zero of multiplicity
\(1+\TwoAdicValuation(N)\) at \(N\) can be reduced, using Bessel recurrences, to a triangular finite
basis whose coefficient matrix has an explicit Thue--Morse or \(q\)-binomial factorization.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{conjecture}[Quantitative Abel convergence]
\label{p7:conj:Abel-rate}
For \(n\ge1\) and \(r\) sufficiently close to one,
\begin{equation}
 \left|\epsilon_n-\prod_{j=0}^{J_n}H_r\!\left(
 \frac{\pi(n+1/2)}{2^j}\right)\right|
 \le C(1-r)\sum_{j=0}^{J_n}\frac{1}{|\sin(\pi(n+1/2)/2^j)|}
 \label{p7:eq:Abel-rate}
\end{equation}
with an absolute constant \(C\), and the reciprocal-sine sum admits a sharp bound in terms
of the binary carry structure of \(n\).
\end{conjecture}

\subsection{Promising research directions}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Rigorous OPUC asymptotics.} Derive the odd Verblunsky asymptotics directly from
the superflat weight using nonlinear steepest descent for the OPUC Riemann--Hilbert problem,
or via fine spectral theory of the CMV matrix.

\item \textbf{Certified coefficient computation.} Combine exact rational recurrences with
modular arithmetic and rational reconstruction. Hundreds of \(\alpha_n\) and \(\beta_n\)
should be feasible without storing enormous intermediate fractions.

\item \textbf{Fast inverse Fabius solvers.} Use the phase equation
\eqref{p7:eq:Theta-implicit} with FFT-evaluated trigonometric sums and safeguarded Newton steps.
The positivity of \(\Re C\) gives a natural monotonicity certificate.

\item \textbf{Entropy and determinant constants.} Numerically evaluate
\(\mathcal S_\rho\), compare it with products of exact \(1-\alpha_n^2\), and use integer-
relation searches only after rigorous tail bounds are available.

\item \textbf{CMV/Jacobi scattering.} Treat the Rvachev Jacobi and CMV matrices as compact or
slowly varying perturbations of the free models. The endpoint-flatness law should appear in
reflection coefficients and spectral phase shifts.

\item \textbf{Base-\(b\) and \(q\)-Rvachev lifts.} For generalized products with scales
\(b^{-k}\) or exponent sequences, construct the analogous circle lift, identify the
Carath\'eodory function, and compare rationality/algebraicity of its Schur parameters.

\item \textbf{Monodromy categories.} Replace the binary finite spline by finite
\(q\)-binomial or digit-weighted splines. Their logarithmic resolvents should carry a
monodromy representation of the corresponding automatic word.

\item \textbf{De Branges and Hermite interpolation.} The integer zero divisor has unbounded
multiplicity. Build a complete interpolation basis adapted to the values of
\(\Phi^{(r)}(n)\) for \(0\le r\le\TwoAdicValuation(n)\), rather than ordinary simple-node Shannon
sampling.

\item \textbf{Bessel sum-rule algebra.} Organize \eqref{p7:eq:Bessel-integer-zero} and its
higher derivatives as identities in the Bessel differential algebra; search for finite
closed combinations at powers of two.

\item \textbf{Mellin matching.} Match the unit-circle phase representation to the repository's
Mellin/Newton and Lambert--\(W\) endpoint expansions, obtaining uniform approximations from
the center to the boundary.

\item \textbf{Formal verification.} The moment recurrence, exact Chebyshev transform,
Carath\'eodory series, and contour zero count are suitable for Lean formalization. The
analytic boundary and Szeg\H{o} portions would require more functional-analysis
infrastructure.

\item \textbf{Interval numerics.} Recompute the figures with ball arithmetic, certify the
Schur parameters and Bessel identities, and establish explicit truncation bounds for the
phase series and Abel product.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Conclusion}
\label{p7:sec:conclusion}

The audited repository already contains a remarkably broad representation theory: random
series, splines, Thue--Morse coefficients, sinc and canonical products, exact moments,
Bernoulli/Bell structure, resolvents, logarithmic finite transforms, determinant and
continued-fraction forms, multiresolution expansions, integral transforms, inverse-function
identities, and endpoint asymptotics. The main advance here is therefore not another
isolated formula, but a new organizing bridge.

The probability--Chebyshev moments \(c_n\) lift the Rvachev law to the circle. From that lift,
one analytic function \(C\) simultaneously yields the density, the Fabius phase series, the
inverse phase equation, the Joukowski Cauchy transform, Schur and CMV data, and Toeplitz
products. The same coefficients turn the infinite sinc and hyperbolic products into ordinary
and modified Bessel series. On the arithmetic side, the integer zero divisor of \(\Phi\)
recovers the complete binary digit sum by the argument principle; finite logarithmic
resolvents recover Thue--Morse words by monodromy; and Abel regularization gives a smooth
finite-product representation.

These bridges place the Rvachev--Fabius system at an intersection of probability,
Fourier analysis, special functions, OPUC, operator theory, complex monodromy, automatic
sequences, and dyadic asymptotics. The conjectures above are concrete enough for exact
computation and theoretical attack, yet broad enough to connect several currently separate
campaigns in the repository.

\appendix

\section{Proof details and convergence notes}
\label{p7:app:proofs}

\subsection{Smoothness of the circle weight}

Near \(\theta=0\), \(1-\cos\theta\asymp\theta^2\). Since \(\rho\) and all its derivatives
vanish at \(x=1\), the composite \(\rho(\cos\theta)|\sin\theta|\) and all its derivatives tend
to zero at the origin. The same holds at \(\pi\) and \(2\pi\). This justifies the
\(C^\infty\) periodic extension used in \cref{p7:thm:boundary-rho}, hence the superalgebraic
decay of \(c_n\).

\subsection{Local uniform convergence of Bessel series}

For \(|z|\le R\), the standard bound \(|J_n(z)|\le (R/2)^n/n!\,e^{R^2/4}\) is sufficient.
Since \(|c_n|\le1\), the series in \eqref{p7:eq:Phi-Bessel} converges uniformly on compact sets.
An analogous factorial-in-order bound for \(I_n\) proves local uniform convergence of
\eqref{p7:eq:M-Bessel}. Termwise differentiation follows from Weierstrass convergence.

\subsection{Log-integrability of the circle weight}

The established endpoint analysis of \(\rho(1-h)\) has logarithm growing on the scale of a
quadratic or mildly corrected quadratic polynomial in \(\log(1/h)\). With
\(h\asymp\theta^2\), any finite power of \(\log(1/\theta)\) is integrable near zero:
\[
 \int_0^\varepsilon \bigl|\log^k\theta\bigr|\Differential\theta<\infty.
\]
The extra factor \(|\sin\theta|\) contributes only \(\log|\theta|\). Therefore
\(\log W\in L^1\), as required by Szeg\H{o}'s theorem.

\subsection{A direct expression for the circle measure}

For any continuous \(h\) on \(\T\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\T}h(\zeta)\Differential\nu(\zeta)
 =\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{h(e^{\ImaginaryUnit\arccos x})+h(e^{-\ImaginaryUnit\arccos x})}{2}
 \rho(x)\Differential x.
 \label{p7:eq:nu-pushforward}
\end{equation}
This formula is often the fastest route for transporting real-line identities to the circle.

% ed.: arabic section numbering restored (the standalone wave-2
% volume let \appendix lettering run across part boundaries)
\setcounter{section}{0}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\arabic{section}}
\part{Dyadic Multiresolution and Sampling Frontiers for the Fabius--Rvachev System}
% Absorbed from Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations/Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations.tex




\begin{abstract}
The bounded Fabius distribution function $F$, Rvachev's compactly supported
function $\RvachevUp$, and the entire sinc product that is the Fourier transform of
$\RvachevUp$ possess unusually many mutually illuminating descriptions.  This report
first audits all $92$ TeX paths recursively present under
\path{Analysis/FabiusFunction/docs} at the pinned repository commit.  Existing
probability, convolution, functional--differential, Volterra, finite
Thue--Morse spline, $q$-binomial, Fourier product, canonical-product,
Fourier--Legendre, half-integer cosine, moment, Lambert--$W$, Bell-polynomial,
and inverse-quantile representations are catalogued as prior corpus material,
not reannounced as discoveries.

The main new theorem package is dyadic and multiresolution in nature.  Exact
rational values of $F$ at dyadic points give a uniformly convergent
Faber--Schauder expansion with rational coefficients, an exact-rational
piecewise-affine limit for the inverse Fabius function, a Haar expansion of
the density, and a locally uniformly convergent Haar--$\SincRad^2$ expansion of
the infinite sinc product.  Haar Parseval then gives a positive rational series
for
\[
 A_2=\int_0^1F(x)^2\Differential x
    =\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^\infty
      \prod_{j\ge1}\SincRad^2\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right)\Differential t,
\]
together with an all-orders dyadic tail expansion.  This turns an arithmetically
opaque sinc integral into monotone exact rational approximants and produces a
high-order acceleration yielding about sixty stable decimal digits from level
$16$ dyadic data.

Several additional representation families are derived.  Expanding every
uniform coordinate into fair binary digits reorganizes the Fabius random
series into independent binomial layers and proves the product identity
\[
 \prod_{j\ge1}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right)
 =\prod_{n\ge2}\cos\!\left(\frac{t}{2^n}\right)^{n-1}.
\]
Paley--Wiener sampling reconstructs the same entire function solely from its
odd $\pi$-lattice samples; the known even zero lattice then yields a hierarchy
of new sample sum rules.  Chebyshev and Gegenbauer coefficients become
Bessel transforms of the sinc product.  Dyadic Bernstein polynomials give
exact-rational polynomial limits for $F$ and $\RvachevUp$, while their Fourier images
are finite sums of Kummer functions.  Finally, finite approximants are
identified with sublevel volumes and hyperplane sections of weighted cubes.
Proofs, status labels, conjectures, a Lean formalization roadmap, and a fully
commented Python verification program are included.
\end{abstract}


\clearpage

\section{Audit boundary, status discipline, and contribution map}
\label{p8:sec:audit}

\subsection{Pinned recursive corpus}

The source boundary of this report is the Git commit
\begin{center}
\nolinkurl{f7daf632f2db839fe091de0d080e22a9b50c286a}
\end{center}
of Vladimir Reshetnikov's \repo{} repository~\cite{p8:proveit}.  The audit root is
\path{Analysis/FabiusFunction/docs}.  A previously reconstructed pinned Git
manifest contained $87$ recursive TeX paths.  During the audit the branch
advanced by $25$ commits; pinning the new head and comparing trees revealed
five added TeX paths and no removals.  Thus the current boundary contains
exactly $92$ TeX paths.  The complete list, the five additions, and the
modified-path ledger are included as
\path{corpus_manifest_current.txt} in the accompanying archive.

The five additions are three large integral/transform manuscripts and two
generated numerical fragments.  They materially change the novelty boundary:
finite and complex-power antiderivatives, fractional Volterra calculus,
quantile beta transforms, Cauchy--Stieltjes hierarchies, inverse-Fabius
resolvents, autocorrelations, order statistics, and a rational Jacobi
continued fraction were already present by the pinned commit.  Those results
are therefore baseline here.

The audit did not treat archived revisions as independent corroboration.
Historical copies were read for mathematically distinctive material, then
deduplicated by lineage.  Imported TeX transcriptions of the papers of Arias
de Reyna and of work on lacunary products were included in the collision
search.  Targeted repository searches were also made for the exact phrases
and formula shapes used below.  No occurrence was found for the Faber--Schauder
midpoint-defect expansion, the rational Haar energy series, the triangular
independent-binomial decomposition, the odd-sample cardinal formula, the
Chebyshev--Bessel sinc integral, the dyadic Bernstein--Kummer transform, or the
weighted-cube section representation.

\subsection{Meaning of the status labels}

Three labels are used throughout.

\begin{itemize}
\item \baseline means that the result, perhaps in a different normalization,
      is explicit in the audited corpus or one of its imported sources.
\item \newresult means that a proof is supplied here and that no explicit
      counterpart was located in the pinned corpus after formula-level
      searches.  It is not a claim of absolute publication priority.
\item \conjstatus means that no proof is claimed.  Exact computations and
      structural evidence are stated separately from the conjecture.
\end{itemize}

This distinction is especially important in the Fabius project: a new
representation may be an elementary consequence of deep existing identities,
while a visually similar formula may fail because it suppresses the
nonconstant periodic phase in the endpoint asymptotics.

\subsection{Existing material deliberately excluded from novelty claims}

The following are treated as established inputs.

\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.28\textwidth}p{0.66\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Layer & Audited prior material \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Layer & Audited prior material \\
\midrule
\endhead
Basic functions & Separation of the bounded CDF $F$, the compactly supported even bump $\RvachevUp$, and the signed global extension $\FabiusGlobal$; uniqueness, smoothness, support, symmetry, strict shape, and flat endpoints.\\
Probability & Weighted sums of independent uniform coordinates, finite atomic and spline approximants, weak convergence, exact support bounds, and reflection.\\
Arithmetic & Rational moments, reciprocal-power values $F(2^{-n})$, arbitrary dyadic evaluation, Thue--Morse formulas, $q$-Pochhammer and Gaussian-binomial forms, denominator and $2$-adic information.\\
Fourier analysis & Infinite sinc and cosine products, Fourier inversion, Poisson and half-integer sampling identities in physical space, integer zeros with multiplicity $1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$, canonical products, sharp real-axis decay, spectral zeta and heat trace.\\
Approximation & Finite Thue--Morse splines, repeated integration, Fourier--Legendre expansion, exact Strang--Fix reproduction, positive quadrature, and Richardson acceleration of truncated products.\\
Integral transforms & Mellin, Laplace, Fourier, Cauchy, Stieltjes, logarithmic, correlation, spacing, order-statistic, inverse-quantile, and fractional-resolvent transforms.\\
Endpoint theory & Correct lower-Lambert coordinate, nonconstant periodic Gamma--zeta correction, all-order Bell-polynomial expansion, inverse-quantile reversion, and phase-aware Richardson extraction.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\subsection{New theorem package}

The report proves the following linked results.

\begin{enumerate}
\item An exact rational Faber--Schauder expansion of $F$, with sharp
      $4^{-J}$ interpolation scale, sign symmetry, and curvature recovery;
      inversion gives exact-rational piecewise-affine approximants to $G=F^{-1}$.
\item A rational Haar expansion of $F'$ and $\RvachevUp$, and a corresponding
      Haar--$\SincRad^2$ series for the entire Fourier transform.
\item A positive rational series for $A_2=\int_0^1F^2$, a certified geometric
      tail bound, and a complete asymptotic expansion of its dyadic level
      energies and tails.
\item An independent-binomial layer representation obtained by regrouping
      the binary digits of the uniform coordinates, including a finite
      combinatorial CDF and probability polynomial.
\item An odd-$\pi$-lattice Shannon reconstruction of the sinc product and a
      hierarchy of sample identities forced by the higher even-zero
      multiplicities.
\item Chebyshev and Gegenbauer expansions whose coefficients are
      Bessel transforms of the sinc product; the Legendre specialization
      turns known rational coefficients into exact Bessel-product integrals.
\item Exact-rational dyadic Bernstein limits and finite Kummer-function
      formulas for their Fourier transforms.
\item Weighted-cube sublevel-volume and hyperplane-section limits unifying
      the probabilistic, spline, and Fourier-slice pictures.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Normalization and established representation atlas}
\label{p8:sec:atlas}

\subsection{The three functions}

Let $F:\RealNumbers\to[0,1]$ denote the bounded Fabius distribution function~\cite{p8:fabius1966,p8:arias1982,p8:arias2018}:
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=0\quad(x\le0),\qquad
 F(x)=1\quad(x\ge1),                                      \label{p8:eq:F-boundary}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=2F(2x)\quad(0<x<\tfrac12),
 \qquad F(x)+F(1-x)=1\quad(0\le x\le1).                  \label{p8:eq:F-basic}
\end{equation}
Rvachev's function is~\cite{p8:rvachev1971}
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(y)=
 \begin{cases}
   F(1-|y|),& |y|\le1,\\
   0,& |y|>1.
 \end{cases}                                             \label{p8:eq:up-definition}
\end{equation}
It is even, nonnegative, $C^\infty$, supported on $[-1,1]$, and normalized by
$\int\RvachevUp=1$.  The conversion formulas are
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1),\qquad
 F(x)=\RvachevUp(x-1)\quad(0\le x\le1),                         \label{p8:eq:F-up-conversion}
\end{equation}
and, for $0\le x\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=1-F(x)=F(1-x).                                   \label{p8:eq:up-survival}
\end{equation}

The signed global continuation $\FabiusGlobal$ satisfies
$\FabiusGlobal'(x)=2\FabiusGlobal(2x)$ on all of $\RealNumbers$ and agrees with $F$ on
$[0,1]$.  It is indispensable for global derivative masks, but the new
multiresolution formulas below concern the bounded $F$ and compactly supported
$\RvachevUp$.  We never silently substitute one global convention for another.

\subsection{Probability and infinite convolution}

\baseline Let $U_1,U_2,\ldots$ be independent uniform random variables on
$[0,1]$~\cite{p8:jessen1935} and let $V_j=2U_j-1$, uniform on $[-1,1]$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 X=\sum_{j=1}^\infty\frac{U_j}{2^j},
 \qquad
 Y=2X-1=\sum_{j=1}^\infty\frac{V_j}{2^j},                \label{p8:eq:random-series}
\end{equation}
$F(x)=\Probability(X\le x)$, and $Y$ has density $\RvachevUp$.  Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp=\mathop{\ast}_{j=1}^{\infty}
 \left(2^{j-1}\IndicatorOf{[-2^{-j},2^{-j}]}
 \right)              \label{p8:eq:infinite-convolution}
\end{equation}
in the sense of weak limits and, in fact, in the smooth-density topology
available from the corpus.

The survival interpretation
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(t)=\Probability(X>t),\qquad 0\le t\le1,                    \label{p8:eq:survival-probability}
\end{equation}
immediately gives, for absolutely continuous $H$,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1H'(t)\RvachevUp(t)\Differential t
   &=\Expectation[H(X)-H(0)],                                      \label{p8:eq:master-survival}\\
 \int_0^1H'(t)F(t)\Differential t
   &=H(1)-\Expectation H(X).                                       \label{p8:eq:master-cdf}
\end{align}
This is the compact master identity behind many moment, Laplace, logarithmic,
and resolvent integrals in the audited transform reports.

\subsection{Functional--differential and Volterra forms}

\baseline Integrating \eqref{p8:eq:F-basic} gives the primitive self-similarity
\begin{equation}
 F(x/2)=\int_0^xF(t)\Differential t,
 \qquad 0\le x\le1.                                     \label{p8:eq:volterra-basic}
\end{equation}
Repeated integration produces exact primitive ladders.  The compact bump
satisfies the global functional--differential equation
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp'(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x+1)-2\RvachevUp(2x-1),                         \label{p8:eq:up-differential}
\end{equation}
and all higher derivatives are finite translates with Thue--Morse signs.
The current corpus extends these identities to integer and fractional
Volterra orders and to broad arbitrary-kernel transmutation formulas.

\subsection{Fourier, Laplace, product, and canonical-product forms}

We use the angular Fourier convention
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(t)=\int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(y)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit ty}\Differential y.           \label{p8:eq:Phi-definition}
\end{equation}
The cycles-per-unit transform in the repository is
$\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(\xi)=\Phi(2\pi\xi)$.  Throughout,
$\SincRad z=\sin z/z$ with $\SincRad0=1$.

\baseline Independence in \eqref{p8:eq:random-series} gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Phi(t)=\prod_{j=1}^\infty\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right).}
                                                               \label{p8:eq:sinc-product}
\end{equation}
The moment-generating function of $X$ is therefore
\begin{equation}
 M_X(s)=\Expectation\EulerE^{sX}
 =\prod_{j=1}^\infty\frac{\EulerE^{s/2^j}-1}{s/2^j}
 =\EulerE^{s/2}\Phi(\ImaginaryUnit s/2).                                \label{p8:eq:mgf-product}
\end{equation}
Its logarithm yields Bernoulli cumulants and Bell-polynomial moments.

The complete complex zero divisor is the nonzero lattice $2\pi\IntegerNumbers$, with
multiplicity $1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$ at $2\pi n$.  Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(t)=\prod_{n=1}^\infty
 \left(1-\frac{t^2}{4\pi^2n^2}\right)^{1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)},     \label{p8:eq:canonical-product}
\end{equation}
where the symmetric genus-zero product converges normally.  The corpus
contains the associated spectral zeta, determinant, heat trace, zero jets,
and sharp real-axis decay theory.

Fourier inversion gives
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(y)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
          \Phi(t)\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit ty}\Differential t,                       \label{p8:eq:up-fourier-inversion}
\end{equation}
and symmetry gives the CDF representation
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\frac12+\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty
       \Phi(t)\frac{\sin((2x-1)t)}{t}\Differential t,
 \qquad 0<x<1.                                           \label{p8:eq:F-sine-integral}
\end{equation}
The decay is faster than every power, so these are ordinary convergent
integrals rather than distributional shorthand.

The period-two extension of $\RvachevUp$ has only the constant and odd Fourier modes:
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(y)=\frac12+\sum_{m=0}^\infty
   \Phi((2m+1)\pi)\cos((2m+1)\pi y),\qquad |y|\le1.      \label{p8:eq:physical-cosine-series}
\end{equation}
This physical-space cosine series is established corpus material.  The
frequency-space odd-sample reconstruction in \cref{p8:sec:shannon} is a distinct,
dual statement.

\subsection{Finite splines, exact dyadic arithmetic, and limits}

\baseline Put $X_N=\sum_{j=1}^N2^{-j}U_j$ and let $F_N$ be its CDF.  Inclusion--exclusion
for a weighted box gives the finite Thue--Morse spline
\begin{equation}
 F_N(x)=\frac{2^{N(N+1)/2}}{N!}
 \sum_{k=0}^{2^N-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(k)}
 \left(x-\frac{k}{2^N}\right)_+^N.                      \label{p8:eq:finite-spline}
\end{equation}
Because $0\le X-X_N\le2^{-N}$,
\begin{equation}
 F_N(x-2^{-N})\le F(x)\le F_N(x).                        \label{p8:eq:spline-bracket}
\end{equation}
This is simultaneously a probability limit, a piecewise-polynomial limit,
and a finite alternating truncated-power representation.

Every dyadic value is rational.  The reciprocal-power values
$V_n=F(2^{-n})$ obey
\begin{equation}
 V_0=1,
 \qquad
 V_n=\frac{1}{2^{\binom n2}(2^n-1)}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
 \frac{2^{\binom k2}V_k}{(n-k+1)!}.                      \label{p8:eq:V-recurrence}
\end{equation}
The repository further supplies finite Thue--Morse sums, $q$-Pochhammer and
Gaussian-binomial forms, an executable highest-bit recursion for arbitrary
$F(a/2^e)$, and exact denominator and valuation theorems.  Those exact values
will become the coefficients of the new Faber and Haar representations.

\subsection{Moment, orthogonal-polynomial, and endpoint forms}

\baseline The even moments
\begin{equation}
 \mu_{2r}=\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2r}\RvachevUp(y)\Differential y
\end{equation}
are rational and satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(t)=\sum_{r=0}^\infty(-1)^r\mu_{2r}\frac{t^{2r}}{(2r)!}.  \label{p8:eq:moment-series}
\end{equation}
The even cumulants are
\begin{equation}
 \kappa_{2r}=\frac{2^{2r-1}B_{2r}}{r(2^{2r}-1)},
 \qquad \kappa_{2r+1}=0,                                \label{p8:eq:cumulants}
\end{equation}
and complete Bell polynomials convert the cumulants into the moments.
The corpus also proves an absolutely and uniformly convergent
Fourier--Legendre expansion of $\RvachevUp$ with exact rational coefficients.

At the flat endpoints, the natural variable is the exact lower-Lambert
coordinate~\cite{p8:corless1996}
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x)=-\frac{1}{\log2}
 W_{-1}(-x\log2),
 \qquad \lambda2^{-\lambda}=x.                           \label{p8:eq:lower-lambert}
\end{equation}
The full endpoint expansion of $F$ contains a nonconstant one-periodic
Gamma--zeta term in this coordinate, and its coefficients are generated by
Bell-polynomial algebra.  The inverse function $G=F^{-1}$ inherits a
phase-aware Lambert reversion.  These subtle asymptotics are baseline; below
we formulate only new multiresolution questions that retain, rather than
average away, this periodic phase.

\subsection{A compact dictionary of established representations}

For later comparison, \cref{p8:tab:atlas} records the principal established
forms in one place.

\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.22\textwidth}p{0.36\textwidth}p{0.34\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{Established representation atlas used as input.}\label{p8:tab:atlas}\\
\toprule
Object & Representation & Natural use \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Object & Representation & Natural use \\
\midrule
\endhead
$F$ & CDF of $\sum2^{-j}U_j$ & Monotonicity, coupling, quantiles, stochastic integrals.\\
$\RvachevUp$ & Density of $\sum2^{-j}V_j$ & Symmetry, convolution, support, probability.\\
$F$ & $F(x/2)=\int_0^xF$ & Primitive ladders and fractional Volterra calculus.\\
$\RvachevUp$ & Infinite convolution of uniform boxes & Smoothness and finite spline approximants.\\
$F_N$ & Finite Thue--Morse truncated-power sum & Exact computation and certified limits.\\
$F(a/2^e)$ & Rational recurrence, bit recursion, $q$-binomial sums & Arithmetic and exact dyadic evaluation.\\
$\Phi$ & Infinite sinc product & Global Fourier analysis and decay.\\
$\Phi$ & Canonical product over $2\pi\IntegerNumbers$ with multiplicities & Zero spectrum, determinants, zeta and heat trace.\\
$\RvachevUp$ & Fourier inversion integral & Arbitrary real arguments and analytic estimates.\\
$F$ & Sine-integral CDF inversion & Arbitrary arguments and endpoint cancellation.\\
$\RvachevUp$ & Period-two odd cosine series & Physical-space spectral approximation.\\
$\RvachevUp$ & Fourier--Legendre series & Orthogonal approximation with rational coefficients.\\
$M_X$ & Infinite exponential quotient product & Moments, cumulants, Laplace asymptotics.\\
$F,G$ & Mellin, Stieltjes, beta-quantile, spacing transforms & Integral calculus and inverse-function identities.\\
$F,G$ & Lower-Lambert periodic transseries & Sharp endpoint and inverse-endpoint analysis.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\section{Exact rational Faber--Schauder representation}
\label{p8:sec:faber}

\subsection{Dyadic tents and midpoint defects}

Following the classical Haar--Faber--Schauder multiresolution lineage~\cite{p8:haar1910,p8:faber1910,p8:schauder1928,p8:deboor2001}, for $m\ge0$ and $0\le k<2^m$, put
\begin{equation}
 \ell_{m,k}=\frac{k}{2^m},\qquad
 r_{m,k}=\frac{k+1}{2^m},\qquad
 \xi_{m,k}=\frac{2k+1}{2^{m+1}},\qquad
 h_m=2^{-m-1}.                                           \label{p8:eq:faber-cell}
\end{equation}
The normalized tent on this cell is
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_{m,k}(x)=
 \max\!\left(1-2^{m+1}|x-\xi_{m,k}|,0\right).            \label{p8:eq:faber-tent}
\end{equation}
Its support is $[\ell_{m,k},r_{m,k}]$, its height is one,
and its area is $h_m$.  Define the midpoint defect
\begin{equation}
 c_{m,k}=F(\xi_{m,k})-\frac{F(\ell_{m,k})+F(r_{m,k})}{2}. \label{p8:eq:faber-coefficient}
\end{equation}
Every argument in \eqref{p8:eq:faber-coefficient} is dyadic, so
\begin{equation}
 c_{m,k}\in\RationalNumbers.                                           \label{p8:eq:faber-rational}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Exact rational Faber--Schauder series]
\label{p8:thm:faber-series}
For $J\ge0$, let
\begin{equation}
 S_JF(x)=x+\sum_{m=0}^{J-1}\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
              c_{m,k}\Lambda_{m,k}(x).                   \label{p8:eq:faber-partial}
\end{equation}
Then $S_JF$ is exactly the piecewise-linear interpolant of $F$ on the dyadic
mesh $2^{-J}\IntegerNumbers\cap[0,1]$.  Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F(x)=x+\sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 c_{m,k}\Lambda_{m,k}(x)}                               \label{p8:eq:faber-series}
\end{equation}
uniformly on $[0,1]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The level-zero affine interpolant through $(0,F(0))=(0,0)$ and
$(1,F(1))=(1,1)$ is $x$.  Passing from the mesh of level $m$ to that of level
$m+1$ leaves all old nodes fixed and introduces one midpoint in each cell.
The unique correction supported on that cell, zero at the endpoints and
having the required midpoint value, is precisely
$c_{m,k}\Lambda_{m,k}$.  Induction proves the interpolation statement.
Uniform convergence follows from uniform continuity of $F$, since the error
of linear interpolation on a mesh of diameter $2^{-J}$ is bounded by twice
the modulus of continuity at that scale.  The stronger quantitative bound in
\cref{p8:thm:faber-error} follows from $F\in C^2$.
\end{proof}

\newresult The point of \cref{p8:thm:faber-series} is not the abstract existence
of a Schauder expansion; it is that every coefficient is an explicitly
computable rational number supplied by the repository's exact dyadic
arithmetic.  The partial sums are therefore exact rational piecewise-linear
functions, not floating-point projections.

\begin{corollary}[Rvachev form]
\label{p8:cor:up-faber}
For $|y|\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \RvachevUp(y)=1-|y|+
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 c_{m,k}\Lambda_{m,k}(1-|y|).}                           \label{p8:eq:up-faber}
\end{equation}
The series is uniformly convergent on $[-1,1]$ and is zero outside that
interval after the usual compact-support extension.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $x=1-|y|$ in \eqref{p8:eq:faber-series} and use
\eqref{p8:eq:up-definition}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Coefficient kernel, symmetry, signs, and curvature}

\begin{proposition}[Triangular-kernel formula]
\label{p8:prop:faber-kernel}
The midpoint defect has the exact integral representation
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 c_{m,k}=-\frac12\int_{-h_m}^{h_m}
 (h_m-|u|)F''(\xi_{m,k}+u)\Differential u.}                        \label{p8:eq:faber-kernel}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 |c_{m,k}|\le2^{-2m}.                                    \label{p8:eq:faber-bound}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The second symmetric difference satisfies
\[
 F(\xi+h)+F(\xi-h)-2F(\xi)
 =\int_{-h}^{h}(h-|u|)F''(\xi+u)\Differential u.
\]
Equation \eqref{p8:eq:faber-kernel} follows from the definition of $c_{m,k}$.
Moreover, $\|F''\|_\infty=8$: from
$F'(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)$ one has $\|F'\|_\infty=2$, and on the
left half $F''(x)=4F'(2x)$, with the reflected formula on the right half.
Since the triangular kernel has integral $h_m^2$, we obtain
$|c_{m,k}|\le4h_m^2=2^{-2m}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Reflection and sign pattern]
\label{p8:prop:faber-sign}
For every $m,k$,
\begin{equation}
 c_{m,2^m-1-k}=-c_{m,k}.                                 \label{p8:eq:faber-reflection}
\end{equation}
Moreover, $c_{0,0}=0$, and for $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 c_{m,k}<0\quad(0\le k<2^{m-1}),
 \qquad
 c_{m,k}>0\quad(2^{m-1}\le k<2^m).                     \label{p8:eq:faber-sign}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Reflection follows by applying $F(1-x)=1-F(x)$ to the three nodes in
\eqref{p8:eq:faber-coefficient}.  On $(0,1/2)$,
$F''(x)=4F'(2x)>0$, so $F$ is strictly convex and every midpoint lies strictly
below its chord.  On $(1/2,1)$ the reflected function is strictly concave.
The level-zero defect vanishes because $F(1/2)=1/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Curvature recovery]
\label{p8:prop:faber-curvature}
Uniformly in $k$ as $m\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 -2^{2m+3}c_{m,k}
 =F''(\xi_{m,k})+\BigO(4^{-m}).                          \label{p8:eq:faber-curvature}
\end{equation}
More generally, for every fixed $Q\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 c_{m,k}=-\sum_{r=0}^{Q}
 \frac{F^{(2r+2)}(\xi_{m,k})h_m^{2r+2}}{(2r+2)!}
 +\BigO(h_m^{2Q+4}),                                     \label{p8:eq:faber-curvature-full}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $k$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Insert the Taylor expansion of $F''(\xi+u)$ into
\eqref{p8:eq:faber-kernel}.  Odd powers integrate to zero and
\[
 \int_{-h}^{h}(h-|u|)u^{2r}\Differential u
 =\frac{2h^{2r+2}}{(2r+1)(2r+2)}.
\]
The compact interval and smoothness of $F$ make the remainder uniform.
\end{proof}

The coefficients therefore encode exact dyadic samples of curvature.  This
is especially suggestive near the flat endpoints, where the repository's
Lambert--periodic asymptotics can be sampled through a purely rational
sequence.

\subsection{Sharp interpolation scale}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform error and sharp asymptotic constant]
\label{p8:thm:faber-error}
For every $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
