 \|F-S_JF\|_\infty\le4^{-J}.                             \label{p8:eq:faber-error-bound}
\end{equation}
The constant is asymptotically sharp:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \lim_{J\to\infty}4^J\|F-S_JF\|_\infty=1.}              \label{p8:eq:faber-error-limit}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On an interval of length $\Delta=2^{-J}$, the classical linear-interpolation
remainder gives
\[
 |F(x)-S_JF(x)|\le\frac{\|F''\|_\infty}{8}\Delta^2
 =\Delta^2=4^{-J}.
\]
For the matching lower asymptotic, take the cell immediately to the right of
$x=1/4$ and evaluate at its midpoint.  The interpolation remainder is
$-F''(\eta_J)\Delta^2/8$ for some $\eta_J$ in that cell.  Since
$\eta_J\to1/4$ and $F''(1/4)=8$, its absolute value divided by $\Delta^2$
tends to one.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The estimate is stronger than a generic Faber--Schauder convergence theorem:
it identifies the exact global interpolation scale and its limiting constant.
The extremal curvature occurs at the dyadic points $1/4$ and $3/4$, so the
sharp constant is itself compatible with exact dyadic arithmetic.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Low-level exact coefficients}

The first nonzero rows illustrate both rationality and reflection:
\begin{align}
 (c_{1,k})_{k=0}^{1}
   &=\left(-\frac{13}{72},\frac{13}{72}\right),          \label{p8:eq:faber-row1}\\
 (c_{2,k})_{k=0}^{3}
   &=\left(-\frac1{32},-\frac1{32},\frac1{32},\frac1{32}\right), \label{p8:eq:faber-row2}\\
 (c_{3,k})_{k=0}^{7}
   &=\frac1{2073600}
     (-3457,-28943,-28943,-3457,3457,28943,28943,3457).  \label{p8:eq:faber-row3}
\end{align}
At level four, the left half is
\begin{equation}
 -\frac1{147456}(5,149,427,571,571,427,149,5),            \label{p8:eq:faber-row4}
\end{equation}
and the right half is its negative reversal.  These rows were generated from
\eqref{p8:eq:V-recurrence} and the terminating dyadic bit recursion; no numerical
integration entered.

\subsection{A universal kernel dictionary}

Uniform convergence permits termwise integration against every
$K\in L^1(0,1)$:
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1K(x)F(x)\Differential x
 =\int_0^1xK(x)\Differential x
 +\sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 c_{m,k}\int_0^1K(x)\Lambda_{m,k}(x)\Differential x.               \label{p8:eq:faber-kernel-dictionary}
\end{equation}
When $K$ belongs to a parameterized locally bounded family, the same identity
is locally uniform in the parameter.  Thus one exact coefficient table feeds
polynomial moments, Laplace transforms, Fourier transforms, Stieltjes
kernels, and fractional integral kernels.

For $p\in\NaturalNumbers$, define
\begin{equation}
 L_p(c,h)=2h\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{p/2}}
 \binom{p}{2r}\frac{c^{p-2r}h^{2r}}{(2r+1)(2r+2)}.       \label{p8:eq:tent-moment}
\end{equation}
This is exactly the moment of a tent:
\begin{equation}
 \int_{c-h}^{c+h}x^p\left(1-\frac{|x-c|}{h}\right)\Differential x
 =L_p(c,h).                                              \label{p8:eq:tent-moment-integral}
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \int_0^1x^pF(x)\Differential x
 =\frac1{p+2}+
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 c_{m,k}L_p(\xi_{m,k},h_m).}                             \label{p8:eq:faber-polynomial-moment}
\end{equation}
Every summand is rational.  This does not replace the more compact moment
recurrences in the corpus; it gives a new positive-scale decomposition whose
truncation error is controlled directly by \eqref{p8:eq:faber-error-bound}.

Let
\begin{equation}
 A(t)=\int_0^1F(x)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential x.                     \label{p8:eq:A-definition}
\end{equation}
The elementary tent transform is
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1\Lambda_{m,k}(x)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential x
 =h_m\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t\xi_{m,k}}
   \SincRad^2\!\left(\frac{th_m}{2}\right).                \label{p8:eq:tent-transform}
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 A(t)=\frac{\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t}(1+\ImaginaryUnit t)-1}{t^2}
 +\sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 c_{m,k}h_m\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t\xi_{m,k}}
 \SincRad^2\!\left(\frac{th_m}{2}\right),}                \label{p8:eq:F-faber-transform}
\end{equation}
with removable interpretation at $t=0$ and locally uniform convergence in
$t\in\ComplexNumbers$.  Integration by parts also gives the bridge
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(t)=\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t}+2\ImaginaryUnit t\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}A(2t).             \label{p8:eq:A-Phi-bridge}
\end{equation}
Replacing $t$ by $-\ImaginaryUnit s$ turns \eqref{p8:eq:F-faber-transform} into an entire
Laplace series with $\sinhc^2$ kernels.

\subsection{Exact-rational inverse interpolants}

Let $G=F^{-1}:[0,1]\to[0,1]$.  The inverse-frontier manuscripts in the
pinned corpus develop sharp endpoint reversion, inverse derivatives, and
quantile transforms.  The Faber construction supplies a complementary
finite exact representation.

For $J\ge0$ and $0\le k\le2^J$, put
\begin{equation}
 y_{J,k}=F(k/2^J),
 \qquad
 d_{J,k}=y_{J,k+1}-y_{J,k}>0
 \quad(0\le k<2^J).                                  \label{p8:eq:inverse-knots}
\end{equation}
Every $y_{J,k}$ and $d_{J,k}$ is rational.  Define $G_J$ on
$[y_{J,k},y_{J,k+1}]$ by
\begin{equation}
 G_J(y)=\frac{k}{2^J}
 +\frac{2^{-J}}{d_{J,k}}(y-y_{J,k}).                   \label{p8:eq:inverse-piecewise}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, with $s_{J,k}=2^{-J}/d_{J,k}$,
\begin{equation}
 G_J(y)=s_{J,0}y+
 \sum_{k=1}^{2^J-1}(s_{J,k}-s_{J,k-1})(y-y_{J,k})_+.  \label{p8:eq:inverse-hinge}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Exact-rational inverse limit]
\label{p8:thm:inverse-faber-limit}
The map $G_J$ is the inverse of the Faber partial sum $S_JF$.  It is a
strictly increasing piecewise-affine homeomorphism with rational knots,
rational slopes, and the reflection law
\begin{equation}
 G_J(1-y)=1-G_J(y).                                      \label{p8:eq:inverse-interpolant-symmetry}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 G(y)=\lim_{J\to\infty}G_J(y)}                       \label{p8:eq:inverse-faber-limit}
\end{equation}
uniformly on $[0,1]$.  If
\begin{equation}
 \omega_G(\delta)=
 \sup_{|u-v|\le\delta}|G(u)-G(v)|,                  \label{p8:eq:inverse-modulus}
\end{equation}
then the explicit estimate
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \|G_J-G\|_\infty\le\omega_G(4^{-J})}             \label{p8:eq:inverse-faber-error}
\end{equation}
holds.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The dyadic node values of $S_JF$ are $y_{J,k}$, and strict monotonicity of
$F$ makes every chord slope positive.  Thus \eqref{p8:eq:inverse-piecewise} is
exactly $(S_JF)^{-1}$; \eqref{p8:eq:inverse-hinge} is the standard
slope-jump form of the same piecewise-affine map.  Reflection of the dyadic
node table proves \eqref{p8:eq:inverse-interpolant-symmetry}.

Fix $y$ and put $x=G_J(y)$.  Since $S_JF(x)=y$ and
$\|S_JF-F\|_\infty\le4^{-J}$,
\[
 y-4^{-J}\le F(x)\le y+4^{-J},
\]
with the endpoints clipped to $[0,1]$.  Applying the increasing map $G$
places $x$ between the corresponding two quantiles, which gives
\eqref{p8:eq:inverse-faber-error}.  Uniform continuity of $G$ completes the
proof.
\end{proof}

\newresult Equations \eqref{p8:eq:inverse-piecewise} and
\eqref{p8:eq:inverse-hinge} give a finite exact-rational neural-spline or
truncated-power representation of the inverse at every level.  The sharp
Lambert-periodic endpoint expansion already in the corpus may be inserted
into $\omega_G(4^{-J})$ to resolve the non-algebraic global convergence rate.

\section{Rational Haar expansions and a Haar--sinc transform}
\label{p8:sec:haar}

\subsection{The derivative as a dyadic martingale limit}

Define the unnormalized Haar step
\begin{equation}
 H_{m,k}(x)=
 \IndicatorOf{[\ell_{m,k},\xi_{m,k})}(x)
 -\IndicatorOf{[\xi_{m,k},r_{m,k})}(x).                  \label{p8:eq:haar-step}
\end{equation}
Then $\Lambda'_{m,k}=2^{m+1}H_{m,k}$ away from its three
corners.

\begin{theorem}[Exact rational Haar representation]
\label{p8:thm:haar-Fprime}
The derivative of the Fabius function has the Haar expansion
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F'(x)=1+\sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 2^{m+1}c_{m,k}H_{m,k}(x).}                              \label{p8:eq:haar-Fprime}
\end{equation}
The partial sum through $m=J-1$ is the derivative of $S_JF$ and equals the
cell average of $F'$ on every dyadic cell of level $J$.  It converges to
$F'$ in $L^p(0,1)$ for every $1\le p\le\infty$, where $p=\infty$ is
understood in the essential-supremum sense.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the finite identity \eqref{p8:eq:faber-partial}.  On a cell
$[a,b]$ of the level-$J$ mesh, the derivative of the interpolant is
$[F(b)-F(a)]/(b-a)$, which is the average of $F'$ on that cell.  These
conditional averages converge uniformly to the continuous function $F'$ by
uniform continuity.  Values at the finitely many grid corners are irrelevant
in every $L^p$ space.
\end{proof}

Combining \eqref{p8:eq:haar-Fprime} with \eqref{p8:eq:F-up-conversion} yields
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \RvachevUp(2x-1)=\frac12+
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 2^m c_{m,k}H_{m,k}(x).}                                 \label{p8:eq:haar-up}
\end{equation}
Thus the compact $C^\infty$ bump is an $L^\infty$ limit of exact rational
step functions.  The apparent tension is useful: smoothness is encoded by
strong cancellation across infinitely many dyadic scales.

\subsection{Fourier transform of one Haar cell}

A direct calculation gives
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1H_{m,k}(x)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential x
 =\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit tk/2^m}
   \frac{(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t/2^{m+1}})^2}{\ImaginaryUnit t}.              \label{p8:eq:haar-transform}
\end{equation}
Apply this to \eqref{p8:eq:haar-up}, remembering that the centered coordinate is
$y=2x-1$.

\begin{theorem}[Haar--$\SincRad^2$ expansion of the sinc product]
\label{p8:thm:haar-Phi}
For every $t\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \Phi(t)
 &=\SincRad t+
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 \frac{2^m c_{m,k}}{\ImaginaryUnit t}
 \EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t(1-2k/2^m)}
 \left(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t/2^m}\right)^2,                     \label{p8:eq:haar-Phi-complex}\\
 &=\SincRad t-
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}
 \frac{2^{m+2}c_{m,k}}{\ImaginaryUnit t}
 \EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t y_{m,k}}
 \sin^2\!\left(\frac{t}{2^{m+1}}\right),              \label{p8:eq:haar-Phi-centered}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
 y_{m,k}=\frac{2k+1}{2^m}-1.                             \label{p8:eq:haar-center-y}
\end{equation}
Every apparent singularity at $t=0$ is removable, and both series converge
normally on compact subsets of $\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use
\[
 \Phi(t)=2\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}\int_0^1\RvachevUp(2x-1)\EulerE^{-2\ImaginaryUnit tx}\Differential x
\]
and insert the finite Haar partial sums.  Equation
\eqref{p8:eq:haar-transform} with frequency $2t$ gives
\eqref{p8:eq:haar-Phi-complex}; factoring
$(1-\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit a})^2=-4\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit a}\sin^2(a/2)$ gives
\eqref{p8:eq:haar-Phi-centered}.  For $t$ in a fixed compact set,
\eqref{p8:eq:faber-bound} and
$|\sin(t/2^{m+1})|\le C_K2^{-m}$ bound the entire level-$m$ contribution by
$C'_K4^{-m}$.  The Weierstrass test proves normal convergence.
\end{proof}

Pairing reflected coefficients gives a manifestly real formula on the real
axis.

\begin{corollary}[Real paired form]
\label{p8:cor:haar-Phi-real}
For real $t$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Phi(t)=\SincRad t-
 \sum_{m=1}^\infty\frac{2^{m+3}}{t}
 \sin^2\!\left(\frac{t}{2^{m+1}}\right)
 \sum_{k=0}^{2^{m-1}-1}c_{m,k}
 \sin\!\left(t\left[1-\frac{2k+1}{2^m}\right]\right).} \label{p8:eq:haar-Phi-real}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

The representation \eqref{p8:eq:haar-Phi-real} is a series--product bridge.  The
left side is the infinite sinc product; the right side consists of exact
rational curvature coefficients multiplied by elementary sine and
$\sin^2$ factors.  It gives a new way to transfer arithmetic information from
dyadic values of $F$ into the Fourier image.

\section{A positive rational series for the sinc energy}
\label{p8:sec:energy}

\subsection{Haar Parseval}

Let
\begin{equation}
 A_2=\int_0^1F(x)^2\Differential x.                                \label{p8:eq:A2-definition}
\end{equation}
The audited integral report identifies the same constant with a sinc-product
energy, but leaves its arithmetic nature open.  The Haar representation gives
a monotone exact-rational decomposition.

\begin{theorem}[Positive rational energy series]
\label{p8:thm:A2-rational-series}
One has
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 A_2=\frac14+
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty2^m\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}c_{m,k}^2.}       \label{p8:eq:A2-rational-series}
\end{equation}
Every summand is a nonnegative rational number.  Hence the partial sums
\begin{equation}
 R_J=\frac14+
 \sum_{m=0}^{J-1}2^m\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}c_{m,k}^2          \label{p8:eq:Rj-definition}
\end{equation}
are rational and increase monotonically to $A_2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The Haar steps are pairwise orthogonal,
$\|H_{m,k}\|_2^2=2^{-m}$, and all have zero mean.  Parseval applied to
\eqref{p8:eq:haar-Fprime} yields
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1F'(x)^2\Differential x
 =1+\sum_{m=0}^\infty2^{m+2}\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}c_{m,k}^2. \label{p8:eq:Fprime-parseval}
\end{equation}
On the other hand, $F'(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)$ and evenness give
\[
 \int_0^1F'^2
 =2\int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(y)^2\Differential y
 =4\int_0^1F(x)^2\Differential x=4A_2.
\]
Divide \eqref{p8:eq:Fprime-parseval} by four.
\end{proof}

Angular Parseval and \eqref{p8:eq:sinc-product} give the three-way identity
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \frac14+\sum_{m\ge0}2^m\sum_kc_{m,k}^2
 =\int_0^1F(x)^2\Differential x
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^\infty
   \prod_{j=1}^\infty\SincRad^2\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right)\Differential t.} \label{p8:eq:A2-three-way}
\end{equation}
Thus an improper integral of a transcendental infinite product is represented
by a positive series of exact rational squares.

\begin{corollary}[Certified elementary tail bound]
\label{p8:cor:A2-tail-bound}
For every $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 0<A_2-R_J\le\frac43\,4^{-J}.                            \label{p8:eq:A2-tail-bound}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{p8:eq:faber-bound}, the level-$m$ energy is at most
\[
 2^m\cdot2^m\cdot2^{-4m}=4^{-m}.
\]
Sum the geometric tail from $m=J$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Derivative norm scaling}

Put
\begin{equation}
 I_r=\int_0^1\left(F^{(r)}(x)\right)^2\Differential x,
 \qquad r\ge1.                                           \label{p8:eq:Ir-definition}
\end{equation}
The derivative tiling already present in the corpus implies the exact norm
recurrence $I_r=4^rI_{r-1}$.  Since $I_1=4A_2$, this gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 I_r=4^{r(r+1)/2}A_2.}                                   \label{p8:eq:derivative-norm-scaling}
\end{equation}
We use this baseline identity to evaluate every coefficient in the new
energy-tail expansion.

\subsection{All-orders level asymptotics}

Define the contribution of one dyadic scale by
\begin{equation}
 E_m=2^m\sum_{k=0}^{2^m-1}c_{m,k}^2,
 \qquad A_2=\frac14+\sum_{m\ge0}E_m.                     \label{p8:eq:Em-definition}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Complete asymptotic expansion of one level]
\label{p8:thm:Em-asymptotic}
As $m\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 E_m\sim A_2\sum_{q=1}^\infty\beta_q4^{-qm},}            \label{p8:eq:Em-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \beta_q=(-1)^{q+1}
 \frac{2^{q(q+1)+1}(2^{2q}-1)}{(2q+2)!}.}                \label{p8:eq:beta-formula}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_1=1,\
 \beta_2=-\frac83,
 \qquad
 \beta_3=\frac{64}{5},
 \qquad
 \beta_4=-\frac{139264}{945}.                           \label{p8:eq:beta-first}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A detailed remainder proof is given in \cref{p8:app:energy-proof}; here is the
coefficient calculation.  Write $\Delta=2^{-m}$ and $h=\Delta/2$.  At a cell
center,
\[
 c_{m,k}=-\bigl(\cosh(hD)-1\bigr)F(\xi_{m,k}),
 \qquad D=\frac{\Differential}{\Differential x},
\]
in the sense of an arbitrary finite Taylor expansion with uniform remainder.
The midpoint Riemann sum differs from the corresponding integral by a
superalgebraically small quantity because all derivatives entering the
integrand are flat at both endpoints.  Hence
\[
 E_m\sim\Delta^{-2}\int_0^1
 \bigl[(\cosh(hD)-1)F(x)\bigr]^2\Differential x.
\]
Now
\begin{equation}
 (\cosh z-1)^2
 =\sum_{r=2}^\infty
   \frac{2^{2r-1}-2}{(2r)!}z^{2r}.                       \label{p8:eq:cosh-square}
\end{equation}
Repeated integration by parts gives
$\int F D^{2r}F=(-1)^rI_r$.  Substituting
$h=\Delta/2$, setting $q=r-1$, and using
\eqref{p8:eq:derivative-norm-scaling} produces
\eqref{p8:eq:beta-formula}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Leading level constant]
\label{p8:cor:Em-leading}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \lim_{m\to\infty}4^mE_m=A_2.}                           \label{p8:eq:Em-leading}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

The same constant therefore controls the original integral, the total Haar
energy, and the leading asymptotic energy at every sufficiently fine scale.
This self-referential feature is a consequence of the exact derivative norm
scaling.

\subsection{All-orders tail and acceleration}

Summing \eqref{p8:eq:Em-asymptotic} geometrically gives the tail expansion.

\begin{corollary}[Complete rational tail expansion]
\label{p8:cor:A2-tail-asymptotic}
For $J\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 A_2-R_J\sim A_2\sum_{q=1}^\infty\gamma_q4^{-qJ},        \label{p8:eq:A2-tail-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_q=\frac{\beta_q}{1-4^{-q}}.                     \label{p8:eq:gamma-definition}
\end{equation}
The first coefficients are
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_1=\frac43,
 \qquad
 \gamma_2=-\frac{128}{45},
 \qquad
 \gamma_3=\frac{4096}{315},
 \qquad
 \gamma_4=-\frac{2097152}{14175}.                       \label{p8:eq:gamma-first}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \lim_{J\to\infty}4^J(A_2-R_J)=\frac43A_2.}             \label{p8:eq:A2-tail-leading}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

For any fixed truncation order $Q$, define
\begin{equation}
 A_2^{[Q]}(J)=
 \frac{R_J}{1-\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\gamma_q4^{-qJ}}.            \label{p8:eq:A2-accelerator}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 A_2^{[Q]}(J)-A_2=\BigO(4^{-(Q+1)J})                    \label{p8:eq:A2-accelerator-error}
\end{equation}
for fixed $Q$.  The numerator and denominator in
\eqref{p8:eq:A2-accelerator} are rational, so every accelerated value is itself
an exact rational number before decimal conversion.

\numerical Exact level-$16$ data and seven tail coefficients give
\begin{equation}
 A_2\approx
 0.404436767983985281465621491145376633448000519787051529947065. \label{p8:eq:A2-decimal}
\end{equation}
The stable digits agree across neighboring values of $J$ and $Q$, and the raw
level-$16$ rational partial sum is
$0.4044367678584316\ldots$.  The numerical table and convergence plot in
\cref{p8:sec:numerics} show the acceleration explicitly.

\section{Triangular Bernoulli and independent-binomial layers}
\label{p8:sec:triangular}

\subsection{Regrouping the binary digits}

The uniform-coordinate representation \eqref{p8:eq:random-series} hides a second
independence structure.  Choose the nonterminating binary expansion only on
the null set where two choices exist, and write
\begin{equation}
 U_j=\sum_{r=1}^\infty B_{j,r}2^{-r},                    \label{p8:eq:uniform-bits}
\end{equation}
where all $B_{j,r}$ are independent Bernoulli$(1/2)$ variables.  The weight of
$B_{j,r}$ in $X$ depends only on $n=j+r$.

\begin{theorem}[Independent triangular binomial decomposition]
\label{p8:thm:triangular-binomial}
There exist independent random variables
\begin{equation}
 K_n\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(n-1,\tfrac12),
 \qquad n\ge2,                                           \label{p8:eq:Kn-law}
\end{equation}
such that
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 X=\sum_{n=2}^\infty\frac{K_n}{2^n}.}                    \label{p8:eq:X-triangular}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, if $S_{n-1}$ is a sum of $n-1$ independent Rademacher signs and
the sums are independent across $n$, then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 Y=2X-1=\sum_{n=2}^\infty\frac{S_{n-1}}{2^n}.}           \label{p8:eq:Y-triangular}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute \eqref{p8:eq:uniform-bits} into
$X=\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}U_j$ and rearrange the absolutely convergent nonnegative
series:
\[
 X=\sum_{j,r\ge1}B_{j,r}2^{-(j+r)}
  =\sum_{n\ge2}2^{-n}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}B_{j,n-j}.
\]
For fixed $n$ the inner sum has the law in \eqref{p8:eq:Kn-law}; different
$n$ use disjoint sets of bits and are independent.  Centering each binomial
sum proves \eqref{p8:eq:Y-triangular}.
\end{proof}

This is not merely another proof of the random-series representation.  It
changes the elementary building blocks from one uniform variable per scale to
one increasingly concentrated binomial variable per antidiagonal of the
binary digit array.

\subsection{A triangular cosine product}

\begin{corollary}[Product--product identity]
\label{p8:cor:triangular-product}
The moment-generating function and centered Fourier transform factor as
\begin{equation}
 M_X(s)=\prod_{n=2}^\infty
   \left(\frac{1+\EulerE^{s/2^n}}{2}\right)^{n-1}.            \label{p8:eq:triangular-mgf}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Phi(t)=\prod_{n=2}^\infty
   \cos\!\left(\frac{t}{2^n}\right)^{n-1}
 =\prod_{j=1}^\infty
   \SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right).}                 \label{p8:eq:triangular-cos-product}
\end{equation}
Both products converge normally on compact subsets of $\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The first two factorizations follow from independence in
\cref{p8:thm:triangular-binomial}.  For the last equality, use the classical
binary product
$\SincRad z=\prod_{r\ge1}\cos(z/2^r)$ and regroup the normally convergent double
product:
\[
 \prod_{j\ge1}\SincRad(t/2^j)
 =\prod_{j,r\ge1}\cos(t/2^{j+r})
 =\prod_{n\ge2}\cos(t/2^n)^{n-1}.
\]
\end{proof}

The logarithmic identity
\begin{equation}
 \log\Phi(t)=\sum_{n=2}^\infty(n-1)
              \log\cos(t/2^n)                           \label{p8:eq:triangular-log}
\end{equation}
reorganizes every cumulant as a weighted geometric sum.  It also offers a
different truncation strategy: the exponent $n-1$ concentrates many tiny
Rademacher increments into one layer.

Let
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_N^\triangle(t)=\prod_{n=2}^{N}
 \cos(t/2^n)^{n-1}.                                     \label{p8:eq:triangular-finite-product}
\end{equation}
The omitted factor is quantitatively controlled.

\begin{proposition}[Local triangular-product tail]
\label{p8:prop:triangular-tail}
For $|t|\le2^N$, write $\Phi(t)=\Phi_N^\triangle(t)T_N(t)$, where
$T_N(t)>0$ on the real axis.  Then
\begin{equation}
 0\le-\log T_N(t)
 \le \frac{(3N+1)t^2}{9\,4^N}.                           \label{p8:eq:triangular-tail}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $n>N$, $|t|/2^n\le1/2$, and
$0\le-\log\cos u\le u^2$ on $|u|\le1/2$.  Therefore
\[
 -\log T_N(t)
 \le t^2\sum_{n>N}(n-1)4^{-n}
 =\frac{(3N+1)t^2}{9\,4^N}.
\]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Finite combinatorial CDFs}

Define
\begin{equation}
 X_N^\triangle=\sum_{n=2}^{N}\frac{K_n}{2^n},
 \qquad
 \delta_N=\sum_{n>N}\frac{n-1}{2^n}=\frac{N+1}{2^N}.    \label{p8:eq:triangular-truncation}
\end{equation}
The tail lies in $[0,\delta_N]$, so
\begin{equation}
 \Probability(X_N^\triangle\le x-\delta_N)
 \le F(x)\le
 \Probability(X_N^\triangle\le x).                             \label{p8:eq:triangular-cdf-bracket}
\end{equation}
Expanding the independent binomial laws gives the finite sum
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F_N^\triangle(x)=2^{-N(N-1)/2}
 \sum_{k_2=0}^{1}\cdots\sum_{k_N=0}^{N-1}
 \left(\prod_{n=2}^{N}\binom{n-1}{k_n}\right)
 \IndicatorOf{\{\sum_{n=2}^{N}k_n2^{-n}\le x\}}.}     \label{p8:eq:triangular-cdf}
\end{equation}
Then $F_N^\triangle(x)\to F(x)$ at every real $x$.

The complete mass distribution is encoded by the integer polynomial
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 P_N(q)=\prod_{n=2}^{N}
       \left(1+q^{2^{N-n}}\right)^{n-1}.}                \label{p8:eq:triangular-polynomial}
\end{equation}
Its degree and total coefficient sum are
\begin{equation}
 \deg P_N=2^N-N-1,
 \qquad
 P_N(1)=2^{N(N-1)/2}.                                    \label{p8:eq:triangular-polynomial-data}
\end{equation}
If $p_{N,\ell}=[q^\ell]P_N(q)$, then
\begin{equation}
 \Probability(2^NX_N^\triangle=\ell)
 =2^{-N(N-1)/2}p_{N,\ell},                              \label{p8:eq:triangular-mass}
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 2^{-N(N-1)/2}
 \sum_{0\le\ell\le2^Nx}p_{N,\ell}.                    \label{p8:eq:triangular-coefficient-limit}
\end{equation}
This is a limit of cumulative coefficient sums of explicit sparse products.
It is complementary to the alternating Thue--Morse spline
\eqref{p8:eq:finite-spline}: one finite model is signed and polynomial in $x$;
the other is positive and combinatorial on a dyadic lattice.

Every factor in \eqref{p8:eq:triangular-polynomial} is palindromic, so the
coefficient sequence is symmetric.  Computation suggests substantially more.

\begin{conjecture}[Triangular log-concavity]
\label{p8:conj:triangular-logconcavity}
For every $N\ge2$, the coefficients of $P_N(q)$ are log-concave:
\begin{equation}
 p_{N,\ell}^2\ge p_{N,\ell-1}p_{N,\ell+1}
 \qquad(1\le\ell<2^N-N-1).                              \label{p8:eq:triangular-logconcavity}
\end{equation}
Consequently they are unimodal.
\end{conjecture}

\numerical The bundled program verifies
\eqref{p8:eq:triangular-logconcavity} in exact integer arithmetic for every
$2\le N\le16$.  The largest checked polynomial has degree $65519$ and total
mass $2^{120}$.  This is evidence, not a proof: sparse dilation products do
not inherit log-concavity from their factors by a routine closure theorem.

\section{Odd-lattice reconstruction of the Fourier image}
\label{p8:sec:shannon}

\subsection{Paley--Wiener sampling}

Because $\RvachevUp\in C_c^\infty([-1,1])$, its transform satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \Phi\in\PW_1\cap\mathscr S(\RealNumbers):                        \label{p8:eq:Phi-PW}
\end{equation}
$\Phi$ is entire of exponential type at most one, square-integrable on the
real axis, and rapidly decreasing there.  The Whittaker--Shannon cardinal sampling theorem on
$\PW_1$~\cite{p8:paleywiener1934,p8:whittaker1935,p8:shannon1949} gives
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers}\Phi(k\pi)
 \frac{\sin(z-k\pi)}{z-k\pi}.                            \label{p8:eq:shannon-full}
\end{equation}
Rapid decay of the samples strengthens the usual $L^2$ statement to absolute
and locally uniform convergence in $z\in\ComplexNumbers$.

All nonzero even samples vanish because $\Phi(2\pi n)=0$.  Put
\begin{equation}
 s_m=\Phi((2m+1)\pi)
 =\prod_{j=1}^\infty
   \SincRad\!\left(\frac{(2m+1)\pi}{2^j}\right).           \label{p8:eq:odd-samples}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Odd-sample cardinal representation]
\label{p8:thm:odd-shannon}
For all $z\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Phi(z)=\frac{\sin z}{z}
 -\sin z\sum_{m\in\IntegerNumbers}
 \frac{\Phi((2m+1)\pi)}{z-(2m+1)\pi},}                 \label{p8:eq:odd-shannon-two-sided}\\
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Phi(z)=\frac{\sin z}{z}
 -2z\sin z\sum_{m=0}^\infty
 \frac{s_m}{z^2-(2m+1)^2\pi^2}.}                        \label{p8:eq:odd-shannon-paired}
\end{align}
At the apparent poles the expressions are interpreted by their removable
limits.  Both series converge normally on compact subsets of $\ComplexNumbers$ after those
removals.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In \eqref{p8:eq:shannon-full}, the term $k=0$ is $\sin z/z$ and every nonzero
even term vanishes.  For odd $k$,
$\sin(z-k\pi)=-\sin z$, giving
\eqref{p8:eq:odd-shannon-two-sided}.  Pair the samples at $\pm(2m+1)\pi$ and use
evenness of $\Phi$ to obtain \eqref{p8:eq:odd-shannon-paired}.
\end{proof}

\newresult Equation \eqref{p8:eq:physical-cosine-series} reconstructs the
physical bump from the same odd samples.  Equation
\eqref{p8:eq:odd-shannon-paired} instead reconstructs the entire Fourier image
at arbitrary complex arguments.  Together they make the odd sample sequence
a two-sided coordinate system for both domains.

For every compact $K\subset\ComplexNumbers$, rapid sample decay implies the superalgebraic
truncation estimate
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{z\in K}|\Phi(z)-\Phi_M(z)|=\BigO_{K,A}(M^{-A})
 \qquad\text{for every }A>0,                             \label{p8:eq:shannon-superalgebraic}
\end{equation}
where $\Phi_M$ retains $0\le m<M$ in the paired series.

\subsection{Higher zero multiplicities as odd-sample sum rules}

Factor \eqref{p8:eq:odd-shannon-paired} as
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=\sin z\,B(z),                                   \label{p8:eq:Phi-sin-B}
\end{equation}
where, away from its removable odd-lattice singularities,
\begin{equation}
 B(z)=\frac1z-2z\sum_{m=0}^\infty
 \frac{s_m}{z^2-(2m+1)^2\pi^2}.                          \label{p8:eq:B-definition}
\end{equation}
At an even lattice point $z_n=2\pi n\ne0$, $B$ is analytic.  The established
zero order $1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$ of $\Phi$ now becomes a hierarchy of identities among
the odd samples.

\begin{theorem}[Odd-sample zero-jet hierarchy]
\label{p8:thm:sample-jet-hierarchy}
For every nonzero integer $n$,
\begin{equation}
 B^{(r)}(2\pi n)=0
 \quad(0\le r<\TwoAdicValuation(n)),
 \qquad
 B^{(\TwoAdicValuation(n))}(2\pi n)\ne0.                             \label{p8:eq:B-jet-hierarchy}
\end{equation}
In particular, for every positive even $n$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty
 \frac{s_m}{4n^2-(2m+1)^2}=\frac1{8n^2}.}               \label{p8:eq:sample-sum-rule-1}
\end{equation}
If $4\mid n$, then also
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \sum_{m=0}^\infty
 \frac{s_m}{\bigl(4n^2-(2m+1)^2\bigr)^2}
 =\frac1{32n^4}.}                                       \label{p8:eq:sample-sum-rule-2}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The sine factor has a simple zero at $2\pi n$, while $\Phi$ has order
$1+\TwoAdicValuation(n)$; hence $B$ has order $\TwoAdicValuation(n)$ there.  This proves
\eqref{p8:eq:B-jet-hierarchy}.  Setting $B(2\pi n)=0$ and dividing out powers of
$\pi$ gives \eqref{p8:eq:sample-sum-rule-1}.  Differentiate
\eqref{p8:eq:B-definition}:
\[
 B'(z)=-\frac1{z^2}
 -2\sum_m\frac{s_m}{z^2-a_m^2}
 +4z^2\sum_m\frac{s_m}{(z^2-a_m^2)^2},
 \qquad a_m=(2m+1)\pi.
\]
If $4\mid n$, then $B'(2\pi n)=0$.  Substituting
\eqref{p8:eq:sample-sum-rule-1} yields \eqref{p8:eq:sample-sum-rule-2}.
\end{proof}

Higher derivatives of $B$ give explicit rational combinations of
\[
 \sum_{m\ge0}\frac{s_m}
 {(4n^2-(2m+1)^2)^j},\qquad j=1,2,\ldots,                \label{p8:eq:sample-hierarchy-sums}
\]
whenever $2^r\mid n$.  This translates the $2$-adic zero multiplicity into a
nested family of exact cardinal-sample identities.  It is a promising bridge
between the spectral arithmetic already formalized in Lean and classical
sampling theory.

\section{Chebyshev, Gegenbauer, and Bessel--product expansions}
\label{p8:sec:bessel}

\subsection{Chebyshev coefficients}

Write the Chebyshev expansion in the standard form
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\frac{a_0}{2}+\sum_{n=1}^\infty a_nT_n(x),
 \qquad -1\le x\le1,                                    \label{p8:eq:chebyshev-series}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 a_n=\frac2\pi\int_{-1}^{1}
 \frac{\RvachevUp(x)T_n(x)}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\Differential x.                \label{p8:eq:chebyshev-coeff}
\end{equation}
Since $\RvachevUp(\cos\theta)$ is a smooth $2\pi$-periodic even function, the
series converges absolutely and uniformly, and its coefficients decay faster
than every power.

The plane-wave identity
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tx}T_n(x)}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\Differential x
 =\pi\ImaginaryUnit^nJ_n(t)                                        \label{p8:eq:cheb-plane-wave}
\end{equation}
turns the coefficients into transforms of the sinc product.

\begin{theorem}[Chebyshev--Bessel sinc representation]
\label{p8:thm:cheb-bessel}
All odd coefficients vanish, and for $r\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 a_{2r}=\frac{2(-1)^r}{\pi}
 \int_0^\infty J_{2r}(t)
 \prod_{j=1}^\infty\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right)\Differential t.} \label{p8:eq:cheb-bessel}
\end{equation}
The integral is absolutely convergent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Insert Fourier inversion \eqref{p8:eq:up-fourier-inversion} into
\eqref{p8:eq:chebyshev-coeff}, apply Fubini, and use
\eqref{p8:eq:cheb-plane-wave}.  This gives
\[
 a_n=\frac{\ImaginaryUnit^n}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Phi(t)J_n(t)\Differential t.
\]
Parity kills odd $n$ and doubles the positive half-axis for even $n$.
Rapid decay of $\Phi$ makes the integral absolute.
\end{proof}

The first numerical coefficients are approximately
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 a_0&= 0.681978961331094\ldots,\\
 a_2&=-0.514948055352838\ldots,\\
 a_4&= 0.195935940418300\ldots,\\
 a_6&=-0.002356862179497\ldots,\\
 a_8&=-0.028210793776951\ldots,\\
 a_{10}&=0.011427972423053\ldots.
 \end{split}                                             \label{p8:eq:cheb-first}
\end{equation}
The sign change between $a_6$ and $a_8$ shows that a naive strict alternation
conjecture would already be false.

\subsection{A Gegenbauer family}

Let $\lambda>0$, let $C_n^\lambda$ be the Gegenbauer polynomial, and put
\begin{equation}
 h_n^{(\lambda)}=
 \int_{-1}^{1}(1-x^2)^{\lambda-1/2}
 [C_n^\lambda(x)]^2\Differential x
 =\frac{\pi2^{1-2\lambda}\Gamma(n+2\lambda)}
 {n!(n+\lambda)\Gamma(\lambda)^2}.                       \label{p8:eq:gegenbauer-norm}
\end{equation}
Write
\begin{equation}
 \RvachevUp(x)=\sum_{n=0}^\infty a_n^{(\lambda)}C_n^\lambda(x),
 \qquad
 a_n^{(\lambda)}=\frac1{h_n^{(\lambda)}}
 \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)C_n^\lambda(x)
 (1-x^2)^{\lambda-1/2}\Differential x.                            \label{p8:eq:gegenbauer-series}
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}
 K_{n,\lambda}=2^\lambda\sqrt\pi\,
 \Gamma(\lambda+\tfrac12)\frac{(2\lambda)_n}{n!}.      \label{p8:eq:gegenbauer-K}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Gegenbauer--Bessel family]
\label{p8:thm:gegenbauer-bessel}
The odd coefficients vanish, and
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 a_{2r}^{(\lambda)}=
 \frac{(-1)^rK_{2r,\lambda}}{\pi h_{2r}^{(\lambda)}}
 \int_0^\infty
 \Phi(t)t^{-\lambda}J_{2r+\lambda}(t)\Differential t.}            \label{p8:eq:gegenbauer-bessel}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the plane-wave integral
\[
 \int_{-1}^{1}\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit tx}C_n^\lambda(x)
 (1-x^2)^{\lambda-1/2}\Differential x
 =\ImaginaryUnit^nK_{n,\lambda}t^{-\lambda}J_{n+\lambda}(t)
\]

after interpreting the right side by its entire normalized-Bessel
continuation at $t=0$.  Fourier inversion and parity give
\eqref{p8:eq:gegenbauer-bessel}.
\end{proof}

The choice $\lambda=1/2$ recovers Legendre polynomials; standard normalization facts are as in~\cite{p8:szego1975}.  If
\begin{equation}
 L_{2r}=\frac{4r+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)P_{2r}(x)\Differential x \in\RationalNumbers          \label{p8:eq:legendre-coeff}
\end{equation}
is the exact rational coefficient from the established Fourier--Legendre
expansion, and $j_n$ is the spherical Bessel function, then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 L_{2r}=\frac{(4r+1)(-1)^r}{\pi}
 \int_0^\infty\Phi(t)j_{2r}(t)\Differential t.}                   \label{p8:eq:legendre-bessel}
\end{equation}
Thus the corpus's rational Legendre coefficients evaluate an infinite family
of Bessel--sinc-product integrals exactly:
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^\infty j_{2r}(t)
 \prod_{j\ge1}\SincRad(t/2^j)\Differential t
 =\frac{(-1)^r\pi}{4r+1}L_{2r}.                         \label{p8:eq:rational-bessel-integrals}
\end{equation}

\section{Exact-rational Bernstein limits and Kummer transforms}
\label{p8:sec:bernstein}

\subsection{Dyadic Bernstein polynomials for F}

Bernstein's probabilistic approximation scheme~\cite{p8:bernstein1912} starts, for $n\ge1$, with
\begin{equation}
 (B_nF)(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}F(k/n)\binom nkx^k(1-x)^{n-k}.  \label{p8:eq:bernstein-F}
\end{equation}
When $n=2^r$, every sample $F(k/n)$ is rational.  Hence $B_{2^r}F$ is an
exact-rational polynomial in either the Bernstein or monomial basis.

\begin{theorem}[Rational Bernstein limit with explicit error]
\label{p8:thm:bernstein-F}
For $n=2^r$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F(x)=\lim_{r\to\infty}(B_{2^r}F)(x)}                   \label{p8:eq:bernstein-limit}
\end{equation}
uniformly on $[0,1]$.  More precisely,
\begin{equation}
 |B_nF(x)-F(x)|
 \le\frac{\|F''\|_\infty}{2n}x(1-x)
 \le\frac1n.                                            \label{p8:eq:bernstein-error}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $K\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(n,x)$, then
$B_nF(x)=\Expectation F(K/n)$.  Taylor's theorem about $x$, the zero mean of
$K/n-x$, and
$\Expectation(K/n-x)^2=x(1-x)/n$ give the first bound.  Use
$\|F''\|_\infty=8$ and $x(1-x)\le1/4$ for the second.
\end{proof}

This is a different exact-rational limit from the Faber expansion.  Faber
partial sums interpolate all dyadic nodes and converge like $4^{-J}$ when
the mesh is $2^{-J}$; Bernstein polynomials smooth the node data by a binomial
kernel and converge at the slower but shape-preserving order $2^{-J}$.

\subsection{Finite Kummer sums for transforms}

The beta--Kummer integral is
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1\binom nkx^k(1-x)^{n-k}\EulerE^{zx}\Differential x
 =\frac1{n+1}\,{}_1F_1(k+1;n+2;z).                      \label{p8:eq:beta-kummer}
\end{equation}
Consequently the restricted Fabius transform
$A(t)$ from \eqref{p8:eq:A-definition} has the locally uniform entire limit
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 A(t)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac1{2^r+1}
 \sum_{k=0}^{2^r}F(k/2^r)
 {}_1F_1(k+1;2^r+2;-\ImaginaryUnit t).}                            \label{p8:eq:F-bernstein-kummer}
\end{equation}
Every coefficient in every finite sum is rational.

For the bump, put
\begin{equation}
 g(u)=\RvachevUp(2u-1)=F(1-|2u-1|),\qquad 0\le u\le1.           \label{p8:eq:g-up}
\end{equation}
At dyadic nodes, $g(k/2^r)\in\RationalNumbers$.  Approximate $g$ by $B_ng$ and transport
back to $[-1,1]$.  Its Fourier image is the finite sum
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_n(t)=\frac{2\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}}{n+1}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}g(k/n)
 {}_1F_1(k+1;n+2;-2\ImaginaryUnit t).                              \label{p8:eq:up-bernstein-kummer-finite}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Bernstein--Kummer representation of the sinc product]
\label{p8:thm:bernstein-kummer}
Along dyadic degrees $n=2^r$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \prod_{j=1}^\infty\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{2^j}\right)
 =\lim_{r\to\infty}
 \frac{2\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit t}}{2^r+1}
 \sum_{k=0}^{2^r}F(1-|2k/2^r-1|)
 {}_1F_1(k+1;2^r+2;-2\ImaginaryUnit t).}                           \label{p8:eq:bernstein-kummer-limit}
\end{equation}
The convergence is locally uniform in $t\in\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Bernstein's theorem gives $B_ng\to g$ uniformly.  On a compact set
$K\subset\ComplexNumbers$, the Fourier kernels on $[-1,1]$ are bounded by
$\exp(\sup_{t\in K}|\operatorname{Im}t|)$, so uniform approximation of the
physical functions implies locally uniform convergence of their transforms.
Equation \eqref{p8:eq:beta-kummer} evaluates each finite transform exactly.
\end{proof}

This representation combines a limit, a finite hypergeometric series, and
exact dyadic arithmetic.  It is well suited to symbolic experimentation:
for each fixed $r$ the entire approximant satisfies the differential
relations of a finite family of Kummer functions, while the limit is known
from the corpus to be non-holonomic in much stronger senses.

\section{Weighted cube volumes and hyperplane sections}
\label{p8:sec:cube}

\subsection{Sublevel volumes}

Let $a_j=2^{-j}$ and $a^{(N)}=(a_1,\ldots,a_N)$.  The finite CDF in
\eqref{p8:eq:finite-spline} is exactly a weighted-cube sublevel volume:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F_N(x)=\operatorname{vol}_N
 \left\{u\in[0,1]^N:\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_ju_j\le x\right\}.} \label{p8:eq:cube-sublevel}
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F(x)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\operatorname{vol}_N
 \left\{u\in[0,1]^N:\sum_{j=1}^{N}2^{-j}u_j\le x\right\}}              \label{p8:eq:cube-volume-limit}
\end{equation}
for every real $x$.  Equation \eqref{p8:eq:finite-spline} is the
inclusion--exclusion evaluation of the same polytope volume.

One may also write the infinite-dimensional product-measure integral
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\int_{[0,1]^\NaturalNumbers}
 \IndicatorOf{\{\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}u_j\le x\}}\Differential\bm u.  \label{p8:eq:infinite-cube-integral}
\end{equation}
This is the geometric form of the probability model, but it emphasizes that
the Fabius function is the distribution of weighted half-spaces cutting a
Hilbert cube.

\subsection{Hyperplane sections and a Fourier-slice limit}

Let $v$ be uniform on $[-1,1]^N$ and set
\begin{equation}
 Y_N=a^{(N)}\cdot v.
\end{equation}
By the coarea formula, its density $u_N$ is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 u_N(y)=\frac{2^{-N}}{\|a^{(N)}\|_2}
 \Haus^{N-1}
 \left\{v\in[-1,1]^N:a^{(N)}\cdot v=y\right\}.}         \label{p8:eq:cube-section-density}
\end{equation}
The Fourier-slice theorem reduces here to Fubini:
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\RealNumbers}u_N(y)\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit ty}\Differential y
 =\prod_{j=1}^{N}\SincRad(t/2^j).                           \label{p8:eq:cube-fourier-slice}
\end{equation}
The first three sinc factors give an integrable majorant for all later finite
products, so dominated Fourier inversion yields uniform convergence
$u_N\to\RvachevUp$.  Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \RvachevUp(y)=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{2^{-N}}{\|a^{(N)}\|_2}
 \Haus^{N-1}
 \left\{v\in[-1,1]^N:a^{(N)}\cdot v=y\right\}.}         \label{p8:eq:cube-section-limit}
\end{equation}

Equations \eqref{p8:eq:cube-volume-limit}, \eqref{p8:eq:cube-section-limit}, and
\eqref{p8:eq:cube-fourier-slice} form a volume--section--product triangle:
sublevel volumes give $F$, codimension-one sections give $\RvachevUp$, and Fourier
slices give the finite sinc products converging to $\Phi$.

\section{Representation synthesis}
\label{p8:sec:synthesis}

The formulas above are best viewed as a network rather than a list.  Exact
dyadic arithmetic supplies Faber coefficients; differentiation turns them
into Haar coefficients; Haar Parseval gives a positive energy series; Haar
Fourier transforms give a new sinc-product series.  Independently, binary
digits reorganize the same probability law into binomial layers, while
compact support places the Fourier image in a sampling space.  Orthogonal
polynomials and Bernstein polynomials then provide two different spectral and
algebraic envelopes.

\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.31\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.23\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.198\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{New representation families and their characteristic data.}\label{p8:tab:new-synthesis}\\
\toprule
Representation & Formula type & Exact data & Convergence/control \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Representation & Formula type & Exact data & Convergence/control \\
\midrule
\endhead
Faber--Schauder & $F=x+\sum c_{m,k}\Lambda_{m,k}$ & Rational dyadic midpoint defects & Uniform; sharp error $\sim4^{-J}$.\\
Inverse splines & $G_J=(S_JF)^{-1}$, formula \eqref{p8:eq:inverse-hinge} & Rational knots and slopes & Uniform; error $\le\omega_G(4^{-J})$.\\
Rvachev tents & $\RvachevUp(y)=1-|y|+\sum c\Lambda(1-|y|)$ & Same rational table & Uniform on support.\\
Haar density & $F'=1+\sum2^{m+1}cH$ & Rational step coefficients & $L^p$, including essential $L^\infty$.\\
Haar Fourier & $\Phi=\SincRad+\sum c\,\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit\cdot}\sin^2/(\ImaginaryUnit t)$ & Rational coefficients, elementary kernels & Normal on compact subsets of $\ComplexNumbers$.\\
Energy & $A_2=1/4+\sum2^m\sum c^2$ & Positive rational squares & Monotone, certified geometric tail.\\
Energy asymptotic & $E_m\sim A_2\sum\beta_q4^{-qm}$ & Explicit rational $\beta_q,\gamma_q$ & Poincaré all orders.\\
Triangular probability & $X=\sum2^{-n}K_n$ & Independent binomial layers & Almost sure and $L^p$.\\
Triangular product & $\Phi=\prod\cos(t/2^n)^{n-1}$ & Integer exponents & Normal on compacta; local tail bound.\\
Coefficient CDF & Cumulative coefficients of $P_N(q)$ & Positive integers & Bracket width $(N+1)2^{-N}$.\\
Odd cardinal & Rational-kernel sum over $s_m=\Phi((2m+1)\pi)$ & Explicit sinc products & Locally uniform, superalgebraic truncation.\\
Sample sum rules & Zero jets of $B(z)$ & $2$-adic multiplicities & Exact convergent identities.\\
Chebyshev--\newline Gegenbauer & Bessel integrals of $\Phi$ & Existing moments/Legendre rationals & Absolute integrals; uniform polynomial series.\\
Bernstein & Exact-rational polynomials at dyadic degrees & Dyadic values of $F$ & Uniform; explicit $1/n$ bound for $F$.\\
Kummer Fourier & Finite ${}_1F_1$ sums, then $n\to\infty$ & Rational finite weights & Locally uniform entire convergence.\\
Cube geometry & Sublevel volumes and hyperplane sections & Dyadic normal vector & CDF and uniform-density limits.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

Several hybrid formulas deserve emphasis:
\begin{align}
 \text{rational wavelets}+\text{elementary oscillation}
 &\longrightarrow \text{entire sinc product},            \label{p8:eq:hybrid1}\\
 \text{odd sinc-product samples}+\text{rational resolvents}
 &\longrightarrow \text{entire sinc product},            \label{p8:eq:hybrid2}\\
 \text{rational dyadic samples}+{}_1F_1
 &\longrightarrow \text{entire sinc product},            \label{p8:eq:hybrid3}\\
 \text{rational Legendre coefficients}
 &\longleftrightarrow \text{Bessel--sinc integrals}.       \label{p8:eq:hybrid4}
\end{align}
The same function is therefore reconstructed from local curvature, stepwise
martingale data, odd spectral samples, binomial coefficient arrays,
orthogonal-polynomial periods, and weighted-polytope geometry.

\section{Numerical and exact verification}
\label{p8:sec:numerics}

\subsection{Reproducibility design}

The archive contains
\path{fabius_multiresolution_experiments.py}, a standalone, fully commented
Python program.  Its arithmetic and analytic checks are intentionally
separated.

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Exact layer.}  Python's \texttt{Fraction} type evaluates
      \eqref{p8:eq:V-recurrence}, every $F(a/2^{16})$, all Faber coefficients
      below level $16$, every $R_J$, and every coefficient of $P_N$ for
      $N\le16$.  Log-concavity is tested with integer inequalities.
\item \emph{Independent analytic layer.}  The reference values of $F$ and
      $\RvachevUp$ come from the period-two cosine series whose coefficients are
      evaluated by the sinc product.  Haar, triangular, cardinal, Bessel, and
      Kummer formulas are checked against this independent product route.
\item \emph{Outputs.}  The run writes human-readable and JSON results, the
      TeX tables included below, and four figures.  No network access or
      hidden data files are used.
\end{itemize}

The exact level-$16$ table contains $65537$ rational dyadic values and is
constructed by the terminating highest-bit recursion in roughly linear time
in the table size.  The bundled run uses $90$ decimal digits for mpmath
operations.

\subsection{Faber convergence}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{assets/Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations/figures/faber_approximants.png}
\caption{The exact-rational dyadic interpolants are the Faber partial sums.
The level-$6$ curve is already visually indistinguishable from the independent
sinc-product reconstruction on most of the interval.}
\label{p8:fig:faber-approximants}
\end{figure}

The observed maximum errors satisfy the rigorous bound $4^{-J}$ and the
scaled errors approach the sharp constant one from
\eqref{p8:eq:faber-error-limit}.  At level $J=10$, the maximum grid residual
against the independent Fourier evaluator is approximately $9.54\times10^{-7}$,
consistent with $4^{-10}=9.5367\times10^{-7}$.

\subsection{Energy partial sums and acceleration}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{assets/Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations/figures/energy_convergence.png}
\caption{Raw positive rational partial sums converge geometrically.  Using
four terms of the all-orders tail expansion removes several additional powers
of $4^{-J}$.  Values below machine precision relative to the numerical
reference are omitted automatically from the logarithmic plot.}
\label{p8:fig:energy-convergence}
\end{figure}

\input{assets/Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations/generated_tables.tex}

The decimal in \eqref{p8:eq:A2-decimal} is not advertised as a formally certified
$60$-digit interval: the all-orders theorem is asymptotic and the bundled run
does not compute explicit constants in its remainder.  It is, however,
stable under changes of both level and acceleration order.  For example, the
five-term estimates from $J=12,13,14,15,16$ successively stabilize through
roughly $8,12,16,20,$ and $21$ additional digits beyond the raw partials.  A
rigorous high-precision enclosure can be obtained by combining a finite
higher-order expansion with explicit derivative sup norms and an
Euler--Maclaurin remainder.

\subsection{Haar, triangular, and cardinal checks}

Using levels $m<14$, the Haar--$\SincRad^2$ formula
\eqref{p8:eq:haar-Phi-complex} has absolute residuals between
$10^{-10}$ and $8\times10^{-9}$ at the tested real and complex arguments.
This is the expected unresolved tail size.  The finite triangular product at
$N=36$ agrees with the sinc product to the precision predicted by
\eqref{p8:eq:triangular-tail}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{assets/Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations/figures/odd_sampling_convergence.png}
\caption{Maximum residual of the odd-sample cardinal formula at two real and
one complex test point.  The small nonmonotonic ripples reflect cancellation;
the envelope decreases superalgebraically.}
\label{p8:fig:odd-sampling}
\end{figure}

With $40$ positive odd-sample pairs, the absolute residuals are
$1.2\times10^{-14}$ to $3.9\times10^{-13}$ at the six test points listed in
\path{numerical_results.txt}.  The explicit sum rules
\eqref{p8:eq:sample-sum-rule-1} and \eqref{p8:eq:sample-sum-rule-2} can be checked
from the same sample table.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{assets/Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations/figures/triangular_distribution.png}
\caption{The positive mass distribution encoded by $P_{12}$.  Its broad,
nearly flat central region is a finite-layer effect quite different from a
single binomial law.}
\label{p8:fig:triangular-distribution}
\end{figure}

Exact integer arithmetic confirms palindromicity and log-concavity for every
$2\le N\le16$; selected degrees are shown in
\cref{p8:tab:triangular-check}.  The checks are exhaustive over every interior
coefficient, not random spot tests.

\subsection{Bessel and Bernstein checks}

The cosine-grid and Bessel-product evaluations in
\cref{p8:tab:chebyshev-check} agree to about $2\times10^{-8}$.  The residual is
set by the finite cosine grid and finite Bessel integration cutoff, not by a
symbolic approximation in the theorem.

For the rational Bernstein approximants to $F$, the observed uniform errors
are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}
\toprule
$n$ & observed error & rigorous bound $1/n$\\
\midrule
8   & $7.1353\times10^{-2}$ & $1.25\times10^{-1}$\\
16  & $4.1193\times10^{-2}$ & $6.25\times10^{-2}$\\
32  & $2.2565\times10^{-2}$ & $3.125\times10^{-2}$\\
64  & $1.1906\times10^{-2}$ & $1.5625\times10^{-2}$\\
128 & $6.1361\times10^{-3}$ & $7.8125\times10^{-3}$\\
256 & $3.1214\times10^{-3}$ & $3.90625\times10^{-3}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
At degree $32$ and $t=2.7$, direct quadrature of the Bernstein bump and the
finite Kummer sum \eqref{p8:eq:up-bernstein-kummer-finite} differ by
$4.5\times10^{-17}$.

\section{Conjectures and frontier directions}
\label{p8:sec:frontier}

\subsection{Arithmetic nature of the energy constant}

\begin{conjecture}[Irrationality of the Fabius energy]
\label{p8:conj:A2-irrational}
The number
\[
 A_2=\int_0^1F(x)^2\Differential x
\]
is irrational; plausibly it is transcendental.
\end{conjecture}

The positive rational expansion \eqref{p8:eq:A2-rational-series} is more
arithmetically structured than the original sinc integral.  A first serious
attack should study the reduced denominators of $R_J$, their $p$-adic growth,
and whether the tail asymptotic can be converted into rational-approximation
bounds incompatible with algebraicity.  The main obstacle is that an
excellent asymptotic expansion by itself does not provide the denominator
separation needed for an irrationality proof.

\subsection{Endpoint phase tomography through rational wavelets}

For a cell entirely in $(0,1/4)$, the differential equation
$F''(x)=8F(4x)$ and \eqref{p8:eq:faber-kernel} give the exact scaled identity
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 -4^{m-1}c_{m,k}
 =\int_0^1\min(s,1-s)
 F\!\left(2^{2-m}(k+s)\right)\Differential s.}                    \label{p8:eq:endpoint-wavelet-integral}
\end{equation}
Thus every fixed-$k$ coefficient ray is a positive triangular average of the
endpoint function at a dyadically shrinking scale.

\begin{conjecture}[Wavelet recovery of the Lambert phase]
\label{p8:conj:wavelet-phase}
For each fixed $k\ge0$, the logarithm of
$-c_{m,k}$ has an all-orders expansion obtained by applying boundary Laplace
analysis to \eqref{p8:eq:endpoint-wavelet-integral}.  Its leading exponential
part is governed by the same lower-Lambert coordinate as $F$, and its bounded
oscillatory part is a nonconstant translate of the repository's one-periodic
endpoint correction.  Distinct $k$ determine distinct phase shifts but the
same Fourier spectrum.
\end{conjecture}

This would turn exact rational coefficient rays into a tomography device for
the periodic endpoint phase.  It is important not to replace the phase by its
mean: the audited endpoint work proves that such averaging fails at the
natural error scale.

\subsection{The triangular coefficient problem}

Conjecture \ref{p8:conj:triangular-logconcavity} asks for a structural proof of
log-concavity of a highly nonstandard sparse product.  Promising approaches
include:
\begin{itemize}
\item induction through interlacing inequalities stronger than ordinary
      log-concavity;
\item a probabilistic proof using ultra-log-concavity under dyadically
      weighted Bernoulli convolution;
\item total positivity of the shift-and-add operators that multiply by
      $(1+q^{2^r})^a$ when the lower scales are already present;
\item a stable-polynomial or Lorentzian homogenization in extra variables,
      followed by a carefully justified diagonal specialization.
\end{itemize}
A local central limit theorem with explicit lattice corrections for
$X_N^\triangle$ would also quantify how the positive coefficient limit
approaches the smooth Fabius density.

\subsection{Odd samples as intrinsic spectral coordinates}

The sampling formula uses all odd samples because the even samples are forced
to vanish.  This suggests a constrained Paley--Wiener space
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal V=\{f\in\PW_1:f(2\pi n)=0\text{ for }n\ne0,
 \text{ with the prescribed higher }2\text{-adic jets}\}.              \label{p8:eq:constrained-PW}
\end{equation}

\begin{openproblem}[de Branges/sampling model]
Construct an intrinsic reproducing-kernel or de Branges description of the
closure generated by the Fabius zero divisor, and determine whether the odd
samples form a natural Riesz coordinate sequence after the higher even-zero
constraints are imposed.  Derive sharp weighted truncation inequalities for
\eqref{p8:eq:odd-shannon-paired} that exploit the exact zero multiplicities.
\end{openproblem}

The identities in \cref{p8:thm:sample-jet-hierarchy} are the first algebraic
constraints such a model must reproduce.

\subsection{Nonlinear wavelet energies}

For $p=2$, orthogonality collapses the energy to the positive diagonal sum
\eqref{p8:eq:A2-rational-series}.  For even $p>2$, products of Haar functions are
still finite on each dyadic tree but no longer diagonal.

\begin{openproblem}[Exact dyadic tree formulas for $L^p$ energies]
Find finite-level tree recurrences and convergent rational series for
\[
 A_p=\int_0^1F(x)^p\Differential x,
 \qquad
 U_p=\int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)^p\Differential x,
\]
starting with $p=3,4$.  Determine whether any rearrangement is positive and
whether its level tails are again controlled by the derivative-norm spectrum.
\end{openproblem}

The order-statistic formulas in the current corpus evaluate many $A_p$-type
integrals probabilistically; the wavelet problem asks for a genuinely local,
multiscale arithmetic decomposition.

\subsection{Orthogonal-polynomial coefficient geometry}

The Chebyshev coefficients in \eqref{p8:eq:cheb-first} do not alternate forever
in the simplest way.  More nuanced questions remain.

\begin{openproblem}[Sign and zero laws]
Study the sign changes, asymptotic phase, and zero distribution of
$a_{2r}^{(\lambda)}$ as functions of $r$ and $\lambda$.  Determine whether the
Bessel integrals \eqref{p8:eq:gegenbauer-bessel} admit saddle descriptions tied
to the sharp real-axis decay rays of $\Phi$, and identify values of
$\lambda$ for which the coefficients have additional arithmetic structure.
\end{openproblem}

The Legendre case is especially attractive because its coefficients are
rational, making \eqref{p8:eq:rational-bessel-integrals} an exact bridge between
orthogonal-polynomial arithmetic and oscillatory integral analysis.

\subsection{Base-b and geometric-weight extensions}

The repository already develops a base-$b$ sinc-product and zero-spectrum
theory.  Every new construction above has a plausible geometric-weight
analogue:
\begin{itemize}
\item $b$-adic Faber systems with exact node arithmetic;
\item regrouping base-$b$ digits along antidiagonals, producing multinomial
      rather than binomial layers;
\item cardinal sampling after identifying the appropriate forced zero
      sublattice;
\item Jacobi/Gegenbauer coefficients as Bessel transforms of the base-$b$
      product;
\item Bernstein limits along degrees $b^r$.
\end{itemize}
The dyadic case is exceptional because binary digit regrouping and Haar
multiresolution are perfectly aligned; identifying which conclusions survive
for $b>2$ should clarify which phenomena are genuinely dyadic.

\subsection{Lean formalization roadmap}

The new results are unusually formalization-friendly because their analytic
core can be divided into finite exact layers and standard convergence lemmas.
A practical order is:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Define $\Lambda_{m,k}$ and prove that the finite Faber sum is the dyadic
      linear interpolant.  Import the existing exact dyadic evaluator to prove
      coefficient rationality.
\item Prove the $C^2$ interpolation bound and the sharp limit at $1/4$.
\item Differentiate finite interpolants, identify conditional averages of
      $F'$, and obtain $L^2$ convergence.  Haar orthogonality then proves
      \eqref{p8:eq:A2-rational-series} with minimal measure-theoretic overhead.
\item Formalize the coefficient kernel \eqref{p8:eq:faber-kernel}, the uniform
      bound, and the first few energy-tail coefficients.  The all-orders
      theorem can follow after an endpoint-flat Euler--Maclaurin library is in
      place.
\item Construct the triangular Bernoulli array on the existing infinite
      product probability space.  Regroup nonnegative series and prove
      independence of antidiagonals.
\item Apply the existing Fourier product API to prove
      \eqref{p8:eq:triangular-cos-product}; keep the finite polynomial and exact
      coefficient checks as executable definitions.
\item Reuse a Paley--Wiener sampling theorem from mathlib if available, or
      first formalize the rapidly decreasing special case needed for
      \eqref{p8:eq:odd-shannon-paired}.  The sample-jet identities then reduce to
      differentiation of normally convergent rational series.
\item Add the beta--Kummer integral and Bernstein convergence as a separate
      approximation module; these require no endpoint asymptotics.
\end{enumerate}

The energy identity is the best first target: it combines exact dyadic
arithmetic, finite orthogonality, and a single $L^2$ limit, and it creates a
new formally verified representation of a constant already central to the
integral reports.

\section{Conclusion}
\label{p8:sec:conclusion}

The Fabius--Rvachev system is often introduced through one striking identity:
a compactly supported $C^\infty$ function whose Fourier transform is an
infinite product of sinc functions.  The audited corpus already shows that
this is only one face of a much larger structure involving uniform random
series, Thue--Morse signs, $q$-binomial algebra, exact dyadic values, spectral
multiplicities, Lambert--$W$ endpoint phases, Bell polynomials, and a rich
integral-transform calculus.

The new results add a multiresolution and sampling layer to that structure.
Exact dyadic values are not merely isolated arithmetic curiosities: they are
the coordinates of a rational Faber basis, and inversion of its finite
interpolants gives exact-rational quantile splines.  Their discrete derivatives are
rational Haar coefficients of the smooth density.  Orthogonality turns those
coefficients into a positive rational expansion of the central sinc energy,
and smooth self-similarity determines the complete tail expansion.  Binary
digits reorganize the same law into independent binomial layers; compact
support reorganizes the Fourier image into odd cardinal samples; plane-wave
identities reorganize it into Bessel transforms; Bernstein's probabilistic
polynomials reorganize it into finite Kummer sums; and finite-dimensional
geometry reorganizes it into cube volumes and sections.

No one representation dominates all others.  The sinc product is best for
zeros and global frequency decay; the Faber series is best for exact local
dyadic approximation; the Haar series is best for energy; the triangular
layers are best for positive combinatorics; odd sampling is best for entire
reconstruction from sparse spectral data; Bessel expansions connect to
orthogonal approximation; Bernstein--Kummer limits provide finite entire
approximants; and cube sections expose the underlying geometry.  The most
promising next step is to formalize the rational energy series and then use
its exact denominators and all-order tail to investigate the arithmetic nature
of $A_2$.

\appendix

\section{Remainder proof for the all-orders energy expansion}
\label{p8:app:energy-proof}

This appendix supplies a finite-order argument behind
\cref{p8:thm:Em-asymptotic}.  It avoids treating the nowhere-analytic function
$F$ as if its formal differential-operator series converged.

Let
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=2^{-m},\qquad h=\Delta/2,
 \qquad x_k=(k+\tfrac12)\Delta.                          \label{p8:eq:appendix-delta}
\end{equation}
For an arbitrary fixed integer $M\ge1$, Taylor's theorem with a uniform
remainder gives
\begin{equation}
 c_{m,k}=-\sum_{r=1}^{M}
 \frac{h^{2r}}{(2r)!}F^{(2r)}(x_k)
 +\BigO(h^{2M+2}),                                       \label{p8:eq:c-finite-taylor}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $k$.  Squaring this identity and retaining terms through any
prescribed power of $\Delta$ is therefore legitimate.

The level energy may be written as
\begin{equation}
 E_m=\Delta^{-2}
 \left[\Delta\sum_{k=0}^{\Delta^{-1}-1}c_{m,k}^2\right]. \label{p8:eq:Em-midpoint}
\end{equation}
Every coefficient in the finite expansion of $c_{m,k}^2$ is a product
$F^{(a)}F^{(b)}$ with $a,b\ge2$.  All derivatives of $F$ of positive order
vanish at $0$ and $1$.  Hence each such product and all of its derivatives
vanish at both endpoints.  The midpoint Euler--Maclaurin formula therefore
has no boundary correction of any algebraic order: for every fixed smooth
product $G$ of this form and every $L\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \Delta\sum_{k=0}^{\Delta^{-1}-1}G(x_k)
 =\int_0^1G(x)\Differential x+\BigO_L(\Delta^L).                  \label{p8:eq:flat-midpoint}
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{p8:eq:c-finite-taylor}--\eqref{p8:eq:flat-midpoint} shows that the
Poincaré coefficients of $E_m$ are the same as those of
\begin{equation}
 \Delta^{-2}\int_0^1
 \left[\bigl(\cosh(hD)-1\bigr)F(x)\right]^2\Differential x,       \label{p8:eq:operator-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
where the right side is interpreted only as a formal device for extracting
any finite number of Taylor coefficients.

Since $\cosh(hD)-1$ contains only even positive powers of $D$, repeated
integration by parts has no boundary terms.  Thus, coefficientwise,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1[(\cosh(hD)-1)F]^2\Differential x
 &=\int_0^1F(\cosh(hD)-1)^2F\Differential x                       \label{p8:eq:operator-square}\\
 &\sim\sum_{r=2}^\infty
 \frac{2^{2r-1}-2}{(2r)!}h^{2r}(-1)^rI_r.               \label{p8:eq:operator-coeff}
\end{align}
Now set $r=q+1$, use $h=\Delta/2$, and substitute
$I_{q+1}=2^{(q+1)(q+2)}A_2$.  The coefficient of
$\Delta^{2q}=4^{-qm}$ is
\begin{align}
 (-1)^{q+1}
 \frac{2^{2q+1}-2}{(2q+2)!}
 \frac{2^{(q+1)(q+2)}}{2^{2q+2}}A_2
 &=(-1)^{q+1}
 \frac{2^{q(q+1)+1}(2^{2q}-1)}{(2q+2)!}A_2,            \label{p8:eq:beta-derivation}
\end{align}
which is $\beta_qA_2$.

More explicitly, for every fixed $Q\ge1$ the preceding finite-order
argument gives
\begin{equation}
 E_m=A_2\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\beta_q4^{-qm}
 +\BigO_Q(4^{-(Q+1)m}).                                  \label{p8:eq:Em-finite-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
Summing the remainder over $m\ge J$ proves the finite-order version of
\eqref{p8:eq:A2-tail-asymptotic} and the acceleration estimate
\eqref{p8:eq:A2-accelerator-error}.

\section{Exact dyadic evaluation used by the experiments}
\label{p8:app:dyadic-algorithm}

The experiments do not solve a discretized differential equation.  They use
the repository's exact arithmetic in the following terminating form.

\begin{algorithm}[Highest-bit evaluator]
\label{p8:alg:highest-bit}
Assume the values $V_n=F(2^{-n})$ have been computed from
\eqref{p8:eq:V-recurrence}.  To evaluate $F(a/2^e)$ with
$0<a<2^e$, write
\begin{equation}
 a=2^b+q,
 \qquad 0\le q<2^b,
 \qquad r=e-b,
 \qquad y=q/2^e.                                        \label{p8:eq:bit-decomposition}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 F(a/2^e)=
 \sum_{j=0}^{r}
 \frac{2^{j(j+1)/2}V_{r-j}}{j!}y^j-F(q/2^e).}           \label{p8:eq:highest-bit-evaluator}
\end{equation}
Because $q<a$, a left-to-right table of all numerators memoizes every
subproblem and terminates after clearing the set bits of $a$.
\end{algorithm}

For a fixed exponent $e$, the algorithm computes all $2^e+1$ values using
$\BigO(e2^e)$ rational operations.  A single level-$16$ table then supplies
all coarser Faber coefficients by exact subsampling.

\item[\path{Fabius_Rvachev_Multiresolution_Representations.tex}]
The complete source of this report.

The compiled report.

Exact and numerical verification code with detailed comments.

Four plots generated by the script.

snapshot, and the modified-path comparison ledger.

The command used for the bundled numerical run is
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash]
python fabius_multiresolution_experiments.py \
    --max-exact-level 16 --precision 90
\end{lstlisting}
The program was executed with Python~3, NumPy, SciPy, mpmath, and Matplotlib.
It does not access the network.

\section*{Postscript: the Faber--Schauder and tent layers, formal}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Postscript: the Faber--Schauder and tent layers, formal}

Two elementary layers underpinning the hat-function pictures of this volume are now
kernel-verified.

\smallskip
\textbf{Faber--Schauder midpoint defects.}  \path{FaberSchauderDefect.lean} defines, for a
bounded Fabius solution $F$, the second difference across the level-$m$ dyadic cell,
\[
  c_{m,k}\;=\;F\!\left(\tfrac{2k+1}{2^{m+1}}\right)-\tfrac12\left(F\!\left(\tfrac{k}{2^m}\right)+F\!\left(\tfrac{k+1}{2^m}\right)\right)
\]
(\nolinkurl{Fabius.faberSchauderDefect}), and proves that it is \emph{antisymmetric}
under the dyadic reflection,
$c_{m,\,2^m-1-k}=-c_{m,k}$
(\nolinkurl{Fabius.faberSchauderDefect\_reflect}).  The sign is forced: $x\mapsto1-x$
carries the strictly convex first half of $F$ onto the strictly concave second half, so
it reverses every second difference.  Accordingly the defect is strictly negative on the
first half
(\nolinkurl{Fabius.faberSchauderDefect\_neg\_succ}, \nolinkurl{Fabius.faberSchauderDefect\_neg})
and strictly positive on the second
(\nolinkurl{Fabius.faberSchauderDefect\_pos\_succ}), with the exact values
$c_{0,0}=0$, $c_{1,0}=-13/72$, $c_{2,0}=c_{2,1}=-1/32$ recorded as theorems.

\smallskip
\textbf{Tent moments.}  \path{TentMoment.lean} supplies the exact polynomial moments of
a dyadic tent.  The symmetric single-power integral obeys a clean parity dichotomy,
\[
  \int_{-h}^{h}\left(1-\frac{|u|}{h}\right)u^{n}\,du
  =\begin{cases}
     \dfrac{2h^{n+1}}{(n+1)(n+2)}, & n \text{ even},\\[2mm]
     0, & n \text{ odd},
   \end{cases}
\]
(\nolinkurl{Fabius.integral\_tentProfile\_mul\_pow}), and the shifted moment of degree $p$
about a centre $c$ is the corresponding binomial sum
(\nolinkurl{Fabius.integral\_tentProfile\_mul\_pow\_shift}, and
\nolinkurl{Fabius.integral\_tent\_mul\_pow} for the truncated tent \nolinkurl{Fabius.tent}
itself).  Neither the corpus nor Mathlib previously carried any tent or B-spline
moment API.
